The plural *jannāt* is the Qur'an's habitual way of speaking about Paradise — multiple gardens, multiple levels, multiple experiences within one mercy. Verse 12 places the foremost *in* these gardens, not hovering above them in a purely abstract bliss. Islamic eschatology is garden-shaped. The body is not discarded; it is completed.

**Naʿīm as Pure Joy**

*Naʿīm* is joy without the aftertaste of anxiety that stains earthly pleasure. The question of 102:8 — "Then you will surely be asked that Day about *al-naʿīm*" — hangs over every worldly enjoyment. The foremost are those who answered that question well in advance, by gratitude and right use, and who therefore inherit the unstained *naʿīm* of the gardens.

**Imagining Pure Joy**

Sit occasionally and try to imagine joy without anxiety, beauty without possession, company without rivalry. This is a spiritual exercise verse 12 authorizes. Consumer culture trains for intense brief purchasable joy with a crash. Naʿīm trains for pure lasting relational joy. The imagination, like the body, needs rehearsal for the destination it hopes to reach.

---
## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:13

> **many from those of old,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Demography of the Foremost**

*Thullatun mina al-awwalīn* — "a large group from those of old." The word *thullah* means a throng, a company, a substantial body of people — not a handful, not a remnant, but many. The foremost are numerous among the earlier generations. This statement has been read in two primary ways by the classical tradition. The first, favored by Ibn Kathīr, the Jalālayn, and al-Ṭabarī among others, takes "those of old" (*al-awwalīn*) to mean the earlier religious communities — the followers of the prophets before Muhammad ﷺ — and "those of later times" in the next verse to mean the community of Muhammad ﷺ. The second takes both phrases as referring to earlier and later generations *within* the Muslim community itself, in accordance with the famous ḥadīth:

> *"The best of people are my generation, then those who follow them, then those who follow them."* (al-Bukhārī 3651; Muslim 2533)

Both readings affirm a sobering historical pattern: the density of the foremost is greater in earlier times.

**Why Many Among the Earlier Generations?**

Ibn ʿAjībah offers a careful nuance: those of earlier times were greater in quantity, not necessarily in quality. The conditions of earlier prophetic ages — the freshness of revelation, the presence of the prophet himself, the relative simplicity of distractions — made the production of spiritual outstrippers more demographically common. This is not a romanticization of the past; it is a recognition that environments shape outcomes. A garden with clean air, good soil, and careful gardeners produces more prize roses than a garden in a polluted lot. The earlier generations had, in many respects, the cleaner air of proximity to revelation's source.

**The Warning Embedded in the Demography**

If the foremost are many among the earlier and few among the later, then later generations are on notice. The passage of time is not automatically progressive in spiritual terms. The modern myth of inevitable progress — that later is always better, that the newest is the truest — is here quietly inverted. The Qur'an elsewhere confirms the pattern:

> *"And most of the people, although you strive [for them], are not believers."* (Qur'an 12:103)

And the Prophet ﷺ warned of a time when holding to one's religion would be like holding a burning coal. Verse 13, by celebrating the many foremost of old, implicitly asks the later reader: what environment are you creating, and what environment are you accepting, such that the production of the foremost has become rare?

**Hadith of Spiritual Devolution**

The Prophet ﷺ said to his Companions:

> *"You live at a time when whoever omits one-tenth of what he has been commanded shall be ruined. But there will come a time when whoever fulfills one-tenth of what he has been commanded shall be saved."*

This report, cited in the commentary tradition including by Ibn ʿAjībah, does not lower the ideal; it adjusts the pastoral expectation. In a time of general lassitude, the person who holds even a portion of the full practice with sincerity may be marked for salvation — and, by the logic of verse 14, the few who hold the full practice in such a time may attain ranks of extraordinary nearness. The demography of the foremost is not a closed history; it is an open challenge.

**Thullah of the Earlier Generations**

A *thullah* is a substantial company. The foremost of earlier times were not a handful of isolated geniuses; they were a demographic fact of healthier spiritual ecosystems. Prophetic proximity, simpler distraction-structures, and collective momentum produced outstrippers in number. Verse 13 is historical sociology under revelation's gaze.

**Honoring the Early Without Freezing the Later**

To say the earlier produced many foremost is to honor the Salaf without saying the later cannot produce any. Verse 14 will protect the later few. Together, 13 and 14 train a balanced historical consciousness: deep respect for early excellence, open possibility for late excellence under harder conditions.

**Honoring Early Excellence**

Respect the density of foremost in earlier generations without freezing history. Healthier spiritual ecosystems produced more outstrippers. Verse 13 is historical sociology under revelation. Create micro-ecosystems now — homes, circles, mosques — that make foremost-making more possible. Environment shapes outcomes; steward the environment you can reach.

**Among the Foremost's Portrait**

This verse belongs to the sūrah's portrait of the sābiqūn al-muqarrabūn — those who outstripped and were brought nigh. Al-Rāzī discusses whether they pass the Reckoning lightly or not at all; Ibn Kathīr emphasizes the prophets among them; the spiritual tradition emphasizes the elite of nearness in every generation. Cross-reference with 83:18–28 (ʿIlliyyūn and the sealed wine of the near) and with the ḥadīth qudsī of drawing near through nawāfil (al-Bukhārī 6502) deepens the portrait. Verse 13 is one panel. Read it as part of the whole gallery of nearness, couches, drink without harm, fruits of choice, companionship like concealed pearls, and speech that is only peace. The foremost are defined by Whom they are near; everything else is hospitality.

---
## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:14

> **and few from those of later times—**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sobering Minority**

*Wa-qalīlun mina al-ākhirīn* — "and few from those of later times." Where verse 13 gave the foremost a large company among the earlier generations, verse 14 gives them only a few among the later. The word *qalīl* is unambiguous: small in number. The spiritual elite does not disappear in later times, but it becomes rare. This is one of the more austere demographic statements in the Qur'an, and the tradition has not softened it.

**The Higher Quality of the Later Few**

Ibn ʿAjībah, however, adds a note of unexpected hope. Those toward the end of time, he suggests, may be of a higher degree in quality if not in quantity, because they have awakened in a time of heedlessness and exerted themselves in a time of lassitude. Because they achieve spiritual awakening without many of the supports available to previous generations — without the physical presence of the Prophet ﷺ, without the collective momentum of a freshly converted society, without the simplicity of a world less saturated with distraction — God may grant them a rank given to no others. This reading is supported by the ḥadīth spirit that values the difficult act over the easy one, and by the report:

> *"A group among my community ceases not to follow the truth (ẓāhirīn ʿalā al-ḥaqq) until the Hour is come, unharmed by those who fail to support them and those who oppose them."* (related in the collections; cited by Ibn Kathīr)

The few of later times are not a defeated remnant; they are a fortified company.

**The Conditions That Thin the Ranks**

A ḥadīth describing the end times names the conditions that make the foremost rare: "rapacity reigns, passions are obeyed, the world is given priority, and each person admires his own opinion." Each of these four is a machine for producing the opposite of the *sābiqūn*. Rapacity destroys generosity. Obedience to passion destroys the governance of the soul by revelation. Prioritizing the world destroys the investment in the Hereafter. Self-admiration destroys the capacity to be taught. A society organized around these four will still produce believers, and many of them may be companions of the right; but the conditions for producing the foremost — those who outstrip in sincerity, initiative, and nearness — are hostile. Verse 14 is, among other things, a diagnosis of that hostility.

**Against the Myth of Continuous Progress**

From the standpoint of intellectual history, this verse is a direct challenge to any ideology that treats later generations as automatically wiser, freer, or more advanced than earlier ones. Technological progress is real; spiritual progress is not guaranteed by the calendar. The Qur'an entertains the possibility — indeed, it asserts the reality — of spiritual devolution alongside material development. A civilization may gain the ability to split the atom and lose the ability to govern the self. The few foremost of later times are those who refuse this trade.

**Personal Application: Choosing to Be Among the Few**

"Few" is not a sentence of exclusion; it is a description of a choice's cost. The path of the foremost is available in every generation. What verse 14 says is that fewer people take it. The reader who finds in himself a desire for nearness, a willingness to go first in good, and a refusal to let the age's lassitude set his ceiling, is being invited to swell the ranks of that few. In a time when the foremost are rare, each additional one is proportionally more precious — and, by Ibn ʿAjībah's logic, may be more greatly rewarded.

**The Higher Degree of the Later Few**

Ibn ʿAjībah's suggestion that the later few may be higher in quality because they awaken in a time of sleep is a mercy-reading of an austere verse. It matches the ḥadīth spirit that rewards the difficult act. Holding a coal of faith in an ice-age of heedlessness is a particular kind of foremost-making. Verse 14 is austere demography with a hidden door of honor.

**Refusing the Age's Ceiling**

Every age tries to set the ceiling of what is spiritually possible. Verse 14 says the ceiling of the foremost is still there, even when few reach it. Do not let the age's average become your maximum. The race is still on. The few are still being counted.

**Being Among the Later Few**

Few from later times is description of cost, not sentence of exclusion. Awakening in a time of sleep may carry special honor. Refuse the age's ceiling. Rapacity, obeyed passion, prioritized world, self-admired opinion — these four machines produce the opposite of sābiqūn. Resist the four. Join the few.

**Among the Foremost's Portrait**

This verse belongs to the sūrah's portrait of the sābiqūn al-muqarrabūn — those who outstripped and were brought nigh. Al-Rāzī discusses whether they pass the Reckoning lightly or not at all; Ibn Kathīr emphasizes the prophets among them; the spiritual tradition emphasizes the elite of nearness in every generation. Cross-reference with 83:18–28 (ʿIlliyyūn and the sealed wine of the near) and with the ḥadīth qudsī of drawing near through nawāfil (al-Bukhārī 6502) deepens the portrait. Verse 14 is one panel. Read it as part of the whole gallery of nearness, couches, drink without harm, fruits of choice, companionship like concealed pearls, and speech that is only peace. The foremost are defined by Whom they are near; everything else is hospitality.

---
## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:15

> **upon embroidered couches,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Furniture of Nearness**

*ʿAlā sururin mawḍūnah* — "upon embroidered couches," or "upon couches woven with gold" as some of the early exegetes glossed *mawḍūnah*. The word *surur* (singular *sarīr*) means couches or thrones — furniture of repose and of rank. *Mawḍūnah* is interpreted by al-Qurṭubī, al-Shawkānī, and others to mean encrusted or inwoven with gold, pearls, and rubies; the root suggests something laid or set in with ornamental work. The foremost, having been defined by nearness and located in gardens of bliss, are now shown in a posture: they are seated, and what they sit upon signals both comfort and honor.

**Couches Across the Qur'anic Paradise**

The image of the people of Paradise reclining upon couches recurs throughout the eschatological sūrahs:

> *"Reclining upon couches lined with silk brocade."* (Qur'an 55:54)

> *"They and their spouses, in shade, reclining on couches."* (Qur'an 36:56)

> *"Upon couches, gazing about."* (Qur'an 83:23)

The repetition is deliberate. In the desert culture of the first audience, prolonged reclining upon fine furniture was a luxury of the wealthy few. The Qur'an takes that rare luxury and makes it the ordinary furniture of the saved — and especially of the foremost. The eschatological imagination is being trained to expect a reversal: the posture of ease, which in this world is often purchased by the exploitation of others, is in the next world the reward of those who spent this life in the labor of faith.

**Thrones of Rank**

Some commentators emphasize that these couches are thrones, indicating that those reclining upon them have attained a high rank. The foremost are not merely comfortable; they are ennobled. Islamic theology has always held that Paradise contains ranks (*darajāt*) as Hell contains depths (*darakāt*). The embroidered couch is a visible marker of an invisible nearness. Just as earthly courts use furniture to signal hierarchy, the court of the Hereafter uses it — with the decisive difference that the hierarchy of the Hereafter tracks piety, patience, and nearness to God rather than blood, wealth, or force.

**Rest as a Theological Category**

There is a deeper point. The foremost spent their worldly lives as people who outstripped others — racing, striving, going first. Their defining verb was a verb of motion. In Paradise, their defining posture is repose. This is not inconsistency; it is consummation. The race was always toward a rest. The Prophet ﷺ used to say, when the hardship of the world pressed upon him, words to the effect that the true rest is that of the Hereafter. Verse 15 makes that rest visible. The couch is the opposite of the restless anxiety that characterizes so much of modern life. A culture that cannot sit still, that fills every silence with a screen, that treats busyness as a moral badge, is a culture that has forgotten what couches of the foremost are for. The believer practices for verse 15 every time he sits in the prayer's sitting posture with a present heart, or sits with the Qur'an without checking a phone, or sits with a grieving friend without rushing. Stillness, freely chosen and Godward, is rehearsal for embroidered couches.

**Surur Mawḍūnah and the Aesthetics of Honor**

Embroidered, inwoven, encrusted couches — the aesthetics of the foremost's seating are aesthetics of honor. Paradise is not bare minimality; it is beauty adequate to the Beautiful (*al-Jamīl*). God is beautiful and loves beauty (ḥadīth in Muslim). Verse 15 is one of the places that love of beauty becomes furniture.

**Rest After Racing**

The foremost raced. Their couches are the rest that racing was always toward. A culture that cannot rest cannot understand verse 15. Sabbathless worklessness-as-identity is not the prophetic way. The Prophet ﷺ worked intensely and also rested, married, joked, and enjoyed permitted good. The couch of the foremost is the perfection of a rest already practiced under God.

**Practicing Stillness**

Sit with the Qur'an without a phone. Sit with a grieving friend without rushing. Sit in the prayer's sitting posture with a present heart. Stillness freely chosen and Godward is rehearsal for embroidered couches. Verse 15's rest is the consummation of a race, not the reward of laziness. Learn both the race and the rest.

**Among the Foremost's Portrait**

This verse belongs to the sūrah's portrait of the sābiqūn al-muqarrabūn — those who outstripped and were brought nigh. Al-Rāzī discusses whether they pass the Reckoning lightly or not at all; Ibn Kathīr emphasizes the prophets among them; the spiritual tradition emphasizes the elite of nearness in every generation. Cross-reference with 83:18–28 (ʿIlliyyūn and the sealed wine of the near) and with the ḥadīth qudsī of drawing near through nawāfil (al-Bukhārī 6502) deepens the portrait. Verse 15 is one panel. Read it as part of the whole gallery of nearness, couches, drink without harm, fruits of choice, companionship like concealed pearls, and speech that is only peace. The foremost are defined by Whom they are near; everything else is hospitality.

---
## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:16

> **reclining upon them, facing one another.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Posture of Contentment**

*Muttakiʾīna ʿalayhā mutaqābilīn* — "reclining upon them, facing one another." The active participle *muttakiʾīn* from *ittakaʾa* means leaning, reclining, supporting oneself in a posture of ease. Several verses indicate that the people of Paradise recline upon couches, and the commentators consistently read this as a sign that they are content and at peace (see 18:31; 55:54). There is no vigilance left to maintain, no threat left to watch for, no status left to defend. The body itself has learned the lesson the soul already knows: it is safe.

**Facing One Another: The Democracy of Exaltation**

The second participle, *mutaqābilīn*, "facing one another," is theologically rich. It implies, as the commentators note, that none shall need to sit behind another, since all will be equally exalted in the fundamental dignity of salvation — even as ranks among them remain. Cross-references confirm the pattern:

> *"They will be reclining on couches facing each other."* (Qur'an 15:47; cf. 37:44; 44:53)

In earthly banquets, seating arrangements encode hierarchy with sometimes brutal clarity: who faces whom, who sits at the head, who is placed at the margin. In the banquet of the foremost, the geometry is circular or reciprocal rather than linear. Each faces each. The social anxiety that poisons so much human gathering — am I well placed? am I seen? am I slighted? — is structurally impossible. The architecture of the gathering enforces the equality of honor.

**The End of Envy**

Facing one another also implies the end of the particular disease that the Qur'an calls *ḥasad*, envy. In this world, even the righteous may feel a twinge when another is preferred. In that world, the preference of each is so completely fitted to each, and the joy of each is so transparent to the others, that envy finds no purchase. The Prophet ﷺ described the people of Paradise as having hearts like a single heart, without rancor. Verse 16's *mutaqābilīn* is the spatial expression of that interior unity. You can face someone fully only when you do not need to protect yourself from him.

**Conversation Without Corruption**

Later verses will specify that the speech of this company is "Peace! Peace!" (56:25–26). The reclining and facing of verse 16 is the bodily setup for that speech. In a world where so much conversation is angled — half-facing a phone, half-facing a person, performing for an invisible audience — the image of undistracted, reciprocal facing is itself a moral icon. The foremost are people who, already in this life, practiced the art of being present to one another. Paradise perfects the art.

**Practical Rehearsal: The Ethics of Facing**

A simple practice flows from this verse. When sitting with family, students, or fellow worshippers, practice facing them — body and attention. Put the phone away. Let the geometry of the gathering be *mutaqābilīn* rather than a row of people each facing a private screen. The Prophet's ﷺ gatherings were characterized by a quality of attention that his Companions never forgot. Verse 16 suggests that such attention is not merely good manners; it is a foretaste of the seating plan of the foremost.

**Mutaqābilīn and the End of Social Anxiety**

Facing one another without anyone behind is the end of the seating-chart anxiety that poisons earthly gatherings. No one is at the "bad" end of the table. The geometry of Paradise's company is the geometry of mutual honor. Verse 16 is social architecture as eschatological mercy.

**Practicing Reciprocal Facing**

Put the phone away. Face the person. Give the full attention that *mutaqābilīn* names. The gatherings of the Prophet ﷺ were remembered for a quality of presence that made each Companion feel he was the most honored. That quality is rehearsal for verse 16. The couch of the foremost is prepared by the ethics of attention now.

**Ethics of Facing**

When with family or companions, face them — body and attention. Let the geometry be mutaqābilīn rather than a row of private screens. The Prophet's gatherings were remembered for presence. Verse 16 suggests that such attention is not mere manners; it is foretaste of the foremost's seating plan. Envy finds no purchase where facing is full and hearts are one.

**Among the Foremost's Portrait**

This verse belongs to the sūrah's portrait of the sābiqūn al-muqarrabūn — those who outstripped and were brought nigh. Al-Rāzī discusses whether they pass the Reckoning lightly or not at all; Ibn Kathīr emphasizes the prophets among them; the spiritual tradition emphasizes the elite of nearness in every generation. Cross-reference with 83:18–28 (ʿIlliyyūn and the sealed wine of the near) and with the ḥadīth qudsī of drawing near through nawāfil (al-Bukhārī 6502) deepens the portrait. Verse 16 is one panel. Read it as part of the whole gallery of nearness, couches, drink without harm, fruits of choice, companionship like concealed pearls, and speech that is only peace. The foremost are defined by Whom they are near; everything else is hospitality.

---
## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:17

> **Immortal youths wait upon them**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Service Without Servitude's Sting**

*Yaṭūfu ʿalayhim wildānun mukhalladūn* — "Immortal youths wait upon them," or more literally, "there circulate among them youths made everlasting." The verb *yaṭūfu* means to go around, to circulate — the same root as *ṭawāf*, the circumambulation of the Kaʿbah. The service of these youths has a quality of graceful circulation rather than of burdensome labor. The word *wildān* means youths or lads; *mukhalladūn* means made permanent, immortal, preserved in a state that does not age or decay. They do not grow old in service. They do not tire. They do not resent.

**Who Are These Youths? The Range of Reports**

The Islamic tradition has preserved more than one identification. According to a saying attributed to the Prophet's Companion Salmān al-Fārsī, "The servants of heaven are the children of the idolaters" — those who died before the age of accountability and are neither rewarded for faith they did not live to choose nor punished for rejection they did not live to enact (cited by Ibn ʿAjībah, al-Qurṭubī, al-Shawkānī). In a statement attributed to ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib, they are said to be the children of Muslims (also preserved by al-Qurṭubī and al-Shawkānī). Both views share a common intuition: the youths who serve in Paradise are themselves recipients of mercy, not victims of exploitation. Their service is an honor and a joy, not a degradation. This is essential. Paradise contains no oppression. Whatever hierarchy of function exists within it is free of the coercion and contempt that taint earthly service.

**The Contrast with Earthly Service**

In the world the first audience knew — and in much of the world still — domestic service was often indistinguishable from exploitation. The Qur'an and the Sunnah fought this reality with legislation and moral teaching: the command to feed servants what one eats, to clothe them as one clothes oneself, to not burden them beyond capacity, and the repeated promise that the one who mistreats the vulnerable will face God as an adversary. Verse 17 completes that moral trajectory by depicting a service that has been entirely purged of harm. The foremost are served, but their being served does not require anyone else's diminishment. This is part of what makes Paradise paradise.

**Immortality as the End of Loss**

The adjective *mukhalladūn* deserves its own attention. One of the deepest wounds of earthly life is the wound of time: children grow up, youth vanishes, servants and friends and parents depart. The youths of Paradise are *mukhalladūn* — locked in a permanence that is not stagnation but the end of loss. Nothing that belongs to the foremost's joy will be taken from them by age or death. The Qur'an says of the people of Paradise more generally:

> *"They will not taste death therein except the first death."* (Qur'an 44:56)

Verse 17 particularizes that promise in the figures who circulate among them with the hospitality of the Garden.

**A Note on the Ethics of Reading**

Modern readers sometimes flinch at images of servants in Paradise, reading them through the lens of earthly injustice. The flinch is understandable and even morally useful when it sensitizes a person to injustice now. But it should not be allowed to misread the text. The youths of verse 17 are not the oppressed of the earth relocated to celestial kitchens. They are immortal beings in a realm without oppression, participating in a joy whose form includes the circulation of hospitality. The right response to the verse is not embarrassment; it is the resolve to make the service structures of this world as free of coercion as human law and conscience can manage — a resolve the Prophet ﷺ embodied in his own household.

**Mukhalladūn and the End of Temporal Loss**

Immortality of the serving youths means the joy of the foremost is not eroded by the aging or departure of those who serve them. Earthly joy is haunted by time. Paradise joy is not. Verse 17 names one specific form of that un-hauntedness: the attendants do not fade.

**Service Without Oppression**

The most important ethical note in the verse is that Paradise contains no oppression. Whatever the identity of the *wildān* — children of believers, children of disbelievers who died before accountability, or a special creation — their service is honor, not exploitation. Reading the verse through earthly injustice is understandable and should drive justice now; it should not rewrite the verse into a scandal. The Garden is clean of coercion.

**Service Structures Without Coercion**

Make the service structures of this world as free of coercion as law and conscience allow. Paradise's service is clean of oppression; that cleanliness should drive justice now. Verse 17's immortal youths are recipients of mercy in a realm without exploitation. The flinch at servant-imagery is useful when it sensitizes to injustice; it misleads when it rewrites the verse into scandal.

---
## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:18

> **with goblets, ewers, and a cup from a flowing spring,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Vessels as Metonym for the Drink**

*Bi-akwābin wa-abārīqa wa-kaʾsin min maʿīn* — "with goblets, ewers, and a cup from a flowing spring." The mention of drinking vessels is used, as the rhetoricians say, as a metonym for the drink itself. By naming the containers in three distinct forms — *akwāb* (goblets without handles), *abārīq* (ewers or flagons with spouts), and *kaʾs* (a cup of wine already poured) — the verse creates a sense of abundance, variety, and continuous service. The hospitality of the foremost is not a single offering but an ongoing circulation.

**Abārīq: The Glitter of Purity**

Al-Qurṭubī and al-Shawkānī note that *abārīq* is said to derive from the verb *baraqa*, "to shine, glitter, sparkle," and is thus taken to indicate vessels that glitter and sparkle from their purity. The material culture of Paradise is pure in both the moral and the aesthetic sense. Nothing is tarnished. Nothing carries the residue of illicit gain or careless manufacture. The vessels shine because what they hold is pure and because the realm they belong to is pure.

**From a Flowing Spring**

*Min maʿīn* — "from a flowing spring" (cf. 37:45) — indicates that the liquid is pure and the source never runs dry. Earthly hospitality is limited by supply. Even the most generous host eventually empties the jug. The spring of Paradise is defined by inexhaustibility. This is a recurring Qur'anic note: the rivers of Paradise flow continuously; the fruits are never out of season; the joy does not deplete the joy. The metaphysics of the Garden is the metaphysics of gift without scarcity.

**The Wine of Paradise Anticipated**

Although the explicit negation of wine's harms comes in the next verse, the *kaʾs* already signals that the drink of the foremost includes the wine of Paradise — a wine that the Qur'an describes in 47:15 as "rivers of wine delicious for those who drink." The earthly prohibition of wine (4:43; 5:90–91) is not a prohibition of joy; it is a prohibition of joy's counterfeit. The real thing is deferred to a realm where it cannot harm. Verse 18 sets the table for that deferred joy.

**Hospitality as a Divine Attribute Reflected**

God is *al-Karīm*, the Generous, and *al-Akram*, the Most Generous. The hospitality extended to the foremost is a created reflection of that divine generosity. The Prophet ﷺ was himself the most hospitable of people, and he taught that whoever believes in God and the Last Day should honor his guest. Every act of human hospitality in this world — a meal shared, a cup offered, a stranger welcomed — is a small, clumsy imitation of verse 18, and is loved by God for that reason. The believer who pours for others in this life is rehearsing the circulation of goblets that never end.

**The Ethics of Continuous Hospitality**

The circulation of goblets, ewers, and cups is not only a description of luxury; it is a description of a social order in which giving has no scarcity-anxiety. In this world, even generous hosts glance at the diminishing bottle. In that world, the spring flows. The foremost, who spent their lives giving when it cost them, enter a realm where giving and receiving are no longer shadowed by depletion. The Prophet ﷺ said that the upper hand (the giving hand) is better than the lower hand (the receiving hand). Verse 18 shows a realm where the distinction is transformed: everyone is served, and the service itself is an honor, and the source never runs dry. The believer who keeps an open table in this world — within his means, without ostentation — is practicing a small version of the flowing spring.

**Three Vessels, One Hospitality**

Goblets without handles (*akwāb*), ewers with spouts (*abārīq*), and the poured cup (*kaʾs*) together suggest a complete service: storage, pouring, and drinking. The foremost are not handed a single static glass; they are surrounded by a circulating hospitality that matches the circulating youths of verse 17. The spring (*maʿīn*) that feeds this service never becomes a negotiation. In earthly banquets, even kings glance at supply. In this banquet, supply is a divine attribute expressed as liquid. The believer who keeps water and food available for guests, travelers, and the poor is already living in the outer courtyard of this verse.

**Open Table as Rehearsal**

Keep an open table within your means. Pour for others. The circulation of goblets that never end is rehearsed by the circulation of ordinary hospitality now. God is al-Karīm; human hospitality is a clumsy imitation He loves. Verse 18's flowing spring is the eschatological form of a generosity the foremost already practiced when it cost them.

**Among the Foremost's Portrait**

This verse belongs to the sūrah's portrait of the sābiqūn al-muqarrabūn — those who outstripped and were brought nigh. Al-Rāzī discusses whether they pass the Reckoning lightly or not at all; Ibn Kathīr emphasizes the prophets among them; the spiritual tradition emphasizes the elite of nearness in every generation. Cross-reference with 83:18–28 (ʿIlliyyūn and the sealed wine of the near) and with the ḥadīth qudsī of drawing near through nawāfil (al-Bukhārī 6502) deepens the portrait. Verse 18 is one panel. Read it as part of the whole gallery of nearness, couches, drink without harm, fruits of choice, companionship like concealed pearls, and speech that is only peace. The foremost are defined by Whom they are near; everything else is hospitality.

---
## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:19

> **wherefrom they suffer neither headache nor stupefaction,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Negation That Defines the Drink**

*Lā yuṣaddaʿūna ʿanhā wa-lā yunzifūn* — "wherefrom they suffer neither headache nor stupefaction." After naming the vessels and the spring, the sūrah defines the drink by what it does *not* do. Earthly wine is known by its aftermath: the headache (*ṣudāʿ*), the clouding of the mind, the addiction, the quarrel, the loss of dignity. The Qur'an forbids it in this world precisely because of these harms (5:90–91). The wine of Paradise is defined as the same pleasure with the harms surgically removed. *Lā yuṣaddaʿūna* — they are not stricken with headache from it. *Wa-lā yunzifūn* — they are not intoxicated, stupefied, or drained by it. The second verb can also be read *lā yunzifūn* as describing the springs themselves: a well that does not dry up, indicating that this wine will never cease to flow (al-Qurṭubī). Both readings are nourishing: the drinker is unharmed, and the supply is unending.

**Cross-Reference: The Wine of the Righteous**

Sūrah al-Muṭaffifīn offers a parallel description:

> *"They will be given to drink a pure sealed wine, whose seal is musk. So for that let the competitors compete. And its mixture is of Tasnīm, a spring from which those nearest to Allah drink."* (Qur'an 83:25–28)

Notice that Tasnīm is specifically the spring of the *muqarrabūn* — those brought nigh. The foremost of al-Wāqiʿah and the nearest of al-Muṭaffifīn drink from a source that others may receive only as mixture. Rank in Paradise is rank in access to purity.

**The Poet's Gloss: ʿUmar ibn al-Fāriḍ**

The great mystical poet ʿUmar ibn al-Fāriḍ (d. 1235) wrote in his *Wine Ode* of a wine that "refines the character of those who drink it: the irresolute it guides to the path of resolve, the miser's hand it makes generous, and when anger strikes it instills forbearance in one with no clemency." Whether one reads the ode as allegory of divine love or as eschatological anticipation, it captures the tradition's insistence that the wine of Paradise is transformative in the direction of virtue, not vice. Earthly wine loosens the tongue for harm; the wine of *naʿīm* loosens the heart for good.

**The Logic of Deferred Pleasure**

Why not permit a harmless wine in this world? Because in this world, the human being is under trial, the self is prone to excess, and the social harms of intoxication are empirically massive — broken families, violence, addiction, the clouding of the very reason that religion addresses. The prohibition is not ascetic hatred of pleasure; it is diagnostic realism about this world's conditions. Verse 19 is the promise that the pleasure itself was never the enemy. The enemy was the headache, the stupefaction, the social ruin. Remove those, and the cup is not only permitted but celebrated.

**Modern Resonance: Pleasure Without Aftermath**

Much of contemporary consumer culture is a failed search for exactly what verse 19 describes: stimulation without crash, enjoyment without addiction, celebration without regret. The products that promise this — designer intoxicants, curated experiences, endless entertainment — always smuggle the aftermath back in. Verse 19 says the search is valid and the destination is real, but the destination is not for sale in this world's markets. It is reserved for a realm where the self has been purified enough to bear unmixed joy. The practical implication is patience and sobriety now, in the name of a joy that will not need to be recovered from later.

**Wine's Double Record**

Earthly wine is forbidden because of its social and spiritual harms (5:90–91). Heavenly wine is celebrated because those harms are removed (56:19; 37:47; 83:25–28). The double record confounds both the puritan who hates pleasure and the libertine who hates limits. Pleasure was never the enemy. Harm was the enemy. Verse 19 is the surgical separation of the two.

**Ibn al-Fāriḍ and the Transformative Cup**

The mystical poet's Wine Ode describes a drink that produces resolve, generosity, and forbearance rather than their opposites. Whether read as allegory of divine love or as eschatological anticipation, it captures the tradition's insistence that the cup of Paradise improves the drinker. Earthly intoxication degrades. Heavenly drinking elevates. The contrast is the point.

**Patience for Unmixed Joy**

Seeking stimulation without crash is valid; the destination is real; it is not for sale in this world's markets. Verse 19 separates pleasure from harm surgically. Sobriety now is not hatred of joy; it is patience for a joy that will not need recovery later. The cup of Paradise improves the drinker. Wait for that cup under the law of this world's prohibition.

---
## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:20

> **with fruits as they choose,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Choice as a Feature of Paradise**

*Wa-fākihatin mimmā yatakhayyarūn* — "and with fruits as they choose." The verb *yatakhayyarūn* from *ikhtiyār* means to select, to prefer, to pick according to one's own inclination. The fruits of Paradise are not a fixed menu imposed upon the foremost; they are an array from which the foremost take what they wish. This small detail carries large theological weight. Paradise is not a uniform experience identically applied to every resident. It is responsive. It fits itself to the chooser. The God who created individual temperaments does not erase them in the Garden; He hosts them.

**Fruits Across the Eschatological Landscape**

The Qur'an returns again and again to the fruits of Paradise:

> *"They will have fruits therein and whatever they desire."* (Qur'an 36:57)

> *"And fruits of the kind they desire."* (Qur'an 77:42; cf. 37:42; 38:51; 43:73; 52:22)

The low-hanging clusters of 69:23 and the "fruits near at hand" of 55:54 add the note of effortlessness: the fruit is not only chosen but easily reached. In this world, the best produce is often expensive, seasonal, or distant. In that world, the best is the standard, and it answers to desire without delay.

**The Ethics of Desire Purified**

Islamic spirituality does not treat desire (*shahwah*, *raghbah*) as evil in itself. It treats *unregulated* desire as dangerous and *illicit* desire as forbidden. Desire that has been trained by revelation — desire for what God has allowed and promised — is part of the human design and is fulfilled without remainder in Paradise. Verse 20 is a validation of the chooser. The foremost are people whose capacity to choose has been refined, not erased. They choose fruits; they do not choose harm, because the capacity for choosing harm has no object left in a realm where harm does not exist.

**Hadith: "Whatsoever They Will"**

The Qur'an states repeatedly that the inhabitants of Paradise shall have whatsoever they will (16:31; 25:16; 39:34; 42:22; 50:35). The Prophet ﷺ conveyed the divine saying:

> *"I have prepared for My righteous servants what no eye has seen and no ear has heard and what has not occurred to the heart of any human being."* (al-Bukhārī 3244; Muslim 2824; ḥadīth qudsī)

Verse 20's fruits are one visible edge of that invisible preparation. The chooser chooses; and even the chooser's imagination is smaller than what has been prepared.

**Practical Note: Learning to Choose Well Now**

If Paradise includes the dignity of choice, then learning to choose well in this world is part of the preparation. Every decision — what to eat, what to consume with the eyes and ears, what to pursue with the hours of the day — is practice for a chooser. The person who has never disciplined choice on earth is not well prepared for the freedom of the Garden. The person who has learned to prefer the pure over the polluted, the lasting over the flashy, the generous over the selfish, is already moving in the grammar of *yatakhayyarūn*.

**Ikhtiyār as Dignity**

The ability to choose fruits is a dignity conferred on the foremost. They are not processed through a uniform cafeteria of identical experience. God, who created individual temperaments, hosts them individually. Verse 20 is a small verse with a large anthropology: personhood survives into Paradise and is honored there.

**Training Choice Now**

Every choice in this world trains the chooser. Preferring the pure, the just, the kind, and the lasting is practice for *yatakhayyarūn*. Preferring the corrupt, the easy-unjust, and the flashy is practice for a different menu. Verse 20 quietly asks what kind of chooser you are becoming.

**Fruits of Choice and the Dignity of Preference**

God could have issued a single fruit to all the saved. Instead He issues an array and honors preference. This is continuous with the Qur'an's wider affirmation of human diversity within a shared guidance. Temperaments differ; tastes differ; the Garden hosts the differences. Verse 20 is a quiet celebration of personhood's survival into eternity. The self that chose good in the trial is not erased at the gate; it is welcomed as a chooser still.

**Training the Chooser**

Every decision trains the chooser you are becoming. Prefer the pure over the polluted, the lasting over the flashy, the generous over the selfish — this is the grammar of yatakhayyarūn. Verse 20's fruits of choice honor personhood in Paradise. Personhood is shaped now. What kind of chooser are you practicing to be?

**Among the Foremost's Portrait**

This verse belongs to the sūrah's portrait of the sābiqūn al-muqarrabūn — those who outstripped and were brought nigh. Al-Rāzī discusses whether they pass the Reckoning lightly or not at all; Ibn Kathīr emphasizes the prophets among them; the spiritual tradition emphasizes the elite of nearness in every generation. Cross-reference with 83:18–28 (ʿIlliyyūn and the sealed wine of the near) and with the ḥadīth qudsī of drawing near through nawāfil (al-Bukhārī 6502) deepens the portrait. Verse 20 is one panel. Read it as part of the whole gallery of nearness, couches, drink without harm, fruits of choice, companionship like concealed pearls, and speech that is only peace. The foremost are defined by Whom they are near; everything else is hospitality.

## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:21

> **and the meat of birds as they desire,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Desire Answered Without Remainder**

*Wa-laḥmi ṭayrin mimmā yashtahūn* — "and the meat of birds as they desire." Parallel to the fruits of choice in the previous verse, the meat of birds answers to *shahwah*, desire or appetite. The two verbs — *yatakhayyarūn* (they choose) and *yashtahūn* (they desire) — cover both deliberative preference and spontaneous appetite. Paradise answers both.

**The Birds of Paradise in the Hadith**

The birds of Paradise are described in several reports as multicolored creatures with extraordinary necks and wings. Yet when people expressed wonder at their beauty, the Prophet ﷺ is reported to have said:

> *"Those who eat them are more blessed than are they."* (cited by al-Qurṭubī and al-Suyūṭī)

The point is characteristic of the Prophetic pedagogy: do not be so captivated by the beauty of the provision that you forget the greater beauty of the one provided for. The guest is more honored than the dish. The foremost are the point of the banquet.

**Cross-References**

> *"And We will provide them with fruit and meat from whatever they desire."* (Qur'an 52:22)

> *"And fruits of the kind they desire."* (Qur'an 77:42)

The pairing of fruit and meat is itself a sign of completeness: the garden's produce and the sky's creatures, the growing and the flying, both made table-fare for the people of nearness.

**Desire (*Ishtihāʾ*) Purified**

In Islamic moral psychology, *shahwah* is a neutral faculty that becomes praiseworthy or blameworthy according to its object and its regulation. The Qur'an does not promise the foremost the extinction of desire; it promises the fulfillment of desire in a register where fulfillment does not corrupt. This is a more humane eschatology than those that treat the body and its appetites as enemies to be annihilated. The body is returned, perfected, and fed.

**Modern Reflection**

Food culture in the modern world oscillates between indulgence and anxiety — binge and diet, craving and shame. Verse 21 offers a third horizon: appetite without disorder. The believer who eats with the name of God, stops before excess, and shares what he has is already practicing a small version of the table of the foremost. The full version is coming.

**Desire as a Theological Category**

Islamic moral theology distinguishes carefully among types of desire. *Shahwah* directed toward the forbidden is a trial to be resisted. *Shahwah* directed toward the permitted is a mercy to be received with thanks. *Shahwah* directed toward the Hereafter is a virtue to be cultivated. Verse 21's *mimmā yashtahūn* validates the third and the purified form of the second. The foremost are not passionless intellects; they are whole human beings whose appetites have been trained to point in the right direction. The meat of birds as they desire is the destination of a lifetime of saying "no" to the wrong desires and "yes" to the right ones.

**Hadith Imagery of the Birds**

Reports describing the birds of Paradise emphasize colors and forms beyond earthly taxonomy — not as fantasy for its own sake, but as a way of breaking the imagination free from the closed circle of present experience. The Prophet's ﷺ remark that those who eat them are more blessed than the birds themselves recenters the hierarchy: the guest of God is the point of the banquet. Wonder at the provision should lead to wonder at the Provider and at the honor of being provided for.

**Birds, Desire, and the Prophetic Correction**

When the Companions wondered at the beauty of Paradise's birds, the Prophet ﷺ redirected wonder to the eaters: those who eat them are more blessed. The correction is permanent pedagogy. Do not let the beauty of provision outshine the honor of being God's guest. Verse 21's meat of birds as they desire is provision; the foremost are the point. Desire answered is the hospitality; nearness is the host.

**Desire Redirected**

Shahwah is neutral until directed. Forbidden desire is trial; permitted desire is mercy; Hereafter-directed desire is virtue. Verse 21 validates purified appetite. The body is returned, perfected, and fed. Do not treat the body as enemy; treat its unregulated drive as the enemy. The meat of birds as they desire is the destination of a lifetime of right nos and right yeses.

**Among the Foremost's Portrait**

This verse belongs to the sūrah's portrait of the sābiqūn al-muqarrabūn — those who outstripped and were brought nigh. Al-Rāzī discusses whether they pass the Reckoning lightly or not at all; Ibn Kathīr emphasizes the prophets among them; the spiritual tradition emphasizes the elite of nearness in every generation. Cross-reference with 83:18–28 (ʿIlliyyūn and the sealed wine of the near) and with the ḥadīth qudsī of drawing near through nawāfil (al-Bukhārī 6502) deepens the portrait. Verse 21 is one panel. Read it as part of the whole gallery of nearness, couches, drink without harm, fruits of choice, companionship like concealed pearls, and speech that is only peace. The foremost are defined by Whom they are near; everything else is hospitality.

---
## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:22

> **and [there shall be] wide-eyed maidens,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Companionship as a Created Mercy**

*Wa-ḥūrun ʿīn* — "and wide-eyed maidens." The word *ḥūr* (singular *ḥawrāʾ*) refers to beings of intense contrast between the white and black of the eye — a classical Arabic mark of striking beauty — and by extension to the pure companions of Paradise. *ʿĪn* means large-eyed. The phrase is one of the Qur'an's recurring descriptions of the companionship of the Garden (cf. 44:54; 52:20; 55:72).

**Two Grammatical Readings**

Commentators note that this phrase may be read as the subject of the sentence along with the immortal youths of verse 17, meaning that both serve the foremost; or it may be read as another of the blessings that the foremost receive. Both readings affirm that the companionship of Paradise is part of the reward of nearness. Islamic eschatology is not a solitary beatific vision only; it is a social and relational joy, including purified spousal intimacy.

**The Wider Theology of Companionship**

The Qur'an treats companionship — spousal and otherwise — as one of the great goods of existence:

> *"And among His signs is that He created for you from yourselves mates, that you may find rest in them; and He placed between you love and mercy."* (Qur'an 30:21)

Paradise does not abolish this good; it perfects it. The *ḥūr ʿīn* are mentioned as a specific creation of the Garden, while other verses affirm that believing spouses will be reunited (36:56; 43:70). The tradition holds both truths: the companionship of earthly spouses who believed, and the additional companionship of the Garden's own creation.

**Against Reduction and Against Embarrassment**

Two opposite misreadings must be avoided. One reduces Paradise to sensuality and misses the primacy of nearness to God. The other, out of modern embarrassment, tries to delete the sensual and relational dimensions entirely. The Qur'an refuses both. Verse 22 sits inside a passage that began with "those brought nigh" (v. 11). The companionship is the hospitality of nearness, not a replacement for it.

**Recompense Previewed**

The following verses will make explicit that all of this is "as a recompense for that which they used to do" (v. 24). The companionship of the Garden is not arbitrary decoration; it is the fruit of a life that honored covenants, lowered the gaze where required, and treated intimacy as a trust rather than a prey.

**Companionship and the Prophetic Household**

The Prophet ﷺ loved his wives, honored them, consulted them, and taught that the best of the believers are those best to their families. The Qur'anic descriptions of Paradise's companionship stand in continuity with that Prophetic ethic, not in tension with it. Verse 22 is not an escape from the ethics of marriage; it is their eschatological crown. Those who treated intimacy as covenant rather than consumption, who guarded their gazes, who were gentle in their homes, are the ones for whom the companionship of the Garden is a fitting *jazāʾ*.

**Reading with Adab**

The verse should be read with the same adab the tradition has always asked for: without prurience, without embarrassment, without reduction of Paradise to a single dimension. The foremost are defined first by nearness to God (v. 11). Everything else in their portrait — including this — is the hospitality of that nearness. To invert the order, making companionship the center and nearness the periphery, is to misread the sūrah's own sequence.

**Ḥūr ʿĪn and the Ethics That Precede Them**

The companionship of the Garden is *jazāʾ* for lives that honored covenants. Lowering the gaze, guarding chastity, treating spouses with kindness, repenting from falls — these are the earthly grammar of which verse 22 is the eschatological literature. Reading the verse without the ethic empties it into fantasy. Reading the ethic without the verse starves hope. The sūrah gives both, in sequence, under the heading of those brought nigh.

**Covenant Before Companionship**

The companionship of the Garden crowns lives that honored covenants. Read the verse with adab — without prurience, without embarrassment, without reducing Paradise to one dimension. Nearness to God (v. 11) is the definition of the foremost; companionship is the hospitality of that nearness. Keep the order the sūrah keeps.

**Among the Foremost's Portrait**

This verse belongs to the sūrah's portrait of the sābiqūn al-muqarrabūn — those who outstripped and were brought nigh. Al-Rāzī discusses whether they pass the Reckoning lightly or not at all; Ibn Kathīr emphasizes the prophets among them; the spiritual tradition emphasizes the elite of nearness in every generation. Cross-reference with 83:18–28 (ʿIlliyyūn and the sealed wine of the near) and with the ḥadīth qudsī of drawing near through nawāfil (al-Bukhārī 6502) deepens the portrait. Verse 22 is one panel. Read it as part of the whole gallery of nearness, couches, drink without harm, fruits of choice, companionship like concealed pearls, and speech that is only peace. The foremost are defined by Whom they are near; everything else is hospitality.

---
## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:23

> **the likeness of concealed pearls,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Beauty That Has Been Protected**

*Ka-amthāli al-luʾluʾi al-maknūn* — "the likeness of concealed pearls." The simile does two things at once. It indicates exquisite beauty — the pearl was among the most valued objects in the ancient world — and it indicates untouched purity. *Maknūn* means hidden, treasured, kept covered, sheltered from dust and handling. The same image appears in 55:56 and 55:74, which describe the companions of Paradise as untouched before by man or jinn.

**The Pearl in Arabic Imagination**

Pearls in classical Arabic poetry and prose symbolize rarity, luminosity, and the beauty that is enhanced by being withheld from common view. A pearl displayed in the market is valuable; a pearl still in its shell, or kept in a sealed casket, carries an additional aura of exclusivity and care. The Qur'an takes this cultural resonance and sanctifies it. The companions of the foremost are not public spectacles; they are treasured.

**Purity as a Two-Directional Virtue**

The concealment of the pearl is not only about the companions; it reflects back upon the foremost themselves. They are people who, in the world of trial, practiced concealment of what should be concealed — the lowering of the gaze (24:30–31), the guarding of chastity (23:5–7), the refusal to treat other human beings as objects of consumption. The pearl-likeness of the reward matches the pearl-likeness of the deed. God gives the pure what corresponds to purity.

**The Famous Hadith Qudsi of Hidden Reward**

Regarding all such allusions to the blessings of Paradise, the ḥadīth qudsī already cited remains the governing frame:

> *"I have prepared for My righteous servants what no eye has seen and no ear has heard and what has not occurred to the heart of any human being; so recite if you will: 'No soul knows what comfort is kept hidden for it as a reward for what they used to do.'"* (al-Bukhārī 3244; Muslim 2824; cf. Qur'an 32:17)

Even the pearl image is only an approximation. The reality is *maknūn* in a deeper sense: hidden from every earthly imagination.

**Practical Parallel**

In a culture of permanent display — where worth is measured by visibility and intimacy is frequently performed for audiences — the concealed pearl is a counter-icon. Some beauties are increased by being guarded. Some joys are deepened by not being posted. The believer who protects the private sphere of marriage, family, and personal devotion is already living in the grammar of *al-luʾluʾ al-maknūn*.

**Maknūn: The Aesthetics of the Guarded**

Concealed pearls are more luminous in the imagination than pearls on open trays. The Qur'an uses that aesthetic for the companionship of Paradise and, later, for the Book itself (v. 78). What is treasured is guarded. What is guarded is known to be valuable. Verse 23 teaches a counter-cultural aesthetics in an age of mandatory display: some beauty increases by not being posted.

**The Hidden Reward**

The ḥadīth qudsī of the hidden reward (32:17's atmosphere; al-Bukhārī 3244) governs all sensory descriptions of Paradise. Eye has not seen, ear has not heard, heart has not conceived. The pearl image is an approximation pointing beyond itself. Verse 23 is a finger toward a moon that exceeds every pearl.

**Pearl as Pedagogy**

The concealed pearl teaches that value and visibility are not identical. Some of the most valuable things in the religious life — private tears, hidden charity, a marriage protected from display, a night prayer no one knows — are *maknūn*. Verse 23 eschatologizes that pedagogy. The companionship of the foremost is pearl-like because the foremost themselves learned to treasure what is guarded. God gives the guarded to the guardians.

**Guarding What Increases by Being Guarded**

Some beauties increase by being guarded. Private tears, hidden charity, protected marriage, unposted devotion — these are maknūn. Verse 23's concealed pearls eschatologize that pedagogy. In an age of mandatory display, the pearl is a counter-icon. Treasure what should be treasured by not performing it for audiences that do not own it.

**Among the Foremost's Portrait**

This verse belongs to the sūrah's portrait of the sābiqūn al-muqarrabūn — those who outstripped and were brought nigh. Al-Rāzī discusses whether they pass the Reckoning lightly or not at all; Ibn Kathīr emphasizes the prophets among them; the spiritual tradition emphasizes the elite of nearness in every generation. Cross-reference with 83:18–28 (ʿIlliyyūn and the sealed wine of the near) and with the ḥadīth qudsī of drawing near through nawāfil (al-Bukhārī 6502) deepens the portrait. Verse 23 is one panel. Read it as part of the whole gallery of nearness, couches, drink without harm, fruits of choice, companionship like concealed pearls, and speech that is only peace. The foremost are defined by Whom they are near; everything else is hospitality.

## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:24

> **as a recompense for that which they used to do.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Gift and Wage in a Single Word**

*Jazāʾan bi-mā kānū yaʿmalūn* — "as a recompense for that which they used to do." The word *jazāʾ* means recompense, repayment, requital — a matching of outcome to action. After the cascade of blessings — nearness, gardens, couches, youths, drink, fruits, birds, companions — the sūrah pauses to name the principle: this is not arbitrary favoritism. It is continuous with what the foremost used to do.

**Divine Justice and Divine Grace Together**

Islamic theology holds together two truths that lesser systems often separate. On the one hand, Paradise is a *jazāʾ*, a recompense earned under the terms God Himself established. On the other hand, no one enters Paradise by deeds alone in such a way as to put God in debt. The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"None of you will enter Paradise by his deeds alone."* They said, "Not even you, O Messenger of Allah?" He said, *"Not even I, unless Allah covers me with His mercy."* (al-Bukhārī 6463; Muslim 2816)

The resolution is that deeds are the divinely appointed *cause* and *sign* of entrance, while mercy is the ultimate *ground*. Verse 24 names the cause; the mercy is assumed throughout the Qur'an's eschatology. The foremost worked; God gave. Both statements are true.

**"Used to Do": The Habit of Good**

The verb form *kānū yaʿmalūn* — "they used to do" — indicates ongoing, habitual action, not a single dramatic gesture. The foremost are not people of one heroic day; they are people of a direction sustained across a lifetime. This is pastorally crucial. Many Muslims wait for a moment of extraordinary virtue to define them. The Qur'an more often praises the steady: those who pray consistently, give consistently, tell the truth consistently, repent consistently. The recompense matches the pattern, not the spike.

**Works (*ʿAmal*) as the Currency of the Path**

The root *ʿ-m-l* covers the whole field of human doing — ritual, ethical, social, interior. The foremost's works included the obligatory and the supererogatory, the public and the hidden, the acts of the limbs and the acts of the heart. Ibn Taymiyyah and the scholars of spiritual conduct emphasized that the actions of the heart — love, fear, hope, sincerity, trust — are themselves *aʿmāl* and are weighed. Verse 24's "what they used to do" is therefore not a bare externalism. It is the total life-direction of the person.

**Motivation Without Transactional Coldness**

A danger in reading *jazāʾ* is reducing the relationship with God to a commercial transaction: I pay deeds, He pays gardens. The Qur'an prevents this reduction by repeatedly centering the Face of God (*wajh Allāh*) as the ultimate object of seeking. The foremost did what they did seeking His Face; the gardens are the hospitality of the One whose Face they sought. The recompense is real, and the love is real, and neither cancels the other.

**Jazāʾ and Raḥmah**

Recompense and mercy are not rivals in Islamic theology. Deeds are the appointed path; mercy is the ground of arrival. The Prophet ﷺ denied that anyone enters Paradise by deeds alone, including himself, except as God covers with mercy (al-Bukhārī 6463). Verse 24 names the path-side (*jazāʾ bi-mā kānū yaʿmalūn*); the mercy-side is assumed in every promise of the Garden. Hold both.

**Habitual Works**

*Kānū yaʿmalūn* indicates habit, not spike. The foremost are people of direction sustained across time. A single heroic day does not define a destiny; a pattern does. This is liberating for those who fear they have missed their heroic day, and sobering for those who rely on one good season to cover a life of neglect.

**Works of the Limbs and Works of the Heart**

*Bi-mā kānū yaʿmalūn* covers the full range of *ʿamal*. Ibn Taymiyyah and the scholars of the heart insisted that love, fear, hope, sincerity, and trust are themselves works and are weighed. The foremost outstripped others not only in prostrations counted but in hearts directed. Verse 24's recompense is for the total life-direction. Reduce it to externalism and you misread both the verse and the people it describes.

**Pattern Over Spike**

Build patterns, not only spikes. Habitual prayer, habitual truth, habitual repentance, habitual charity — these are kānū yaʿmalūn. Verse 24's recompense matches the pattern. A single heroic day does not define a destiny. Hold jazāʾ and raḥmah together: deeds are the path; mercy is the ground. Seek God's Face in the works, and the gardens will be hospitality of the Face sought.

**Among the Foremost's Portrait**

This verse belongs to the sūrah's portrait of the sābiqūn al-muqarrabūn — those who outstripped and were brought nigh. Al-Rāzī discusses whether they pass the Reckoning lightly or not at all; Ibn Kathīr emphasizes the prophets among them; the spiritual tradition emphasizes the elite of nearness in every generation. Cross-reference with 83:18–28 (ʿIlliyyūn and the sealed wine of the near) and with the ḥadīth qudsī of drawing near through nawāfil (al-Bukhārī 6502) deepens the portrait. Verse 24 is one panel. Read it as part of the whole gallery of nearness, couches, drink without harm, fruits of choice, companionship like concealed pearls, and speech that is only peace. The foremost are defined by Whom they are near; everything else is hospitality.

## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:25

> **They hear no idle talk therein, nor incitement to sin,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Soundscape of the Garden**

*Lā yasmaʿūna fīhā laghwan wa-lā taʾthīmā* — "They hear no idle talk therein, nor incitement to sin." After the visual and gustatory blessings, the sūrah turns to the auditory environment of the foremost. Paradise has a soundscape, and what is absent from it is as important as what is present.

**Laghw: The Weight of Weightless Speech**

*Laghw* means idle talk, vanity, frivolous speech, the chatter that fills time without filling the heart. The Qur'an praises believers as those who turn away from *laghw* (23:3) and describes the Garden repeatedly as free of it (19:62; 78:35; 88:11). In a world saturated with noise — notifications, commentary, outrage cycles, ambient media — the promise of a realm without *laghw* is almost physically relieving. The ears of the foremost will rest.

**Taʾthīm: No Incitement to Sin**

*Taʾthīm* means the attribution or incitement of sin, and by extension lying or any form of sinful speech (cf. 52:23). Not only is empty speech absent; speech that leads toward harm is absent. No gossip. No mockery. No seduction toward what God has forbidden. No ideological pressure to betray conscience. The social atmosphere of the Garden is safe for the soul.

**Cross-References**

> *"They will not hear therein ill speech or commission of sin — only a saying: 'Peace, peace.'"* (Qur'an 56:25–26, reading ahead)

> *"They will not hear therein any idle talk."* (Qur'an 88:11)

> *"There they will not hear any idle talk, but only peace."* (Qur'an 19:62)

The consistency of this motif across Makkan and Madinan sūrahs shows how central the purification of speech is to the Qur'anic vision of felicity.

**Speech as a Moral Environment**

Modern ethics often focuses on actions and neglects speech ecosystems. The Qur'an does not. It treats the verbal environment as a shaper of character. A person who spends years in *laghw* and *taʾthīm* — even as a consumer rather than a producer — is being formed by them. Verse 25 implies that the foremost were already people who curated their hearing in this world: avoiding backbiting sessions, refusing to circulate rumors, leaving gatherings of mockery. The silence of the Garden is prepared by the disciplines of the tongue and the ear on earth.

**The Prophet's Teaching on the Tongue**

> *"Whoever believes in Allah and the Last Day, let him speak good or remain silent."* (al-Bukhārī 6018; Muslim 47)

And:

> *"A servant may say a word that no attention is paid to, and by it slip into the Fire farther than the distance between east and west."* (al-Bukhārī 6477; Muslim 2988)

Verse 25 is the eschatological counterpart of these warnings: a place where the tongue's danger has been permanently retired.

**Curating the Soundscape Now**

If Paradise is free of *laghw* and *taʾthīm*, the believer practices for it by curating what he listens to. Backbiting circles, mockery platforms, rage-media, and empty chatter are anti-rehearsals. The Prophetic rule — speak good or remain silent — is the practical form of verse 25 in the trial-world. The ears are a gate of the heart; verse 25 describes the heart's final auditory environment.

**Speech Ecosystems**

Modern ethics often underweights speech ecosystems. The Qur'an does not. It treats verbal environments as formative. A workplace, family, or friendship-group saturated with *laghw* and *taʾthīm* is forming companions of a different destiny. Verse 25 implies a duty to build speech-environments that anticipate the Garden's soundscape: truthful, gentle, useful, peace-bearing.

**The Prophet's Guard on the Tongue**

> *"Whoever believes in Allah and the Last Day, let him speak good or remain silent."* (al-Bukhārī 6018; Muslim 47)

Verse 25 is the eschatological environment that this ḥadīth practices for. The tongue that learned silence and good speech in the trial enters a realm where idle talk and sinful incitement simply do not exist. The training was never arbitrary; it was pre-adaptation to the Garden's soundscape.

**Curate What You Hear**

Leave backbiting sessions. Refuse rumor circulation. Exit mockery gatherings. Curate feeds and conversations. Verse 25's soundscape of the Garden is prepared by the disciplines of ear and tongue now. Speech ecosystems form souls. Build ecosystems that anticipate laghw-free, taʾthīm-free air. Speak good or remain silent.

**Among the Foremost's Portrait**

This verse belongs to the sūrah's portrait of the sābiqūn al-muqarrabūn — those who outstripped and were brought nigh. Al-Rāzī discusses whether they pass the Reckoning lightly or not at all; Ibn Kathīr emphasizes the prophets among them; the spiritual tradition emphasizes the elite of nearness in every generation. Cross-reference with 83:18–28 (ʿIlliyyūn and the sealed wine of the near) and with the ḥadīth qudsī of drawing near through nawāfil (al-Bukhārī 6502) deepens the portrait. Verse 25 is one panel. Read it as part of the whole gallery of nearness, couches, drink without harm, fruits of choice, companionship like concealed pearls, and speech that is only peace. The foremost are defined by Whom they are near; everything else is hospitality.

## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:26

> **save that "Peace! Peace!" is uttered.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Only Speech Left Is Peace**

*Illā qīlan salāman salāmā* — "save that 'Peace! Peace!' is uttered." After negating idle talk and sinful speech, the sūrah names the positive content of the Garden's discourse. The exception is not a partial return of ordinary conversation; it is the elevation of speech to a single, sufficient word: *salām*.

**The Meanings of Salām**

*Salām* in Arabic is a dense word. It means peace, safety, soundness, freedom from defect, and is one of the Beautiful Names of God: *al-Salām*, the Source of Peace. As employed here, the commentators note, it comprises spiritual soundness, freedom from all evils and defects, and inner contentment. When the inhabitants of the Garden say *salām*, or hear it said, they are participating in a speech that both describes and enacts their state.

**Three Readings of the Verse**

The tradition offers complementary readings: that the inhabitants will hear only the greeting "Peace be upon you," with which Muslims already greet one another in this world (al-Shawkānī); that they will hear only peaceful speech in general; or that they will hear the words "Peace, Peace" as the very discourse of the Garden (Ibn ʿAjībah). Ibn ʿAjībah adds that the discourse of the people of the Garden is said to be remembrance of God and wisdom. All three readings agree that the verbal atmosphere is entirely wholesome.

**Salām as the Greeting of the Prophets and Angels**

> *"And their greeting therein will be: 'Peace.'"* (Qur'an 10:10; cf. 14:23; 16:32)

> *"Peace be upon you for what you patiently endured."* (Qur'an 13:24)

The angels greet the people of Paradise with *salām*; the people of Paradise greet each other with *salām*; God Himself addresses them with peace in some reports. The word is the currency of the realm.

**Rehearsing Salām Now**

Every Muslim already practices this verse multiple times a day in the ritual prayer's closing and in the social greeting *al-salāmu ʿalaykum*. The practice is so familiar that its eschatological depth is often forgotten. To say *salām* to a fellow believer is to wish upon him, in advance, the soundscape of verse 26. To mean it — to actually will peace, safety, and soundness upon the other — is to align the heart with the speech of the Garden. In a world of aggressive communication, the quiet radicalism of a greeting that wishes peace should not be underestimated.

**Salām as Name and Greeting**

*Al-Salām* is one of God's Names. The greeting of peace is a participation in the atmosphere of that Name. When the people of the Garden say *salāman salāmā*, they are living inside a Name. Verse 26 is dhikr as environment — not a moment of remembrance but a climate of it.

**From Worldly Greeting to Eschatological Climate**

The daily *al-salāmu ʿalaykum* is so familiar it can become automatic. Verse 26 invites the recovery of its depth. Each greeting is a wish that the other be admitted to the soundscape of Paradise. Mean it, and the familiar phrase becomes a continuous prayer for other people's final welcome.

**Peace as the Only Remainder**

After the negations of laghw and taʾthīm, only salām remains. The exception is total: the discourse of the Garden has been refined to a single sufficient word-family. Peace, safety, soundness, the Name of God as al-Salām — all are present when "Peace! Peace!" is uttered. Verse 26 is minimalism as perfection, not as poverty. When everything harmful is removed from speech, what remains is enough for eternity.

**Mean the Greeting**

Recover the depth of al-salāmu ʿalaykum. Each greeting wishes the other the soundscape of verse 26. Mean it. In aggressive communication cultures, the quiet radicalism of wishing peace should not be underestimated. Salām as Name of God, as greeting, as climate of the Garden — practice all three registers in the one familiar phrase.

**Among the Foremost's Portrait**

This verse belongs to the sūrah's portrait of the sābiqūn al-muqarrabūn — those who outstripped and were brought nigh. Al-Rāzī discusses whether they pass the Reckoning lightly or not at all; Ibn Kathīr emphasizes the prophets among them; the spiritual tradition emphasizes the elite of nearness in every generation. Cross-reference with 83:18–28 (ʿIlliyyūn and the sealed wine of the near) and with the ḥadīth qudsī of drawing near through nawāfil (al-Bukhārī 6502) deepens the portrait. Verse 26 is one panel. Read it as part of the whole gallery of nearness, couches, drink without harm, fruits of choice, companionship like concealed pearls, and speech that is only peace. The foremost are defined by Whom they are near; everything else is hospitality.
