## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:27

> **And the companions of the right; what of the companions of the right?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Second Amazement, a Broader Company**

*Wa-aṣḥābu al-yamīni mā aṣḥābu al-yamīn* — "And the companions of the right; what of the companions of the right?" The sūrah now returns to the second of the three groups, having completed the portrait of the foremost. The grammatical construction is the same amazement-formula used in verse 8, and the commentators read it as expressing wonder at their state (Ibn ʿAjībah) or as an expression of God's grace: "How fortunate are the companions of the right!" (Ibn Kathīr).

**Why Repeat the Formula?**

The repetition is not redundancy. The foremost have been described; now the camera pans to a different company with a different set of blessings. The amazement is renewed because the mercy is renewed in another register. If the foremost are the elite of nearness, the companions of the right are the broad population of salvation. Their wonder is the wonder of ordinary believers who, despite faults, are received with gardens, shade, fruit, and companionship.

**Yamīn and Maymanah**

Here the word is *al-yamīn* (the right) rather than *al-maymanah* (the right/blessed side) used in verse 8. The two are complementary designations of the same company. *Yamīn* emphasizes the right hand that receives the record; *maymanah* emphasizes the blessing that defines the side. Together they name a people marked by both the legal fact of a favorable verdict and the experiential fact of beatitude.

**Pastoral Consolation**

This verse is a door of hope. Many sincere Muslims read the descriptions of the foremost and feel disqualified — too sinful, too inconsistent, too late. Verse 27 exists to say: there is another company, still wondrous, still received, still the object of divine amazement-at-mercy. The Prophet ﷺ taught that his community would be the majority of the people of Paradise. The companions of the right are that majority's Qur'anic name. Aim for the foremost; and if you fear you have not reached them, do not stop working, and do not despair of the right hand.

**The Camera Pan of Revelation**

Literary structure matters in the Qur'an. After the intense close-up on the foremost (vv. 10–26), verse 27 pans to a wider company. The amazement-formula is repeated so that the reader feels a second wave of wonder — not a lesser copy of the first, but a distinct mercy with its own landscape. The companions of the right will receive thornless lote trees, clustered plantains, extended shade, gushing water, abundant fruit, raised beds, and loving peers. Their portion is not a consolation prize. It is Paradise.

**Majority Mercy**

Ibn Kathīr's observation that the companions of the right comprise the majority of the Garden's residents should be held firmly by every teacher and preacher. Islamic eschatology has an elite (the foremost) and it has a majority (the right hand). Erasing the elite flattens aspiration. Erasing the majority produces despair. Verse 27 keeps the majority visible and wondrous.

**Second Wave of Wonder**

The amazement-formula's return is a literary mercy. The reader who felt disqualified by the foremost's portrait is given a second wonder that includes him, God willing. The companions of the right are not a footnote to the foremost; they are a full company with a full landscape about to be described. Verse 27 is the camera's pan from peak to plateau — and the plateau is still Paradise.

**Hope's Second Door**

If the foremost's portrait disqualified you in your own eyes, walk through verse 27's door. The right hand is wondrous, broad, and real. Aim for the foremost; do not despair of the right. The Prophet's community is the majority of Paradise's people. Verse 27 names that majority's wonder. Keep working. Keep hoping. Keep the two wings flying.

**Among the Right Hand's Landscape**

This verse belongs to the landscape of aṣḥāb al-yamīn — thornless lote, clustered plantains, extended shade, gushing water, abundant fruit neither cut off nor forbidden, raised beds, new creation, amorous peers, many from old and later times. Ibn Kathīr notes they are the majority of the Garden. Al-Qurṭubī and others carefully distinguish their portion from the foremost's without demoting it from Paradise. Verse 27 is a panel in a landscape of broad mercy. The pastoral implication is permanent: aim for the foremost; do not despair of the right; the throng of later times still has seats. Cross-reference 69:19–24 and 84:7–9 for the record in the right hand that defines this company legally.

---
## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:28

> **Among thornless lote trees,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Shade Without the Wound**

*Fī sidrin makhḍūd* — "Among thornless lote trees." The *sidr* is the lote tree, a species familiar to the Arabian audience — known for dense shade and, in this world, for thorns. The adjective *makhḍūd* means stripped of thorns, or, in another reading, laden with fruit so heavily that the branches bend. Ibn Kathīr transmits the view that whereas the lote trees of this world have thorns and few fruits, those of Paradise have no thorns and many fruits.

**Why the Lote Tree?**

Arabian lote trees are believed to be mentioned as trees of Heaven because of their large leaves under which one can find shade (al-Iṣfahānī and others). The image is thus primarily an image of shade, shelter, and rest — the contentment of being protected from glare and heat. In the desert, shade is not a decoration; it is a mercy that can mean the difference between life and death. Paradise takes a desert mercy and perfects it: shade without thorns, shelter without injury.

**The Lote Tree of the Boundary**

The Qur'an elsewhere mentions the Lote Tree of the Furthest Boundary (*sidrat al-muntahā*, 53:14–16), the celestial tree that marks the limit of the created order's ascent, which the Prophet ﷺ saw during the Miʿrāj. The *sidr* of verse 28 is not necessarily identical with that transcendent tree, but the shared name creates a resonance: the lote tree is already, in the Qur'anic lexicon, a marker of sacred nearness and eschatological landscape.

**Makhḍūd as Fruit-Laden**

If *makhḍūd* is read as "fruit-laden" rather than (or in addition to) "thornless," the image gains a second dimension: abundance so great that the tree almost touches the ground with its bounty. The companions of the right do not climb and struggle for their fruit; the fruit descends toward them. This matches the "neither out of reach nor forbidden" of verse 33.

**Spiritual Analogy**

Earthly goods often come with thorns: wealth with anxiety, relationship with friction, status with envy. The thornless lote tree is an emblem of good without the usual accompanying harm. The believer who has learned that every worldly rose has its thorn is being told that a garden exists where that rule is suspended. Patience with the thorns now is part of the price of the thornless then.

**Shade as a Desert Theology**

For the first audience, shade was not a poetic ornament. It was survival. A tree that offered shade without thorns was an almost paradoxical mercy — the world's lote trees teach the hand to be careful even as they shelter the head. Verse 28 removes the paradox: shelter without injury. In the spiritual life, this is the quality of God's own shade — the shade of His mercy, which covers without wounding, unlike the "shade" of patrons, ideologies, and appetites that always extract a price in thorns.

**Makhḍūd in the Lexicons**

The lexical range of *makhḍūd* — thornless, pruned of spines, or so fruit-laden that branches bend — allows the verse to carry both comfort and abundance. Classical tafsīr often holds both: the tree does not harm, and the tree gives much. The companions of the right need both messages. Their life in the world may have been full of small thorns; their provision may have been thin. The lote tree of Paradise answers both pains in a single image.

**Lote Tree Across the Qur'an**

The lote appears at the boundary of the Miʿrāj (*sidrat al-muntahā*, 53:14–16) and in the landscape of the right hand (56:28). The shared name creates resonance without forcing identity. The lote is already, in the Qur'anic lexicon, a tree of sacred nearness and eschatological geography. Thornless, fruit-bearing, shade-giving — verse 28 takes a desert tree and perfects it into a mercy without remainder.

**Seeking Shade Without Thorns**

Examine your shelters. Do they cool without wounding? Wealth, patrons, relationships, ideologies — many shade with thorns. Verse 28's thornless lote is emblem of good without the usual accompanying harm. Patience with earthly thorns is part of the price of the thornless then. Prefer already, where choice exists, shelters that do not extract blood for cover.

**Among the Right Hand's Landscape**

This verse belongs to the landscape of aṣḥāb al-yamīn — thornless lote, clustered plantains, extended shade, gushing water, abundant fruit neither cut off nor forbidden, raised beds, new creation, amorous peers, many from old and later times. Ibn Kathīr notes they are the majority of the Garden. Al-Qurṭubī and others carefully distinguish their portion from the foremost's without demoting it from Paradise. Verse 28 is a panel in a landscape of broad mercy. The pastoral implication is permanent: aim for the foremost; do not despair of the right; the throng of later times still has seats. Cross-reference 69:19–24 and 84:7–9 for the record in the right hand that defines this company legally.

## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:29

> **clustered plantains,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Abundance Stacked from Top to Bottom**

*Wa-ṭalḥin manḍūd* — "and clustered plantains." The word *ṭalḥ* is interpreted by most classical commentators, including the Jalālayn, as the plantain or banana tree, called *ṭalḥ* in the Yemeni dialect. Others, including a line preserved by Ibn Kathīr, held that what is meant is a large thorny shrub familiar in the Ḥijāz. The weight of the tradition favors a tall, fruit-bearing tree whose produce is a bounty of the Garden.

**Manḍūd: Stacked, Layered, Dense**

*Manḍūd* means stacked one upon another, clustered, layered so densely that the fruit covers the tree from top to bottom (Jalālayn) and the trunk itself is no longer visible (Ibn ʿAjībah). The image is one of overwhelming abundance. In this world, fruit is scattered, seasonal, and finite. In that world, it is stacked in continuous density. The eye finds no bare branch.

**The Aesthetics of Overflow**

Paradise in the Qur'an is consistently depicted with the aesthetics of overflow rather than of minimalist sufficiency. Rivers, not cups; gardens, not pots; clustered fruit, not single specimens. This is not vulgar excess; it is the visual language of a generosity that has no need to ration. God is *al-Wāsiʿ*, the Vast, and the landscape of His reward reflects His vastness.

**Pairing with the Lote Tree**

The thornless lote and the clustered plantain together give the companions of the right a landscape of shade and fruit, protection and provision. One tree emphasizes shelter; the other emphasizes food. Together they say: you will lack neither cover nor sustenance. The pair is also a quiet reply to the conditions of Arabian life, where both shade and fresh produce could be scarce. Revelation speaks to the hungers its first hearers knew, and through them to the hungers of every later reader.

**Abundance Without Corruption**

In this world, extreme abundance often corrupts — waste, indifference, the numbness of the overfed. The clustered plantains of Paradise are abundance without that corruption. The companions of the right can receive overflow because they have been remade as people who can bear overflow without being spoiled by it. This is part of the meaning of the "new creation" themes that run through the sūrah. The capacity to enjoy unlimited good without becoming bad is itself a gift of the Garden.

**Yemen and the Ḥijāz**

The lexical debate over *ṭalḥ* — plantain of Yemen versus the thorny shrub of the Ḥijāz — illustrates how the Qur'an speaks through the vocabulary of its first audience while remaining open to the experience of every later audience. Whether the tree is read as plantain or as another tall fruit-bearer, the image of dense, stacked produce communicates across climates and centuries. Revelation is locally precise and universally legible.

**Manḍūd as Visual Theology**

Fruit stacked so densely that the trunk disappears is visual theology of divine generosity. The eye finds no bare branch. Scarcity-anxiety, which drives so much earthly sin, has no visual support in this landscape. Verse 29 is overflowing as a teaching method. God is al-Wāsiʿ, the Vast; the clustered plantains are one of His vastnesses made visible for the companions of the right.

**Trusting Overflow**

Practice generosity that assumes God's vastness. Scarcity-anxiety drives sin; verse 29's stacked fruit feeds trust in abundance. The eye that finds no bare branch in the Garden is prepared by the hand that gives when the earthly branch looks thin. God is al-Wāsiʿ. Act accordingly in the trial of apparent finitude.

**Among the Right Hand's Landscape**

This verse belongs to the landscape of aṣḥāb al-yamīn — thornless lote, clustered plantains, extended shade, gushing water, abundant fruit neither cut off nor forbidden, raised beds, new creation, amorous peers, many from old and later times. Ibn Kathīr notes they are the majority of the Garden. Al-Qurṭubī and others carefully distinguish their portion from the foremost's without demoting it from Paradise. Verse 29 is a panel in a landscape of broad mercy. The pastoral implication is permanent: aim for the foremost; do not despair of the right; the throng of later times still has seats. Cross-reference 69:19–24 and 84:7–9 for the record in the right hand that defines this company legally.

## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:30

> **and extended shade,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Shade That a Rider Cannot Outrun**

*Wa-ẓillin mamdūd* — "and extended shade." The adjective *mamdūd* means extended, stretched, prolonged — shade that does not shrink at noon and does not fail at the edge of a small tree's reach. It is shade as a continuous environment rather than as a temporary patch.

**The Hadith of the Hundred-Year Shade**

According to a ḥadīth cited by al-Qurṭubī, al-Rāzī, and al-Ṭabarī, the Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"In the Garden there is a tree in whose shade a rider would travel for a hundred years without the shade being broken. That is the extended shade."*

The hyperbole is deliberate and Prophetic: it stretches the imagination until ordinary categories of shade collapse. The point is not the arithmetic of years; it is the qualitative difference between earthly shade and the *ẓill mamdūd*.

**Ẓill as Shelter, Protection, and Happiness**

*Ẓill* denotes shade, covering, shelter, and can be used figuratively to imply protection (al-Iṣfahānī). It is also taken by some to indicate a state of ease, pleasure, and plenty — hence happiness. Verse 30 and other Qur'anic references to shade can thus be read as allusions to the eternal security and felicity of Paradise, not merely to temperature control. To be "in the shade" of God is, in the wider religious lexicon of Islam, to be under His protection. The extended shade is extended protection, extended ease, extended joy.

**Contrast Ahead: The Shadow of Black Smoke**

The sūrah will later describe the companions of the left as suffering "the shadow of black smoke, neither cool nor refreshing" (56:43–44). The two images are designed to be read against each other. Shade is not automatically a mercy; it depends on what casts it. The shade of the Garden cools and protects. The "shade" of Hell obscures and chokes. The same English word, and even related Arabic imagery, divides into two destinies.

**Practical Shade**

In this life, the believer seeks shade in several senses: the shade of the mosque, the shade of just governance, the shade of good company, the shade of private devotion. The Prophet ﷺ described seven types of people who will be shaded by God's shade on the Day when there is no shade but His (al-Bukhārī 660; Muslim 1031) — including the just ruler, the youth raised in worship, the one whose heart is attached to the mosques, and those who give charity in secret. Extended shade then is prepared by seeking the right shade now.

**The Hundred-Year Shade**

The ḥadīth of the tree whose shade a rider travels for a hundred years without exhausting it (al-Bukhārī and Muslim in related forms; cited widely in tafsīr) is designed to break ordinary measurement. Extended shade is not slightly longer shade; it is a different order of shelter. Verse 30 points to that order with a single adjective: *mamdūd*.

**Seven Under the Shade**

The famous ḥadīth of the seven who are shaded by God on the Day when there is no shade but His (al-Bukhārī 660; Muslim 1031) is the practical companion of verse 30. Just ruler; youth raised in worship; heart attached to mosques; those who love each other for God; those who resist temptation from status and desire; those who give secretly; those who remember God and weep. Extended shade then is prepared by seeking God's shade now.

**Ẓill as Protection and Felicity**

Shade in Arabic idiom extends to protection and to a state of ease. The extended shade of verse 30 is therefore extended safety and extended joy, not merely temperature control. Reading it with the ḥadīth of the hundred-year shade and the ḥadīth of the seven shaded on the Day produces a full doctrine: God's shade is the only shade that does not fail, and it is prepared by specific virtues now.

**Seeking the Seven Shades**

Work toward the qualities of the seven shaded on the Day when there is no shade but His: justice in authority, youth in worship, heart in mosques, love for God, resistance to status-temptation, secret charity, tearful remembrance. Verse 30's extended shade is prepared by seeking God's shade now. Ordinary shade fails at noon and at death. His does not.

**Among the Right Hand's Landscape**

This verse belongs to the landscape of aṣḥāb al-yamīn — thornless lote, clustered plantains, extended shade, gushing water, abundant fruit neither cut off nor forbidden, raised beds, new creation, amorous peers, many from old and later times. Ibn Kathīr notes they are the majority of the Garden. Al-Qurṭubī and others carefully distinguish their portion from the foremost's without demoting it from Paradise. Verse 30 is a panel in a landscape of broad mercy. The pastoral implication is permanent: aim for the foremost; do not despair of the right; the throng of later times still has seats. Cross-reference 69:19–24 and 84:7–9 for the record in the right hand that defines this company legally.

## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:31

> **gushing water,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Water That Comes Without Effort**

*Wa-māʾin maskūb* — "and gushing water," or "water poured out." The word *maskūb* from *sakaba* means poured forth, flowing freely, not confined. Ibn Kathīr, al-Rāzī, al-Ṭabarī, and al-Zamakhsharī all note that it indicates water not restricted to channels — water that flows to those in Paradise wherever and however they wish, without effort on their part (Ibn ʿAjībah, al-Zamakhsharī).

**The Tripartite Hydrology of the Sūrah**

Al-Wāqiʿah constructs a deliberate contrast in drinks. The foremost receive wine from a flowing spring without headache or stupefaction (vv. 18–19). The companions of the right receive pure gushing water (v. 31). The companions of the left receive boiling liquid (vv. 42, 54). Each group's drink matches their state: ecstatic nearness, wholesome refreshment, and scalding recompense. Water, the most basic of the three, is not a lesser gift; it is the gift of pure life. In the desert, water is the first mercy.

**Water as Qur'anic Sign**

> *"And We made every living thing from water."* (Qur'an 21:30)

> *"Have you considered the water that you drink? Is it you who sent it down from the clouds, or is it We Who send down?"* (Qur'an 56:68–69 — later in this very sūrah)

The gushing water of Paradise is continuous with the water-theology of the whole Qur'an: water is life, gift, proof of the Creator, and, here, eternal refreshment. The companions of the right, who thanked God for water in the world or at least did not claim it as their own manufacture, drink it without measure in the next.

**Effortlessness as Eschatological Mark**

A recurring feature of Paradise's description is the removal of toil. Fruit within reach, water that comes of itself, shade already extended. In this world, even water often costs labor — wells dug, pipes laid, bills paid. Verse 31 is the end of that labor. The mercy that had to be carried in skins and pulled from depths now pours itself out at the wish of the blessed.

**Maskūb: Poured Out**

The passive participle *maskūb* suggests water that has been poured — freely, generously, without rationing. In human hospitality, pouring is the host's act. In verse 31, the host is God, and the pouring does not diminish the source. The companions of the right, who may have rationed water in desert journeys or paid dearly for every skin of it, enter a hydrology of pure gift.

**Between Wine and Boiling Liquid**

Placement is theology. The foremost drink wine without harm. The left drink boiling liquid with harm. The right drink water — pure, gushing, effortless. Water is the middle mercy, the mercy of wholesome life rather than ecstatic nearness or scalding justice. Ordinary believers should not despise ordinary mercies. The glass of cool water in this world is already kin to verse 31; thanking for it is already alignment with the companions of the right.

**Water Between Two Drinks**

Placement between the foremost's wine and the left's ḥamīm makes the right hand's water a middle mercy — pure life rather than ecstasy or scalding. Ordinary believers should not despise ordinary mercies. The glass of cool water in this world, taken with basmalah and ḥamd, is already correspondence with verse 31. The gushing, effortless water of the Garden is that correspondence completed.

**Thanking for Water Always**

Let every drink become a small correspondence with verse 31. Basmalah before, ḥamd after. Effortless gushing water in the Garden crowns gratitude for effortful water in the trial. Between the foremost's wine and the left's ḥamīm, the right hand's water is wholesome life. Do not despise ordinary mercies. They are kin to Paradise.

**Among the Right Hand's Landscape**

This verse belongs to the landscape of aṣḥāb al-yamīn — thornless lote, clustered plantains, extended shade, gushing water, abundant fruit neither cut off nor forbidden, raised beds, new creation, amorous peers, many from old and later times. Ibn Kathīr notes they are the majority of the Garden. Al-Qurṭubī and others carefully distinguish their portion from the foremost's without demoting it from Paradise. Verse 31 is a panel in a landscape of broad mercy. The pastoral implication is permanent: aim for the foremost; do not despair of the right; the throng of later times still has seats. Cross-reference 69:19–24 and 84:7–9 for the record in the right hand that defines this company legally.

## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:32

> **and abundant fruit,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Fruit as the Signature of the Garden**

*Wa-fākihatin kathīrah* — "and abundant fruit." The adjective *kathīrah* means much, plentiful, abundant. Where the foremost had fruits "as they choose" (v. 20), the companions of the right have fruit in sheer quantity. The two descriptions are complementary rather than competitive: choice and abundance are both mercies, distributed across the two companies of the saved.

**Fruit as a Qur'anic Constant**

From the earliest Makkan revelations to the latest, fruit is one of the Qur'an's favorite signs of both worldly providence and eschatological reward. The gardens of this world produce fruit as a sign (6:99; 13:4; 16:11); the gardens of the next produce it as a home. The continuity teaches that the God of harvest is the God of Paradise, and that gratitude for an apple now is practice for the abundant fruit then.

**Kathīrah: Quantity Without Monotony**

Abundance in Paradise is never depicted as monotonous bulk. The wider Qur'anic picture includes variety of kinds, perpetual season, and responsiveness to desire. *Kathīrah* guarantees that scarcity — one of the defining anxieties of earthly life — is abolished. No rationing. No queue. No price spike. The fruit is simply there, and there is much of it.

**Gratitude Training**

Every piece of fruit eaten in this world with the basmalah and with thanks is a small correspondence with verse 32. The Prophet ﷺ taught that God is pleased with the servant who praises Him after eating and after drinking. The companions of the right are, among other things, people who learned to see fruit as gift rather than as entitlement. Abundance in the next world crowns gratitude in this one.

**Fruit and the Memory of Earth**

Why does the Qur'an insist so often on fruit in Paradise? Because fruit is already, in this world, one of the most complete sensory mercies — color, scent, taste, texture, juice — and because it arrives from trees that human beings did not design. Every orange is a small argument for a generous Creator. Verse 32 takes that argument to its conclusion: the Creator who gives fruit as sign will give fruit as home. Abundant fruit is abundant signage fulfilled.

**Kathīrah and the Fear of Scarcity**

Much human sin is driven by the fear of scarcity — hoarding, cheating, refusing to give, viewing other people as rivals for a finite pie. The promise of *fākihah kathīrah* addresses that fear at its root. There is a realm where the pie is not finite. The person who believes this is freer to give in the realm where the pie still appears finite. Charity in this world is easier for those who trust the abundance of the next.

**Abundance and the Fear That Drives Sin**

Fear of scarcity drives hoarding, fraud, and refusal to give. Verse 32's abundant fruit addresses that fear eschatologically. There is a realm where abundance is the default. Trusting that realm frees the hand in this one. The charitable person is often the person who most believes in the abundance of God — here and hereafter. Verse 32 feeds that belief with an image.

**Fruit as Sign and Home**

Thank for fruit in this world as sign; hope for fruit in the next as home. The continuity teaches that the God of harvest is the God of Paradise. Verse 32's abundance addresses scarcity-fear. Belief in that abundance frees charity now. The charitable are often those who most believe God has more.

**Among the Right Hand's Landscape**

This verse belongs to the landscape of aṣḥāb al-yamīn — thornless lote, clustered plantains, extended shade, gushing water, abundant fruit neither cut off nor forbidden, raised beds, new creation, amorous peers, many from old and later times. Ibn Kathīr notes they are the majority of the Garden. Al-Qurṭubī and others carefully distinguish their portion from the foremost's without demoting it from Paradise. Verse 32 is a panel in a landscape of broad mercy. The pastoral implication is permanent: aim for the foremost; do not despair of the right; the throng of later times still has seats. Cross-reference 69:19–24 and 84:7–9 for the record in the right hand that defines this company legally.

## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:33

> **neither out of reach, nor forbidden,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Access Without Barrier**

*Lā maqṭūʿatin wa-lā mamnūʿah* — "neither out of reach, nor forbidden," or "not cut off, nor withheld." Two negations define the fruit of the companions of the right. It is not *maqṭūʿah* — cut off, interrupted, seasonal, exhausted. It is not *mamnūʿah* — forbidden, withheld, prohibited by rule or by force.

**Out of Reach: The End of Frustration**

In this world, desired goods are frequently visible but inaccessible — behind prices, behind distances, behind social barriers, behind the limits of the season. The "low-hanging clusters" of 69:23 and the "fruits near at hand" of 55:54 belong to the same family of images as verse 33. Paradise abolishes the particular pain of seeing without having. What is desired is reachable.

**Not Cut Off: The End of Seasonality**

The alternative translation "not cut off" points to the absence of seasons in which fruit fails (Ibn Kathīr, al-Rāzī, al-Zamakhsharī). Earthly agriculture is a cycle of presence and absence. The fruit of the Garden is a continuum. This is part of the larger eschatological pattern: time in Paradise does not erode goods.

**Not Forbidden: The End of the Prohibition Trial**

In this world, some good things are forbidden for the sake of the trial — wine, illicit intimacy, others' property. The forbidden is a test of obedience. In Paradise, the structure of trial is over. The *mamnūʿah* is lifted. What remains is permission without remainder. The companions of the right, who respected the boundaries of the forbidden in the world, inherit a realm where the boundaries have done their work and been retired.

**The Psychology of Withholding**

Much human suffering comes from the sense that good things are being withheld — by fate, by other people, by an unjust system, or, in the angry imagination, by God Himself. Verse 33 is a direct address to that suffering: there is a place where the withholding ends. Patience with the *mamnūʿah* of this world is the bridge to the un-forbidden of the next.

**Two Negations, One Freedom**

*Lā maqṭūʿah* and *lā mamnūʿah* together abolish the two great frustrations of earthly desire: interruption and prohibition. Seasons interrupt. Laws (and before them, divine commands) prohibit. Both interruption and prohibition are mercies in the trial-world — seasons teach dependence, prohibitions teach obedience. In the Garden, their work is done. The fruit is continuous and permitted. Freedom in Paradise is not the freedom to do evil; it is the freedom of good without the structures of trial.

**The Low-Hanging Clusters**

Reading verse 33 with 69:23 and 76:14 and 55:54 produces a gallery of accessibility: clusters lowered, fruits near, neither cut off nor forbidden. The God of the Qur'an does not delight in teasing the saved with unreachable goods. The trial of unreachability belongs to this world. The next world is designed for the opposite pedagogy: whatever you rightly desire, you may have.

**Access as Eschatological Mark**

Neither out of reach nor forbidden: the two great frustrations of earthly desire are abolished. Seasons and prohibitions were mercies of the trial — teaching dependence and obedience. In the Garden their pedagogical work is done. Verse 33 is freedom properly defined: not freedom to harm, but freedom of good without the scaffolding of trial. The low-hanging clusters of related verses complete the gallery of access.

**Patience with Interruption and Prohibition**

Seasons interrupt; laws prohibit. Both are mercies of the trial. Verse 33 promises a realm where the pedagogical work of interruption and prohibition is complete. Patience with the maqṭūʿ and mamnūʿ of this world is the bridge to the continuous and permitted of the next. Do not resent the scaffolding while the building is unfinished.

**Among the Right Hand's Landscape**

This verse belongs to the landscape of aṣḥāb al-yamīn — thornless lote, clustered plantains, extended shade, gushing water, abundant fruit neither cut off nor forbidden, raised beds, new creation, amorous peers, many from old and later times. Ibn Kathīr notes they are the majority of the Garden. Al-Qurṭubī and others carefully distinguish their portion from the foremost's without demoting it from Paradise. Verse 33 is a panel in a landscape of broad mercy. The pastoral implication is permanent: aim for the foremost; do not despair of the right; the throng of later times still has seats. Cross-reference 69:19–24 and 84:7–9 for the record in the right hand that defines this company legally.

## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:34

> **and [upon] raised beds.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Couches Raised, and a Possible Allusion to Spouses**

*Wa-furushin marfūʿah* — "and [upon] raised beds," or "raised couches/carpets." The word *furush* (singular *firāsh*) means beds, carpets, mattresses — furniture of rest. *Marfūʿah* means raised, elevated, lofty. The companions of the right, like the foremost on their embroidered couches, are given elevated places of repose.

**The Dual Sense of Furush**

In classical Arabic, *firāsh* can also serve as an allusion to a spouse (al-Iṣfahānī and the lexical tradition). Several commentators therefore relate this verse to 36:55–56:

> *"Indeed, the companions of Paradise, that Day, will be amused in [joyful] occupation — they and their spouses — in shade, reclining on couches."*

This interpretation is supported by the use of the feminine plural pronoun *hunna* in the following verse ("We brought them into being"), so that verse 34 could be read as alluding to "spouses made high" or raised in status — connecting to the wide-eyed maidens of earlier verses and to 44:54; 52:20; 56:22. Both the furniture-reading and the spouse-reading are attested; both nourish; neither needs to cancel the other in a text this dense.

**Elevation as Honor**

Whether beds or spouses, *marfūʿah* signals elevation. The companions of the right are lifted — in their seating, in their company, in their rank. The lowliness they may have endured in the world, the humble beds they may have slept on, the overlooked status they may have borne, is answered by a raising. God is *al-Rāfiʿ*, the One who raises, and this verse is one of His raisings.

**Domestic Eschatology**

If the spousal reading is taken seriously, the verse contributes to what might be called the Qur'an's domestic eschatology: the home, the marriage, the shared rest — perfected. Salvation is not only a cosmic drama; it is also the healing and completion of the household.

**Raised Above the Dust**

The companions of the left become, in a sense, people of dust and smoke. The companions of the right are people of raised beds. Elevation in seating is elevation in dignity. The Qur'an elsewhere speaks of God raising some in ranks above others (e.g., 58:11 for those who believe and are given knowledge). Verse 34 is a spatial rendering of that raising. The lowly bed of the patient poor, the overlooked mat of the night-prayer devotee, is answered by *furush marfūʿah*.

**Household Eschatology Continued**

If the spousal reading is taken, the verse also teaches that the repair of intimacy is part of salvation's work. Many earthly marriages carry wounds that repentance and good character mitigate but do not entirely erase. The raised companionship of the Garden is the completion of that repair — a doctrine of hope for every believer who has loved imperfectly and been loved imperfectly, and who asks God to complete in the next world what was sincerely attempted in this one.

**Elevation After Lowliness**

Raised beds answer lowly beds. The overlooked mat of the night-prayer devotee, the thin mattress of the patient poor, the humble household of the righteous — these are answered by furush marfūʿah. Whether read as furniture or as elevated companionship, the adjective marfūʿah is the key: God raises. Verse 34 is one of His raisings, assigned to the right hand.

**Hope for Raised Things**

Whatever is lowly in your righteous life — humble bed, overlooked status, wounded intimacy patiently borne — hold verse 34 as promise of raising. God is al-Rāfiʿ. Furniture or companionship, marfūʿah is the adjective of the right hand's rest. Domestic eschatology is real: the household's repair is part of salvation's work.

**Among the Right Hand's Landscape**

This verse belongs to the landscape of aṣḥāb al-yamīn — thornless lote, clustered plantains, extended shade, gushing water, abundant fruit neither cut off nor forbidden, raised beds, new creation, amorous peers, many from old and later times. Ibn Kathīr notes they are the majority of the Garden. Al-Qurṭubī and others carefully distinguish their portion from the foremost's without demoting it from Paradise. Verse 34 is a panel in a landscape of broad mercy. The pastoral implication is permanent: aim for the foremost; do not despair of the right; the throng of later times still has seats. ## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:35

> **Truly We brought them into being as a [new] creation,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Creation Without the Wear of Time**

*Innā anshaʾnāhunna inshāʾā* — "Truly We brought them into being as a [new] creation," or "in an immediate manner." The verb *anshaʾa* means to originate, to bring into being, to raise up. The cognate accusative *inshāʾā* intensifies: a thorough, distinctive act of creation. The pronoun *hunna* is feminine plural, picking up the allusion of the previous verse.

**Always Fresh**

The verse implies that the maidens of Heaven — or the spouses raised in the Garden — are created in a state that does not age, tire, or diminish. They are always fresh, always new. "As a new creation" can also be translated "in an immediate manner," suggesting creation without the ordinary processes of gestation and growth — a direct divine origination befitting the realm where God's creative command *kun* ("Be") operates without the usual secondary causes.

**The Theology of Re-Creation**

The same God who creates the companions of the Garden anew is the God who re-creates the dead for resurrection. Verse 35 is a small instance of a large power. If God can originate the beings of Paradise in a mode unknown to earthly biology, He can certainly reassemble the scattered dust of human graves. The sūrah will press this logic explicitly in verses 57–62.

**Comfort for the Aged and the Worn**

A ḥadīth related to the following verses (35–37) states that those who in this world were gray, bleary-eyed, and worn will be remade as youthful peers. Verse 35's "new creation" is the theological ground of that promise. Aging, which the modern world pathologizes and the consumer economy exploits, is in the Islamic vision a temporary condition of the trial-world. The new creation is coming. The gray hair of the believer is not a defeat; it is a passport awaiting renewal.

**Inshāʾ and the Creative Command**

The cognate accusative *inshāʾā* emphasizes that this creation is a distinct, willed, complete act. God is not bound to the reproductive patterns of the trial-world. The command *kun fa-yakūn* ("Be, and it is") operates in the Garden with a freedom that earthly biology only faintly echoes. Verse 35 is therefore also a verse about divine power: the One who creates the companions of the Garden anew is the One who will reassemble every human being from the grave.

**Hope for Bodies That Have Suffered**

Bodies in this world suffer aging, illness, disfigurement, and exhaustion. The promise of a new creation speaks to every believer whose body has been a site of trial. The gray hair, the bent back, the dimmed eye — these are temporary. The God who made the first body makes a further body, and the further body is described under the sign of newness. Verse 35 is medicine for the despair that can accompany physical decline.

**New Creation and the Hope of Bodies**

Bodies age, sicken, and tire. Verse 35's new creation speaks to every believer whose body has been a site of trial. The God who originated the first body originates a further one under the sign of newness. The ḥadīth of gray hair turned to youthful peerage is the narrative form of this grammar. Decline is temporary. Newness is coming.

**Bodies Toward Newness**

Speak to your aging or suffering body with verse 35's hope. New creation is coming. Gray hair is a passport awaiting renewal, not a defeat. The God who makes the companions of the Garden anew reassembles every believer from the grave. Decline is temporary. Serve with the body you have; wait for the body you are promised.

**Among the Right Hand's Landscape**

This verse belongs to the landscape of aṣḥāb al-yamīn — thornless lote, clustered plantains, extended shade, gushing water, abundant fruit neither cut off nor forbidden, raised beds, new creation, amorous peers, many from old and later times. Ibn Kathīr notes they are the majority of the Garden. Al-Qurṭubī and others carefully distinguish their portion from the foremost's without demoting it from Paradise. Verse 35 is a panel in a landscape of broad mercy. The pastoral implication is permanent: aim for the foremost; do not despair of the right; the throng of later times still has seats. Cross-reference 69:19–24 and 84:7–9 for the record in the right hand that defines this company legally.

## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:36

> **then made for them virgins,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Purity as a Created Gift**

*Fa-jaʿalnāhunna abkārā* — "then made for them virgins." The *fa-* indicates sequence or consequence: having brought them into being as a new creation, God then made them *abkār* — virgins, untouched, pure. The word carries the note of a purity that has never been altered.

**Continuity with the Concealed Pearl**

This verse continues the theme of 56:23, the likeness of concealed pearls, and of 55:56, 74. The companions of Paradise are characterized by a purity that matches the purity of the realm. The foremost and the companions of the right, who guarded chastity in a world where it was costly, receive a companionship defined by the same value they protected.

**Not Only a Statement About the Maidens**

"Made for them virgins" is also an indirect statement about the recipients. The "them" are people for whom such a gift is appropriate — people whose own lives, by faith and repentance, have been oriented toward purity. The gift fits the giver's wisdom and the receiver's state. God's gifts in the Hereafter are not random; they are *jazāʾ*, recompense, as verse 24 already established for the foremost and as the whole structure of the sūrah implies for every group.

**The Ethic of Guarding**

In practical terms, the verse reinforces the Qur'anic ethic of *ḥifẓ al-farj* — guarding chastity (23:5; 33:35). This ethic applies to men and women, married and unmarried, and includes the lowering of the gaze, the avoidance of *zinā* and its approaches, and the treatment of intimacy as a covenant rather than a commodity. Verse 36 is one of the eschatological incentives attached to that ethic.

**Abkār and the Value of the Guarded**

In a world that often treats purity as naivety and consumption as sophistication, verse 36 stands as a counter-ethic. The companionship of the Garden is defined by a purity that has been protected. This does not degrade those who have sinned and repented — the Qur'an is full of the promise that repentance renews — but it does affirm that guarding what should be guarded is precious in God's sight. The *abkār* of the Garden match the *ḥifẓ* of the righteous.

**For Men and Women Alike**

The ethic of chastity and the hope of purified companionship apply to believing men and believing women. The Qur'an addresses both (33:35 lists "the men who guard their chastity and the women who do so"). Verse 36 should not be read as a male-only fantasy; it is part of a larger eschatology in which both genders of the saved receive what befits the mercy and justice of God, including the reunion of believing spouses and the joys proper to the Garden.

**Purity as Gift and as Correspondence**

Abkār — virgins, pure, untouched — names a purity that matches the purity of the realm and the purity the recipients sought. Repentance renews those who fell; guarding protects those who stood; both are honored in the wider eschatology. Verse 36 specifically crowns the guarded and the renewed with a companionship defined by purity. The ethic of ḥifẓ al-farj finds one of its eschatological seals here.

**Guarding and Renewing**

Practice ḥifẓ al-farj and never despair of tawbah if you have fallen. Both the guarded and the renewed are honored in the wider eschatology. Verse 36 crowns purity as the quality of the Garden's companionship. The ethic applies to men and women. Intimacy is covenant, not commodity. Live that sentence, and the verse becomes personal hope rather than distant image.

**Among the Right Hand's Landscape**

This verse belongs to the landscape of aṣḥāb al-yamīn — thornless lote, clustered plantains, extended shade, gushing water, abundant fruit neither cut off nor forbidden, raised beds, new creation, amorous peers, many from old and later times. Ibn Kathīr notes they are the majority of the Garden. Al-Qurṭubī and others carefully distinguish their portion from the foremost's without demoting it from Paradise. Verse 36 is a panel in a landscape of broad mercy. The pastoral implication is permanent: aim for the foremost; do not despair of the right; the throng of later times still has seats. Cross-reference 69:19–24 and 84:7–9 for the record in the right hand that defines this company legally.

## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:37

> **amorous peers,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Love and Equality of Age**

*ʿUruban atrābā* — "amorous peers." Two words, each dense. *ʿUrub* (singular *ʿarūb*) means women who are loving, affectionate, amorous toward their spouses — expressing intimacy and delight. *Atrāb* means peers, of the same age, matched. The companionship of the Garden is defined by mutual love and by compatibility.

**The Hadith of Umm Salamah**

According to a ḥadīth, the Prophet ﷺ was asked about those referred to in verses 35–37 by his wife Umm Salamah. He responded:

> *"They are those who in this world were rendered gray, bleary-eyed, and blind. After their old age God will make them [youthful] peers."* (cited by Ālūsī, Ibn Kathīr, al-Qurṭubī, al-Ṭabarī)

The report anchors the verses in a concrete human hope: the restoration of youth, the matching of spouses, the renewal of what age had worn down. Thirty-three years is the age often mentioned in the tradition for the people of Paradise (Ibn ʿAjībah, al-Qurṭubī) — the age of full vigor without the unsteadiness of adolescence or the decline of elderhood.

**Amorous: The Sanctification of Marital Love**

*ʿUrub* is a word of warm intimacy. The Qur'an does not treat marital affection as a reluctant concession to the body; it treats it as a good that Paradise itself includes and perfects. The Prophet ﷺ taught that the best of the believers are the best to their spouses. Verse 37 is the eschatological extension of that teaching: the love that was practiced with kindness on earth is crowned with a love free of the frictions that earth could not entirely remove.

**Peers: Compatibility as Mercy**

*Atrāb* implies both equal age and spiritual compatibility. Much earthly marital suffering comes from mismatch — of age, of temperament, of vision. The peerage of Paradise is the end of mismatch. Each is fitted to each. The God who created temperaments matches them with a wisdom that no earthly matchmaking can equal.

**ʿUrub: The Warmth God Does Not Disdain**

Some religious temperaments are suspicious of warmth, affection, and marital delight, as though seriousness required coldness. The word *ʿurub* refuses that suspicion. God Himself names amorous affection as a feature of the Garden's companionship. The God of the Qur'an is not embarrassed by the love between spouses; He is the One who placed love and mercy between them (30:21) and who crowns it in the Hereafter.

**Atrāb and the End of Mismatch**

Peerage in age and compatibility is a mercy that many earthly lives only partially taste. The *atrāb* of verse 37 are the promise that the God who knows every temperament will match them with a wisdom no algorithm and no well-meaning relative can equal. For those who suffered mismatch with patience, or who never found a spouse, or who were widowed early, the verse is a door of hope: the story of companionship is not over at the grave.

**Affection Sanctified**

ʿUrub refuses the coldness that some religious temperaments mistake for seriousness. God names amorous affection as a feature of the Garden. Atrāb promises peerage and compatibility — the end of mismatch. Together they offer hope to the married who loved imperfectly, the widowed, the unmarried, and all who ask God to complete the story of companionship beyond the grave. Verse 37 is warm and precise at once.

**Warmth and Compatibility as Hope**

Do not mistake coldness for piety. Verse 37's ʿurub sanctifies marital affection. Atrāb promises the end of mismatch. Bring this hope to imperfect marriages, to widowhood, to unmarried longing, to every unfinished story of companionship. God completes beyond the grave what was sincerely attempted before it. Love with kindness now; trust the peerage then.

**Among the Right Hand's Landscape**

This verse belongs to the landscape of aṣḥāb al-yamīn — thornless lote, clustered plantains, extended shade, gushing water, abundant fruit neither cut off nor forbidden, raised beds, new creation, amorous peers, many from old and later times. Ibn Kathīr notes they are the majority of the Garden. Al-Qurṭubī and others carefully distinguish their portion from the foremost's without demoting it from Paradise. Verse 37 is a panel in a landscape of broad mercy. The pastoral implication is permanent: aim for the foremost; do not despair of the right; the throng of later times still has seats. ## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:38

> **for the companions of the right—**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Gift Explicitly Assigned**

*Li-aṣḥābi al-yamīn* — "for the companions of the right." This short verse is a dedicatory label. Everything described in the preceding sequence — the thornless lote trees, the clustered plantains, the extended shade, the gushing water, the abundant fruit, the raised beds, the new creation, the virgins, the amorous peers — is assigned to the companions of the right.

**Clarifying the Distribution**

Commentators have discussed whether the wide-eyed maidens of verse 22 belong to the foremost while the amorous peers of verses 35–37 belong to the companions of the right (a view noted by al-Qurṭubī), or whether both companies share in purified companionship in forms appropriate to each. Verse 38 at minimum makes clear that the block of blessings just described is the portion of the right hand. The sūrah is careful with its attributions. It does not blur the groups. Each has its portrait.

**The Dignity of the Ordinary Saved**

By giving the companions of the right such a rich and extended description — longer, in some respects, than portions of the foremost's portrait — the sūrah confers enormous dignity on ordinary belief. One need not be a prophet or a foremost saint to inherit thornless trees, extended shade, and loving companionship. One needs to be among the people of the right hand: a believer who receives the book in the right hand, whose faith, however tested, held. Verse 38 is a deed of title. The property is theirs.

**Dedication Lines in Revelation**

Verse 38 functions like a title deed's name-line: *for the companions of the right*. The Qur'an is careful with ownership. Blessings are assigned. The reader is meant to ask, "Is this deed mine?" and to work so that the answer is yes. The foremost have their portrait; the right have theirs; the left have theirs. Mixing the portraits sentimentally — "surely everyone gets everything" — is not the Qur'an's method. Hope is wide; it is not formless.

**Title Deed of the Right Hand**

Verse 38 is a legal-style assignment: these blessings are *for* the companions of the right. The Qur'an's care with attribution teaches the reader to care about belonging. Work to be of the company named on the deed. The foremost have their portion; the right have theirs; envy between the saved is impossible in the end, but aspiration from right toward foremost remains praiseworthy in the trial.

**Assigned Portions**

The Qur'an assigns. Verse 38's "for the companions of the right" is a title deed line. Mixing all portraits into a vague universal gift is not the sūrah's method. Hope is wide — the right hand is many from later times — but it is not formless. Belong to the company named on the deed. The work of belonging is the work of faith, repentance, and perseverance.

**Checking the Deed**

Ask: is this deed mine? Work so that the answer is yes. Verse 38 assigns the preceding blessings to the companions of the right. Belonging is the work of faith, repentance, prayer, and perseverance. The foremost have their portrait; the right have theirs. Aspire up; do not envy across; do not blur the map into formless universalism.

**Among the Right Hand's Landscape**

This verse belongs to the landscape of aṣḥāb al-yamīn — thornless lote, clustered plantains, extended shade, gushing water, abundant fruit neither cut off nor forbidden, raised beds, new creation, amorous peers, many from old and later times. Ibn Kathīr notes they are the majority of the Garden. Al-Qurṭubī and others carefully distinguish their portion from the foremost's without demoting it from Paradise. Verse 38 is a panel in a landscape of broad mercy. The pastoral implication is permanent: aim for the foremost; do not despair of the right; the throng of later times still has seats. Cross-reference 69:19–24 and 84:7–9 for the record in the right hand that defines this company legally.

## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:39

> **many from those of old,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Large Company Across Early Time**

*Thullatun mina al-awwalīn* — "many from those of old." The same phrase used of the foremost in verse 13 is now used of the companions of the right. They too have a large company among the earlier generations. The word *thullah* again means a substantial throng, not a remnant.

**The Parallel and the Difference**

The parallel with verses 13–14 is deliberate and instructive. For the foremost: many from the old, few from the later. For the companions of the right: many from the old, and many from the later (v. 40). The elite thin out over time; the broad company of the saved does not. This is one of the most hopeful demographic statements in the Qur'an. However dark the later ages become, the right hand continues to be populated. God's mercy does not shrink to an elite as history proceeds.

**Earlier Communities and Earlier Muslims**

As with verse 13, "those of old" may mean earlier prophetic communities or earlier generations of this community. In either reading, the verse affirms that salvation has always had a numerous party. The God of the Qur'an is not the God of a tiny rescued few only; He is the God whose mercy "encompasses all things" (7:156) and whose Garden has room for throngs.

**Historical Mercy**

"Many from those of old" affirms that God's saving work in history is not a thin trickle. Earlier prophetic communities and earlier generations of this community produced throngs of the right hand. The believer who reads sacred history should not see only the failures and the punishments; he should see the *thullah* — the large company of those who believed, endured, and were taken in mercy. Verse 39 is a corrective to cynical readings of the past.

**Many Among the Early Right**

The throng of the right among earlier generations is a historical mercy. Salvation has always had numbers. The God of the Qur'an is not the rescuer of a tiny elite only. Verse 39, read with verse 40, builds a demography of hope across time: many then, many later, for the broad company of the saved.

**Historical Throngs of the Saved**

Many from those of old: salvation's history is a history of throngs, not only of lonely heroes. Earlier prophetic communities and earlier Muslim generations produced large companies of the right. Verse 39 corrects cynical readings of the past that see only failure. The past is full of those who believed, endured, and were taken in mercy. Remember them as a thullah, not as a remnant.

**Reading History as Throng of the Saved**

When you read sacred history, see the thullah — the large company who believed and endured — not only the failures and punishments. Verse 39 corrects cynical historiography. The past is full of the right hand. Let their density encourage your own entry into the company that continues in verse 40 through later times.

**Among the Right Hand's Landscape**

This verse belongs to the landscape of aṣḥāb al-yamīn — thornless lote, clustered plantains, extended shade, gushing water, abundant fruit neither cut off nor forbidden, raised beds, new creation, amorous peers, many from old and later times. Ibn Kathīr notes they are the majority of the Garden. Al-Qurṭubī and others carefully distinguish their portion from the foremost's without demoting it from Paradise. Verse 39 is a panel in a landscape of broad mercy. The pastoral implication is permanent: aim for the foremost; do not despair of the right; the throng of later times still has seats. ## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:40

> **and many from those of later times.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Right Hand Remains Crowded**

*Wa-thullatun mina al-ākhirīn* — "and many from those of later times." The decisive contrast with verse 14 is here. The foremost are few among the later; the companions of the right are many among the later. As time goes on, the highest tier becomes rare, but the broad tier of salvation remains well populated.

**Mercy That Scales with History**

This verse is a theological anchor against despair about the end times. The Prophet ﷺ described periods of severe trial, moral confusion, and the thinning of true knowledge. A reader of those descriptions might conclude that almost no one will be saved in later ages. Verse 40 corrects that conclusion. Many — a *thullah* — from those of later times will be companions of the right. The trials of later ages make the foremost scarce; they do not shut the gate of the right hand.

**Why the Difference?**

The foremost require a combination of initiative, spiritual ambition, and often supporting conditions that later ages erode. The companions of the right require faith, basic obedience, repentance, and the mercy of God — goods that remain available as long as the message of Islam remains on the earth. The Prophet ﷺ promised that a group of his community would continue upon the truth until the Hour. Verse 40 is consistent with that promise and extends its demographic hope.

**Personal Takeaway**

For the contemporary reader, verse 40 says: you live in "later times," and the right hand still has many seats. Your age does not disqualify you. Your proximity to the end of history, if anything, makes your holding-fast more precious. Do not use the darkness of the age as an excuse for mediocrity; use it as a reason for steadfastness. The throng of the later right hand is still being assembled, and it is still accepting members.

**Demographic Hope for Later Ages**

Verse 40 is one of the most pastoral verses in the Qur'an for anyone who lives late in sacred history. The foremost are few; the right are many. Your century does not sentence you to the left. The conditions of later times make excellence rarer; they do not make salvation impossible. The Prophet ﷺ promised that his community would not be left without a group upon the truth until the Hour. Verse 40 is the demographic face of that promise: a *thullah*, a throng, from those of later times.

**Working in the Later Age**

The correct response to "many from later times" is not complacency ("I will be among the many automatically") but labor ("the many is still being assembled, and I must enter it"). The throng of the later right hand is composed of individuals who chose faith, repentance, prayer, and honesty when the age made other choices easier. Join them by choosing what they chose.

**Later Times Still Populous with the Right**

The decisive demographic hope of the sūrah: the foremost thin out; the right hand does not. Later ages are hard on excellence at the highest tier and still open at the broad tier of salvation. Verse 40 is the pastoral anchor against end-times despair. Your age does not sentence you to the left. The throng of the later right hand is still being assembled. Join it by the choices that compose it.

**Joining the Later Throng**

You live in later times. The right hand still has many seats. Do not use the darkness of the age as excuse for mediocrity; use it as reason for steadfastness. Verse 40 is demographic hope and personal summons. The throng is composed of individuals who chose faith when other choices were easier. Choose what they chose.

**Among the Right Hand's Landscape**

This verse belongs to the landscape of aṣḥāb al-yamīn — thornless lote, clustered plantains, extended shade, gushing water, abundant fruit neither cut off nor forbidden, raised beds, new creation, amorous peers, many from old and later times. Ibn Kathīr notes they are the majority of the Garden. Al-Qurṭubī and others carefully distinguish their portion from the foremost's without demoting it from Paradise. Verse 40 is a panel in a landscape of broad mercy. The pastoral implication is permanent: aim for the foremost; do not despair of the right; the throng of later times still has seats. Cross-reference 69:19–24 and 84:7–9 for the record in the right hand that defines this company legally.

## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:41

> **And the companions of the left; what of the companions of the left?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Amazement at Ignominy**

*Wa-aṣḥābu al-shimāli mā aṣḥābu al-shimāl* — "And the companions of the left; what of the companions of the left?" The sūrah now turns to the third group with the same amazement-formula used for the right, but the wonder is inverted. Just as verse 27 indicated amazement at the state of the companions of the right, here the formula indicates amazement at the ignominy of the companions of the left. The grammar is identical so that the moral difference will be felt as a difference of content, not of rhetoric. The ear hears the same music; the meaning is a different world.

**Shimāl: The Left Hand of the Record**

*Shimāl* is the left. Those who receive their record in the left hand are described with devastating psychological realism in Sūrah al-Ḥāqqah:

> *"But as for one who is given his record in his left hand, he will say, 'O would that I had not been given my record, and had not known what my account is! O would that it had been the final death! My wealth has not availed me. Gone from me is my authority.'"* (Qur'an 69:25–29)

The cry for a final death — a non-existence that never comes — is the measure of the left's despair. Verse 41 of al-Wāqiʿah opens the dossier of that company.

**Why the Sūrah Insists on Looking**

The Qur'an could have summarized the fate of the wicked in a single clause. Instead it forces the reader to look — at scorching wind, boiling liquid, black smoke, Zaqqūm, and the ironic "welcome" of the Day of Judgment. The insistence is medicinal. Human beings are capable of astonishing self-deception about consequences. Concrete depiction is one of revelation's tools for breaking that deception. The companions of the left are described at length so that no one joins them by accident of inattention.

**Adam's Tears Revisited**

The ḥadīth of the Ascension, in which Adam weeps when he looks to his left, should govern the emotional tone of reading this section. The believer does not read about the companions of the left with relish. He reads with fear for himself and sorrow for every soul still capable of turning back. The Prophet ﷺ wept over his community. Verse 41 is not entertainment; it is warning issued by a mercy that still hopes to be heard.

**The Necessity of Looking at the Left**

Some modern readers wish the Qur'an would describe only Paradise. Verse 41 refuses that editorial preference. The amazement at the left is part of revelation's full counsel. Without it, the amazement at the right loses contrast, and the will loses one of its legitimate motives — fear (*khawf*) alongside hope (*rajāʾ*). The scholars have always said that the heart flies to God with two wings: hope and fear. Al-Wāqiʿah systematically strengthens both wings.

**Full Counsel Includes the Left**

A revelation that described only Paradise would be incomplete counsel. Verse 41 opens the third portrait because the heart flies with two wings — hope and fear. The amazement at ignominy strengthens the wing of fear without amputating the wing of hope. Adam weeps; the believer fears and works; the still-turnable are invited. Gloating is not the key. Vigilance is.

**Two Wings at the Left's Door**

As the left's portrait opens, keep both wings: hope and fear. Verse 41's amazement at ignominy strengthens fear without killing hope. You are still able to not be of this company. Read what follows as warning that still has time to be heard, not as entertainment or as someone else's problem only.

**Among the Left's Portrait and Its Lessons**

This verse belongs to the portrait of aṣḥāb al-shimāl — scorching wind, boiling liquid, black smoke, luxury without conscience, persistence in great sin, denial of resurrection, Zaqqūm, drink of parched camels, ironic welcome. The classical commentators refuse to soften the portrait; they also refuse to make it a cause for gloating. Adam weeps for the left in the ḥadīth of the Ascension. Verse 41 should produce fear for the self, sorrow for the still-turnable, and labor in invitation. Cross-reference 69:25–37 and 77:29–40 for parallel severity. The door of tawbah remains open until the throat; this portrait is warning while warning still functions as mercy.

---
## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:42

> **Amidst scorching wind and boiling liquid,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Samūm: The Wind That Enters the Pores**

*Fī samūmin wa-ḥamīm* — "Amidst scorching wind and boiling liquid." *Samūm* is a scorching, poisonous wind, etymologically related to the pores (*masām*) of the body because it is said to penetrate them (Ibn ʿAjībah, al-Qurṭubī, al-Zamakhsharī). It is not a wind that strikes the surface and passes; it is a wind that enters. The torment of the left begins with an environment that invades the person.

**Ḥamīm: Heat and the Extremes of Temperature**

*Ḥamīm* means boiling liquid and can also denote intense heat; some of the tradition notes that the denizens of Hell are afflicted with both extreme heat and extreme cold (cf. 78:24–25: "nothing cool shall they taste therein, nor any drink, except scalding water and [foul] purulence"). The companions of the left are placed *in* (*fī*) these elements — not merely near them, but inside their field. Environment itself has become adversary.

**The Inversion of Climate Mercy**

In this world, wind can cool and water can refresh. Both are named among God's mercies in the Qur'an. For the companions of the left, the elemental gifts are inverted. Wind scorches; liquid boils. The inversion is moral, not arbitrary: those who used the gifts of this world without gratitude or moral consideration find the elemental order itself turned against them. The sūrah will later ask the living whether they have considered the water they drink and the fire they kindle (vv. 68–73). Verse 42 is what those gifts become when the trial is over and the account is adverse.

**Psychological Parallel**

There is a this-worldly foreshadowing of *samūm* and *ḥamīm* in the life of unchecked desire: the "heat" of addiction, the "scorch" of rage, the "boil" of envy. These are not the Fire, but they are its distant rumors in the nervous system. The person who has felt what unregulated passion does to the body already knows, in miniature, that environments can invade. Verse 42 raises that miniature to the scale of the Hereafter.

**Elemental Inversion**

Wind and water, mercies in the trial-world, become *samūm* and *ḥamīm* for the left. The inversion teaches that created things have no fixed moral valence independent of God's assignment and the creature's state. The same water that cools the grateful burns the denier — not because the molecular structure changed in isolation, but because the Day reassigns environments to match destinies. Verse 42 is moral meteorology.

**Pores and Penetration**

*Samūm*'s relation to *masām* (pores) emphasizes invasion. The punishment is not only external impact; it enters. This matches the Qur'an's wider teaching that the Fire reaches the hearts in some descriptions (104:6–7). The left are people whose interiors are no longer protected. The *taqwā* that guarded the heart in the world is absent, and the environment exploits the absence.

**Climate as Recompense**

Scorching wind that enters the pores and boiling liquid that surrounds: the climate of the left is invasive and total. Elemental mercies of the trial-world are inverted. Verse 42 is moral meteorology — environments assigned to match destinies. The samūm of unchecked passion in this world is a distant rumor of this climate. Step out of the rumor before it becomes the weather.

**Stepping Out of Small Samūm**

Where passion scorches and invades your interior now — rage, addiction, envy — recognize the rumor of samūm and ḥamīm. Step out while stepping out is medicine. Verse 42's climate is the full weather of a life that never stepped out. Elemental mercies invert for those who used them without gratitude or moral weight. Use wind and water with thanks now.

**Among the Left's Portrait and Its Lessons**

This verse belongs to the portrait of aṣḥāb al-shimāl — scorching wind, boiling liquid, black smoke, luxury without conscience, persistence in great sin, denial of resurrection, Zaqqūm, drink of parched camels, ironic welcome. The classical commentators refuse to soften the portrait; they also refuse to make it a cause for gloating. Adam weeps for the left in the ḥadīth of the Ascension. Verse 42 should produce fear for the self, sorrow for the still-turnable, and labor in invitation. Cross-reference 69:25–37 and 77:29–40 for parallel severity. The door of tawbah remains open until the throat; this portrait is warning while warning still functions as mercy.

## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:43

> **and the shadow of black smoke,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Shade That Is Not Shade**

*Wa-ẓillin min yaḥmūm* — "and the shadow of black smoke." *Yaḥmūm* is black, thick smoke. The companions of the left have a "shade," but it is the shade of smoke — an ominous parody of the extended shade given to the companions of the right (v. 30). The comforting image of shade is replaced with an image that obscures true vision and indicates impure, choking air.

**The Rhetoric of Inverted Mercy**

Al-Zamakhsharī and others note that the shade of Hell has no positive attributes. The Qur'an elsewhere develops the same irony:

> *"Away to that which you used to deny! Away to a threefold shadow that provides no shade, nor avails against the flame. Indeed, it throws up sparks like massive tree trunks, as if they were yellow camels."* (Qur'an 77:29–33)

A shadow that provides no shade is a perfect emblem of false refuge. It looks like cover. It fails as cover. It may even harm the one who seeks shelter in it.

**Worldly Passions as Black Smoke**

The spiritual commentators have long seen in this image an analogy for the passions and distractions of this world. Such shadows are ephemeral, appearing to grant shade, but only obscuring true vision and cutting off the air of intellectual and spiritual clarity until they blow away. A person who takes refuge in wealth, status, or intoxication as shelter from the glare of mortality is standing under *yaḥmūm*. The smoke darkens; it does not cool. Verse 43 makes the eschatological form of that mistake visible.

**Obscuring True Vision**

Smoke blinds. One of the punishments of the left is the loss of clarity — the very clarity that, had it been accepted in this world, might have saved them. The Qur'an repeatedly describes disbelief as a covering on the heart and a veil on the eyes. The black smoke of the Hereafter is the externalization of that covering. What was chosen as interior darkness becomes exterior environment.

**Parody of Shelter**

Black smoke as "shadow" is parody. It takes the word that should mean shelter and fills it with choking darkness. The Qur'an uses parody as a moral instrument — "glad tidings" of punishment, "welcome" of Zaqqūm, "shade" of smoke. The form of mercy-language with the content of justice exposes the mismatch between the left's self-image and their reality.

**False Refuges Named**

Every false refuge in this world — wealth as ultimate shelter, status as ultimate cover, intoxication as ultimate shade — is a small *yaḥmūm*. It darkens vision and fails to cool. Verse 43 trains recognition. Before you stand under a shadow, ask what casts it. If it is smoke, step out while stepping out is still possible.

**Smoke That Claims to Be Shade**

The shadow of black smoke is false refuge in eschatological form. It looks like cover; it chokes; it darkens vision. Every worldly refuge that promises shelter and delivers obscurity is a small yaḥmūm. Verse 43 trains recognition. Wealth, status, and intoxication as ultimate shelters are smoke. The extended shade of verse 30 is the alternative. Choose the caster of your shadow carefully.

**Auditing Refuges**

List your shelters. Which are extended shade, and which are black smoke? Verse 43's yaḥmūm is false refuge in final form. Wealth, status, and intoxication as ultimate cover darken and fail. If a refuge neither cools the heart nor ennobles character, it is smoke. Exit. The extended shade of God and of righteous company remains available.

**Among the Left's Portrait and Its Lessons**

This verse belongs to the portrait of aṣḥāb al-shimāl — scorching wind, boiling liquid, black smoke, luxury without conscience, persistence in great sin, denial of resurrection, Zaqqūm, drink of parched camels, ironic welcome. The classical commentators refuse to soften the portrait; they also refuse to make it a cause for gloating. Adam weeps for the left in the ḥadīth of the Ascension. Verse 43 should produce fear for the self, sorrow for the still-turnable, and labor in invitation. Cross-reference 69:25–37 and 77:29–40 for parallel severity. The door of tawbah remains open until the throat; this portrait is warning while warning still functions as mercy.

## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:44

> **neither cool nor refreshing.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Two Negations That Empty the Image**

*Lā bāridin wa-lā karīm* — "neither cool nor refreshing." Or: "neither cool nor honorable/generous." The word *karīm* means noble, generous, honorable, and by extension, pleasant or refreshing when applied to a breeze or shade. Two qualities that ordinary shade should have — coolness and pleasantness — are explicitly stripped away. The verse is a surgical negation. It leaves the word "shadow" standing and removes everything that makes a shadow desirable.

**The Precision of Qur'anic Negation**

The Qur'an often defines the punishments of Hell by negated mercies as much as by positive torments. No coolness. No drink except boiling. No friend. No intercessor for those who rejected intercession's conditions. The method is psychologically exact. Human beings understand loss of expected good as clearly as they understand infliction of positive harm. Verse 44 takes the expectation built by the word "shadow" and bankrupts it.

**Karīm as a Moral Word**

If *karīm* is read with its full moral range — noble, generous — the negation deepens. The environment of the left is not only physically unpleasant; it is stripped of nobility. Nothing in it elevates. Nothing in it gives. The companions of the right received a shade that was *mamdūd* (extended) and, by implication, generous. The companions of the left receive a shadow that is neither cool nor *karīm*. The moral quality of the two destinies is written into their climates.

**Application: False Refuges Now**

Any refuge in this life that neither cools the heart nor ennobles the character is a small *yaḥmūm*. Entertainment that leaves the soul more restless, relationships that degrade, ideologies that excuse oppression — these are shadows without coolness. Verse 44 trains the diagnostic eye. Before standing under a shade, ask: does it cool, and does it ennoble? If neither, it is not shade.

**Negation as Definition**

Defining by negation — neither cool nor refreshing — is a precise rhetorical tool. It leaves the noun ("shadow") and removes the expected adjectives. The mind is forced to hold an empty form. That emptiness is the experiential quality of the left's environment: forms of good without the content of good. Verse 44 is the grammar of disappointment raised to eschatological scale.

**Karīm Withheld**

If *karīm* is read as noble/generous, the shadow of the left is stripped even of nobility. Nothing in it elevates or gives. The companions of the right receive shade that is extended and, by implication, generous. The moral climate of the two destinies is written into their weather. Environments preach. Verse 44 is the sermon of a failed shade.

**Empty Form of Shelter**

Neither cool nor refreshing: the noun "shadow" remains; the adjectives that make shadow desirable are stripped. Verse 44 is the grammar of disappointment. Forms of good without content of good — the left's environment in one formula. Before standing under any shade in this world, ask the two questions the verse implies: does it cool, and does it ennoble? If neither, it is not shade.

**Two Questions for Every Shade**

Does it cool? Does it ennoble? Verse 44's double negation supplies the diagnostic. Apply it to entertainment, relationships, ideologies, and careers. Empty forms of shelter are the left's weather in miniature. Prefer shade that is both bārid and karīm — cool and noble — and prepare for the shade that never fails.

**Among the Left's Portrait and Its Lessons**

This verse belongs to the portrait of aṣḥāb al-shimāl — scorching wind, boiling liquid, black smoke, luxury without conscience, persistence in great sin, denial of resurrection, Zaqqūm, drink of parched camels, ironic welcome. The classical commentators refuse to soften the portrait; they also refuse to make it a cause for gloating. Adam weeps for the left in the ḥadīth of the Ascension. Verse 44 should produce fear for the self, sorrow for the still-turnable, and labor in invitation. Cross-reference 69:25–37 and 77:29–40 for parallel severity. The door of tawbah remains open until the throat; this portrait is warning while warning still functions as mercy.
