## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:66

> **[saying] "Truly we have suffered loss."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The First Cry of the Ruined Harvester**

*Innā la-mughramūn* — "'Truly we have suffered loss.'" *Mughram* means one afflicted by debt, loss, or ruinous cost. The first reaction to a destroyed crop is the recognition of loss — the seed-price, the labor, the hoped-for food, all gone. The verse puts this cry in the mouths of those who, had God willed, would be standing over chaff.

**Loss as a Teacher**

The Qur'an does not mock the cry of loss. Loss is real, and the pain of it is acknowledged. What the wider passage challenges is the failure to cry out in gratitude when loss is withheld. The same mouth that would say *innā la-mughramūn* over chaff should say *al-ḥamdu lillāh* over grain. Verse 66 is the negative image of the thanks that should have been.

**Spiritual Loss Parallel**

There is a spiritual *mughram* as well: the person who invests a life in what does not survive the Event, and discovers the loss on a Day when reinvestment is impossible. The ruined harvester of this world can often plant again next season. The ruined harvester of the Hereafter cannot. The crop-image points beyond itself.

**Loss and the Language of Faith**

When loss comes, the Qur'an does not demand cheerful slogans. It records the cry *innā la-mughramūn* as a human reaction. What it demands is that loss not become the only lens — that the same tongue also know *al-ḥamdu lillāh* for what remains and *innā lillāh* for what returns to God. Verse 66 is a diagnostic of the first cry; the wider passage trains the second.

**Mughramūn and the Ledger of Loss**

The cry of loss is human and recorded without mockery. Verse 66 freezes it so that the comfortable may hear their own potential voice and may speak differently while loss is still withheld. The eschatological *mughram* is the one whose life-investment returns as loss on the Day when markets do not reopen.

**First Cry Over Chaff**

Truly we have suffered loss. The cry is human and recorded without mockery. Verse 66 freezes it so that the comfortable hear their potential voice and speak differently while loss is withheld. The eschatological mughram is the one whose life-investment returns as loss on the Day when seasons do not restart. Worldly loss can teach; ultimate loss cannot be reinvested past the Event.

**Loss as Teacher, Not Only Wound**

When loss comes, cry if you must — verse 66 records the cry — then learn. Smaller losses are drills for the recognition of what ultimate loss would mean. Invest in what survives the Event. The mughram of the Day cannot reinvest. The mughram of a season often can. Use the can to prepare for the cannot.

**Among the Proofs of Resurrection**

This verse belongs to the sūrah's third section — the arguments from seed, death and re-creation, crop, water, and fire, culminating in the command to glorify. Al-Rāzī and the mutakallimūn developed these into formal kalām; the ordinary believer receives them as a curriculum of looking. A-fa-raʾaytum structures the pedagogy: have you considered? Verse 66 is one consideration in the chain. The logic is cumulative: the God of the drop, the decree of death, the harvest, the rain, and the flame is the God who gathers at the mīqāt. Affirm the Agent in each theater, and the resurrection ceases to be an isolated demand and becomes the natural second act of a known power. End every consideration in tasbīḥ as verse 74 requires.

## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:67

> **Nay! We are deprived."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Second Cry: Deprivation**

*Bal naḥnu maḥrūmūn* — "'Nay! We are deprived.'" *Maḥrūm* means cut off from provision, barred from the good one hoped for (Ibn ʿAjībah). The *bal* ("nay, rather") intensifies the first cry: not only have we lost what we invested; we are actively deprived, excluded from the harvest that might have been.

**Maḥrūm in the Wider Lexicon**

The Qur'an uses *maḥrūm* for the person who is denied provision and should be included in charity:

> *"And from their wealth is a right for the beggar and the deprived."* (Qur'an 51:19)

In verse 67, the same word is the self-description of people who would have been cut off from the crop. The irony for the companions of the left is that they lived as *mutrafūn* (in luxury) and will end as the truly *maḥrūmūn* — deprived of Paradise. The temporary deprivation of a failed harvest was a warning they did not take.

**Ingratitude's Focus**

The passage implies, as the next pericopes will also, that human beings fail to be thankful for what God provides, focusing more upon what does not come to fruition than upon what does — a habit also implied in verse 82. The psychology is familiar: one failed plan erases the felt memory of ten mercies. Verse 67 freezes that psychology in a single cry, so that the reader may recognize and correct it.

**The Cry of the Maḥrūm**

*Bal naḥnu maḥrūmūn* is the voice of radical disappointment. The Qur'an preserves it so that the comfortable may hear what they would say if God withdrew His gift — and may therefore speak differently while the gift remains. The true *maḥrūm* in the eschatological sense is the one deprived of Paradise. Every lesser deprivation is a warning drill. Treat it as such.

**Maḥrūm as Self-Description**

To say 'we are deprived' over a failed harvest is accurate in the worldly sense. The sūrah wants that accuracy extended: the truly deprived are those deprived of Paradise. Verse 67's cry is a drill for a larger recognition. Treat smaller deprivations as warning drills, not as proof of cosmic indifference.

**Second Cry: Deprivation**

Nay! We are deprived. Bal intensifies: not only loss of investment but exclusion from the harvest that might have been. Maḥrūm in the wider Qur'an includes those who should receive charity. Here it is the self-description of the hypothetically ruined. The true maḥrūm eschatologically is the one deprived of Paradise. Verse 67's cry is a drill. Treat smaller deprivations as warning drills for the larger recognition.

**Redefining Deprivation**

The truly deprived are those deprived of Paradise. Verse 67's worldly maḥrūm-cry is a drill for that redefinition. Hold earthly deprivation lightly enough to learn from it, seriously enough to care for the maḥrūm who need charity (51:19), and eschatologically enough to fear the final deprivation. Three registers, one word.

**Among the Proofs of Resurrection**

Al-Rāzī and the mutakallimūn developed these into formal kalām; the ordinary believer receives them as a curriculum of looking. A-fa-raʾaytum structures the pedagogy: have you considered? Verse 67 is one consideration in the chain. The logic is cumulative: the God of the drop, the decree of death, the harvest, the rain, and the flame is the God who gathers at the mīqāt. Affirm the Agent in each theater, and the resurrection ceases to be an isolated demand and becomes the natural second act of a known power. End every consideration in tasbīḥ as verse 74 requires.

## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:68

> **Have you considered the water that you drink?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Third Proof: Water**

*A-fa-raʾaytumu al-māʾa alladhī tashrabūn* — "Have you considered the water that you drink?" The third *a-fa-raʾaytum* turns to the most basic liquid of life. Seed, crop, water — the chain of survival. Every drinker is addressed. The argument needs no specialized knowledge. If you drink, you are already inside the sign.

**Water in the Qur'anic Cosmos**

> *"And We made every living thing from water."* (Qur'an 21:30)

> *"Have you seen that which you till? ... Have you seen the water that you drink? ... Have you seen the fire that you kindle?"* (Qur'an 56:63–71, the triad)

Water is cited as blessing (35:27; 39:21), as life-giver (2:164; 6:99; 7:57), as a sign that can be removed (18:41; 67:30). Verse 68 opens this dossier with a question as simple as a cup in the hand.

**The Intimacy of the Daily Act**

People drink without thinking. The Qur'an asks them to think once. The glass of water on the table is a theological exhibit. Who put the water cycle in place? Who sweetened what could have been salt? The next verses will press these questions. For now, the command is simply: look at what you drink.

**The Cup as Minbar**

A cup of water can preach. Verse 68 makes it a pulpit: have you considered? The simplicity is the profundity. Intellectual systems may rise and fall; the need to drink remains; the question remains. The believer can make every drink a small act of *tadhakkur* by naming God before it and praising Him after it. The cup becomes a continuous remembrance.

**Water as Continuous Sign**

You drink daily; the sign is daily. Verse 68 does not require pilgrimage to a miracle-site. The miracle is the cup. Have you considered it? The question, once heard, can accompany every subsequent drink until drinking itself becomes dhikr.

**The Cup as Continuous Sign**

Have you considered the water that you drink? Daily need, daily sign. Verse 68 requires no journey to a marvel; the marvel is the cup. Once the question is heard, every subsequent drink can become tadhakkur. Name God before it; praise Him after it. The cup becomes a minbar. Ingratitude for water, the commentators note, implies ingratitude for life itself.

**Cup as Dhikr**

Attach remembrance to drinking. Verse 68's question, once heard, can ride with every cup for the rest of your life. Have you considered? Yes — and I thank. The sign is continuous because the need is continuous. Ingratitude for water is ingratitude for life's substrate. Close that ingratitude one sip at a time.

**Among the Proofs of Resurrection**

This verse belongs to the sūrah's third section — the arguments from seed, death and re-creation, crop, water, and fire, culminating in the command to glorify. Al-Rāzī and the mutakallimūn developed these into formal kalām; the ordinary believer receives them as a curriculum of looking. A-fa-raʾaytum structures the pedagogy: have you considered? Verse 68 is one consideration in the chain. The logic is cumulative: the God of the drop, the decree of death, the harvest, the rain, and the flame is the God who gathers at the mīqāt. Affirm the Agent in each theater, and the resurrection ceases to be an isolated demand and becomes the natural second act of a known power. End every consideration in tasbīḥ as verse 74 requires.

## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:69

> **Is it you who sent it down from the clouds, or is it We Who send down?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Binary Applied to Rain**

*A-antum anzaltumūhu min al-muzni am naḥnu al-munzilūn* The same forced binary as verses 59 and 64. *Muzn* means clouds bearing water. *Inzāl* — sending down — is one of the Qur'an's great verbs, used for rain and for revelation alike. The God who sends down water sends down guidance. Both are *nuzūl*.

**Human Helplessness Before the Cloud**

No civilization, however technical, sends rain from clouds by sovereign command. Cloud-seeding and weather modification are marginal, uncertain, and still parasitic on a system no human built. Verse 69's question remains substantially unanswerable in favor of "you." The clouds do not report to human ministries.

**Rain as Mercy and as Parallel to Revelation**

The Qur'an deliberately uses the same vocabulary for rain and for revelation (*nazzala*, *anzala*). Hearts are likened to land that receives rain or fails to (2:264–265). Verse 69, in a sūrah that will soon speak of the Noble Qur'an (v. 77), quietly prepares that parallel. The One who sends water for bodies sends words for souls.

**Inzāl: Rain and Revelation**

The shared vocabulary of sending-down for rain and for revelation is one of the Qur'an's deepest linguistic theologies. Land that receives rain lives; land that does not dies. Hearts that receive revelation live; hearts that do not die. Verse 69, standing just before the section on the Noble Qur'an, is a hinge between physical and spiritual hydrology.

**Clouds Do Not Report to Us**

The binary of verse 69 remains substantially unanswered in favor of 'you.' Cloud-seeding and meteorology describe and marginally tweak; they do not own the *inzāl*. The Lord of the worlds sends down. Rain and revelation share the verb. Both are gifts. Both require thanks.

**Sending Down as Shared Verb**

Is it you who sent it down from the clouds, or is it We Who send down? Inzāl names both rain and revelation. Hearts are land that receives or rejects. Verse 69, just before the section on the Noble Qur'an, is the hinge between physical and spiritual hydrology. Clouds do not report to human ministries. The Lord of the worlds sends down. Both waters — of the sky and of the Book — require thanks.

**Rain and Revelation Together**

When it rains, remember revelation. Shared inzāl vocabulary makes weather a cousin of the Book. Verse 69 is the hinge. Hearts are land. Some receive and live; some harden and die. Soften. Receive. The clouds do not report to you; the Lord of the worlds sends down what He wills. Ask for both rains — of sky and of guidance.

**Among the Proofs of Resurrection**

This verse belongs to the sūrah's third section — the arguments from seed, death and re-creation, crop, water, and fire, culminating in the command to glorify. Al-Rāzī and the mutakallimūn developed these into formal kalām; the ordinary believer receives them as a curriculum of looking. A-fa-raʾaytum structures the pedagogy: have you considered? Verse 69 is one consideration in the chain. The logic is cumulative: the God of the drop, the decree of death, the harvest, the rain, and the flame is the God who gathers at the mīqāt. Affirm the Agent in each theater, and the resurrection ceases to be an isolated demand and becomes the natural second act of a known power. End every consideration in tasbīḥ as verse 74 requires.

## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:70

> **If We had willed, We would have made it bitter; will you not, then, give thanks?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Counterfactual of Bitterness**

*Law nashāʾu jaʿalnāhu ujājan fa-law-lā tashkurūn* *Ujāj* means bitterly salty, brackish — undrinkable. The sweet water that human beings take for granted is a choice of God, not a necessity of nature. Salt water dominates the planet's surface. Fresh water is the exception. The exception is mercy.

**Shukr: The Required Response**

*Tashkurūn* — give thanks, be grateful. *Shukr* is the Qur'anic opposite of *kufr* (ingratitude/disbelief) in one of the word's deepest polarities. To thank is to believe in a certain mode; to drink without thanks is to practice a small unbelief. Verse 70 makes the cup of water a site of *shukr*. The Prophet ﷺ taught naming God before drinking and praising Him after.

**Other Verses of Water's Contingency**

> *"Say, 'Have you considered: if your water was to become sunken [into the earth], then who could bring you flowing water?'"* (Qur'an 67:30)

> *"Or its water sinks [into the ground], so you would never be able to seek it out."* (Qur'an 18:41)

Al-Wāqiʿah's contribution is to add bitterness as another mode of loss — not only absence of water, but presence of undrinkable water. The mercy is not only that water comes, but that it comes sweet.

**Ingratitude for Water as Ingratitude for Life**

The commentators note that ingratitude for water implies ingratitude for all God's blessings and for the gift of life itself. Water is so foundational that thanking for it is thanking for the substrate of every other good. Verse 70 is therefore a root-command of gratitude. From a glass of water, the whole tree of *shukr* can grow.

**Bitterness Withheld**

Most of the planet's water is already *ujāj* — salt, undrinkable. Fresh water is the minority mercy. Verse 70 asks the drinker to notice the minority mercy and to thank. The counterfactual "if We had willed" is barely counterfactual globally; it is the majority state of earth's water. Your glass is the exception. *Fa-law-lā tashkurūn* — will you not, then, give thanks?

**Sweetness as Choice of God**

If We had willed, We would have made it bitter; will you not, then, give thanks? Most of earth's water is already ujāj. Fresh water is the minority mercy. Verse 70 asks the drinker to notice the minority mercy. Shukr is the required response; shukr is the opposite of kufr in one of the Qur'an's deepest polarities. The glass in your hand is the exception that proves the mercy. Thank.

**Minority Mercy Noticed**

Fresh water is minority mercy on a salt planet. Verse 70 asks you to notice. Shukr is the required response. Make the noticing explicit once a day: this glass could have been ujāj; it is not; thank You. The counterfactual is barely counterfactual globally. Your cup is the exception. Live like someone who knows.

**Among the Proofs of Resurrection**

This verse belongs to the sūrah's third section — the arguments from seed, death and re-creation, crop, water, and fire, culminating in the command to glorify. Al-Rāzī and the mutakallimūn developed these into formal kalām; the ordinary believer receives them as a curriculum of looking. A-fa-raʾaytum structures the pedagogy: have you considered? Verse 70 is one consideration in the chain. The logic is cumulative: the God of the drop, the decree of death, the harvest, the rain, and the flame is the God who gathers at the mīqāt. Affirm the Agent in each theater, and the resurrection ceases to be an isolated demand and becomes the natural second act of a known power. End every consideration in tasbīḥ as verse 74 requires.

## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:71

> **Have you considered the fire that you kindle?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Fourth Proof: Fire**

*A-fa-raʾaytumu al-nāra allatī tūrūn* — "Have you considered the fire that you kindle?" The fourth and final *a-fa-raʾaytum* turns to fire — the energy that cooks, warms, lights, and, in the eschatological register, also punishes. *Tūrūn* from *wrā* means to strike fire, to kindle, to make fire appear from its sources. The desert audience kindled fire from wood and tinder; later audiences kindle it by switches and engines. The question survives the technology.

**Fire as Gift and as Sign**

Fire is listed among the blessings that make human civilization possible. It is also, throughout the Qur'an, the primary image of Hell. This double valence is intentional. The same creature that warms the hands can destroy the house. The same reality that is "an enjoyment for the desert dwellers" (v. 73) is a "reminder" of a greater Fire. Verse 71 asks the kindler to look at what he holds.

**The Chain of Four**

Seed, crop, water, fire — the four proofs of verses 58–73 form a complete elementary curriculum: origin of the person, origin of food, origin of drink, origin of energy. Together they cover the conditions of biological and social life. The one who affirms God's agency in all four is ready to affirm God's agency in resurrection. The one who denies it in the resurrection is invited to notice that he already depends on it in the kitchen and the field.

**Fire as the Final Elementary Sign**

With fire, the curriculum of four signs completes. Origin (seed), food (crop), drink (water), energy (fire): the basics of survival. The denier of resurrection lives inside all four and attributes them to habit, nature, or self. Verse 71 insists on one more look. The kindled flame is a question mark shaped in light and heat. Who made the tree that feeds it? The next verse will ask.

**Fire's Double Face**

The same element cooks dinner and images Hell. Verse 71 begins the final elementary proof by asking for a look at the kindled flame. Looking is the beginning of *tadhkirah*. The next verses will split the look into reminder and enjoyment — the double purpose God built into the flame.

**Fourth Look: The Flame**

Have you considered the fire that you kindle? The curriculum of four signs completes with energy. Seed, crop, water, fire: origin, food, drink, power. Verse 71 asks for one more look at what the hand kindles. Technology changes the ignition method; it does not retire the question. Who stands behind the flame? The next verse pushes from flame to tree to Originator.

**Flame as Dual Teacher**

Let every flame teach twice — enjoyment and reminder. Verse 71 begins the look; verse 73 names the double purpose. Cook with thanks. See with awareness. The sample of Hell's heat is scaled to teach without destroying. Learn the lesson at the stove and the candle so you need not learn it at the jaḥīm.

**Among the Proofs of Resurrection**

This verse belongs to the sūrah's third section — the arguments from seed, death and re-creation, crop, water, and fire, culminating in the command to glorify. Al-Rāzī and the mutakallimūn developed these into formal kalām; the ordinary believer receives them as a curriculum of looking. A-fa-raʾaytum structures the pedagogy: have you considered? Verse 71 is one consideration in the chain. The logic is cumulative: the God of the drop, the decree of death, the harvest, the rain, and the flame is the God who gathers at the mīqāt. Affirm the Agent in each theater, and the resurrection ceases to be an isolated demand and becomes the natural second act of a known power. End every consideration in tasbīḥ as verse 74 requires.

## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:72

> **Is it you who brought into being the tree thereof, or is it We Who bring into being?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Tree Behind the Fire**

*A-antum anshaʾtum shajaratahā am naḥnu al-munshiʾūn* — "Is it you who brought into being the tree thereof, or is it We Who bring into being?" Pre-modern fire in Arabia came especially from certain trees whose wood and twigs were used for kindling — the *markh* and *ʿafār* are named in the poetic and exegetical tradition. The verse pushes the question one step back from the flame to the tree that feeds the flame. You kindle; but who created the fuel?

**Anshaʾa Again**

The verb *anshaʾa*, used earlier for the new creation of the companions of Paradise (v. 35), returns here for the tree of fire. God originates both the beauty of the Garden and the wood of the hearth. The range of *inshāʾ* is the range of creation itself.

**The Binary's Last Application**

As with the seed, the crop, and the water, the binary admits no comfortable third option. Either human beings originate the trees that fuel their fires, or God does. The answer is not in doubt. What is in doubt, for the denier, is whether he will allow the answer to affect his eschatology.

**From Fuel to Resurrection**

If God can store energy in a tree for years and release it at the strike of a flint, He can store the human being in the earth and release him at the strike of the Trumpet. The analogy is not mechanical; it is agentive. The same Originator operates in both theaters.

**The Tree Thereof**

Pushing the question from fire to tree is a characteristic Qur'anic move: do not stop at the proximate cause. The flame depends on fuel; fuel depends on a living tree; the living tree depends on the Originator. Modern supply chains make this chain longer and more opaque — electricity depends on grids, grids on fuels, fuels on geology and photosynthesis — but the terminus of the regress is the same. *Am naḥnu al-munshiʾūn* — or are We the ones who bring into being?

**Regress to the Originator**

From flame to tree to Originator: the Qur'anic habit of not stopping at proximate causes. Verse 72 pushes one step back and asks the binary again. The answer installs God at the root of energy itself. Every subsequent fire — candle, stove, engine, star — sits inside that installation for the one who has affirmed it.

**Fuel and the Originator**

Is it you who brought into being the tree thereof, or is it We Who bring into being? The regress from flame to fuel to living tree to Originator is characteristic Qur'anic reasoning. Do not stop at the proximate cause. Verse 72 installs God at the root of energy. Every subsequent fire sits inside that installation for the one who has affirmed it. Anshaʾa returns from the new creation of verse 35 to the tree of the hearth: one verb, two theaters, one Agent.

**Not Stopping at Proximate Causes**

Practice the regress: flame to fuel to tree to Originator. Verse 72 models not stopping at the proximate. Apply the habit everywhere — success to means to body to Creator; beauty to form to bestower. The habit installs tawḥīd at the root of ordinary perception. Keep pushing one step back until you arrive at Naḥnu al-munshiʾūn.

## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:73

> **We made it a reminder and an enjoyment for the desert dwellers.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Two Purposes of Fire**

*Naḥnu jaʿalnāhā tadhkiratan wa-matāʿan li-l-muqwīn* — "We made it a reminder and an enjoyment for the desert dwellers." Fire has a double divine purpose: *tadhkirah* (reminder, admonition) and *matāʿ* (enjoyment, provision, useful goods). The same flame that cooks the meal reminds the soul of the Fire that does not cook but punishes. God is efficient in His signs; one created thing carries two messages.

**Reminder of Resurrection and of Hell**

Ibn ʿAjībah, al-Bayḍāwī, and al-Ṭabarī note that fire reminds one of the Resurrection or of the Fire of Hell. The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"This fire of yours that human beings kindle contains but a seventieth of the heat of Hell."* (related by al-Ṭabarī and in the ḥadīth literature; cf. al-Bukhārī 3265)

Every earthly fire is a scaled-down sample. The sample is merciful: hot enough to teach, not hot enough to be the thing it teaches about.

**Muqwīn: Desert Dwellers and Travelers**

*Muqwīn* is interpreted as desert dwellers, or as all travelers (al-Qurṭubī, al-Shawkānī, al-Ṭabarī), or as everyone who benefits from fire (same sources). The desert reading is vivid: for the dweller in a cold night without walls, fire is life. The general reading is also true: every human culture is a fire-culture. The enjoyment is universal; the reminder is universal; the gratitude should be universal.

**Holding Both Purposes**

Religious people sometimes emphasize only the reminder (fire as threat) and forget the enjoyment (fire as gift). Worldly people reverse the error. Verse 73 holds both. Use the fire with thanks; see the fire with awareness. The hearth is simultaneously a mercy and a parable.

**Tadhkirah and Matāʿ in Every Gift**

The double purpose of fire — reminder and enjoyment — is a template for reading all worldly goods. Every gift is *matāʿ* (to be used and enjoyed within limits) and *tadhkirah* (to point beyond itself). Wealth, health, children, technology: each is both. The companions of the left treated gifts as *matāʿ* only. The foremost and the right treated them as both. Verse 73 teaches the double vision in the medium of a campfire.

**Template for Reading All Gifts**

Reminder and enjoyment: the double purpose of fire is the template for reading every worldly good. Wealth, health, technology, children — each is *matāʿ* within limits and *tadhkirah* beyond itself. The left took only *matāʿ*. The saved took both. Verse 73 teaches double vision at the campfire.

**Double Purpose as Template**

We made it a reminder and an enjoyment for the desert dwellers. Tadhkirah and matāʿ: the double purpose of fire is the template for reading all worldly goods. Each gift is to be used within limits and to point beyond itself. The left took only enjoyment. The saved took both. The ḥadīth that earthly fire is one-seventieth of Hell's heat makes every flame a scaled sample — hot enough to teach, not hot enough to be the thing it teaches about.

**Double Vision on All Gifts**

Apply tadhkirah and matāʿ to wealth, health, technology, and children. Each is enjoyment within limits and reminder beyond itself. Verse 73's template is portable. The left took only matāʿ. Take both. Enjoy the fire; remember the Fire. Enjoy the gift; remember the Giver. Double vision is the saved's way of seeing.

**Among the Proofs of Resurrection**

This verse belongs to the sūrah's third section — the arguments from seed, death and re-creation, crop, water, and fire, culminating in the command to glorify. Al-Rāzī and the mutakallimūn developed these into formal kalām; the ordinary believer receives them as a curriculum of looking. A-fa-raʾaytum structures the pedagogy: have you considered? Verse 73 is one consideration in the chain. The logic is cumulative: the God of the drop, the decree of death, the harvest, the rain, and the flame is the God who gathers at the mīqāt. Affirm the Agent in each theater, and the resurrection ceases to be an isolated demand and becomes the natural second act of a known power. End every consideration in tasbīḥ as verse 74 requires.

## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:74

> **So glorify the Name of thy Lord, the Magnificent!**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The First Command to Glorify**

*Fa-sabbiḥ bi-smi rabbika al-ʿaẓīm* — "So glorify the Name of thy Lord, the Magnificent!" After the four proofs of creation, the sūrah issues its first explicit command of response. *Fa-* indicates consequence: because God is the Creator of seed, crop, water, and fire, glorify His Name. The section of argument ends in worship. Knowledge that does not terminate in *tasbīḥ* is incomplete.

**Tasbīḥ: Declaring Transcendence**

*Tasbīḥ* means to declare God free of all deficiency, likeness, and unworthy attribution — to say *subḥān Allāh*. Ibn ʿAjībah notes that although God is responsible for all the things mentioned in verses 58–73, He is completely beyond them: He performs countless other acts, and none of the perceived deficiencies of created things can be attributed to Him. Glorification protects the Creator from being reduced to His effects.

**Bi-smi Rabbika: By/With the Name**

The phrase can mean "glorify the Name of thy Lord" or "glorify by [invoking] the Name of thy Lord." In the second reading, it is a command to dhikr — to invoke the Name. The Magnificent (*al-ʿAẓīm*) can modify either "Name" or "Lord" (al-Bayḍāwī). Both attachments are theologically sound: the Lord is Magnificent, and His Name is Magnificent.

**This Verse in the Prayer**

This verse (and its twin at 56:96) is connected in the Sunnah to the posture of rukūʿ (bowing). The Prophet ﷺ taught that when the verse was revealed, he instructed the community to say in bowing:

> *"Glory be to my Lord, the Magnificent."* (Abū Dāwūd 869; Ibn Mājah 887; related also upon the revelation of 56:96 for the prostration's "Glory be to my Lord, the Most High" in connection with 87:1)

The body learns the verse: the back bows under the weight of *al-ʿAẓīm*, and the tongue fills the bow with *tasbīḥ*.

**Why Argument Must End in Praise**

The four proofs could have ended in a syllogism. They end in a command to glorify. Islamic epistemology does not treat God as a conclusion to be filed; it treats Him as a Lord to be praised. Verse 74 is the proper exit from the museum of signs: you have seen the exhibits; now say *subḥān Allāh*.

**From Proof to Praise**

The correct exit from a museum of signs is not a quiz; it is *tasbīḥ*. Verse 74 models the epistemology of *īmān*: knowledge of created things should terminate in glorification of the Creator. Information that does not end in praise has not yet become Islamic knowledge. The four proofs (seed, crop, water, fire) are completed only when the tongue says *subḥāna rabbiya al-ʿaẓīm*.

**Rukūʿ as Embodied Verse**

The Sunnah's linking of this verse to the *tasbīḥ* of bowing means that every prayer reenacts verse 74. The back bows under the Magnificent; the tongue fills the bow with His glory. A Muslim who has prayed for years without connecting rukūʿ to al-Wāqiʿah may begin now. The body has been living the verse; the mind can catch up.

**Exit into Tasbīḥ**

So glorify the Name of thy Lord, the Magnificent. The proof-section exits into worship. Knowledge of signs that does not terminate in tasbīḥ has not yet become īmān's knowledge. Verse 74 is linked in the Sunnah to the tasbīḥ of rukūʿ: subḥāna rabbiya al-ʿaẓīm. Every bowing reenacts this exit. The back bows under the Magnificent; the tongue fills the bow with glory. Argument ends properly in praise.

**Bowing as the Verse's Body**

In the next rukūʿ, consciously connect subḥāna rabbiya al-ʿaẓīm to verse 74 and to the four proofs that preceded it. The body has been living the verse; let the mind catch up. Exit every consideration of created things into tasbīḥ. Information that ends in praise has become Islamic knowledge. Make that ending habitual.

**Among the Proofs of Resurrection**

This verse belongs to the sūrah's third section — the arguments from seed, death and re-creation, crop, water, and fire, culminating in the command to glorify. Al-Rāzī and the mutakallimūn developed these into formal kalām; the ordinary believer receives them as a curriculum of looking. A-fa-raʾaytum structures the pedagogy: have you considered? Verse 74 is one consideration in the chain. The logic is cumulative: the God of the drop, the decree of death, the harvest, the rain, and the flame is the God who gathers at the mīqāt. Affirm the Agent in each theater, and the resurrection ceases to be an isolated demand and becomes the natural second act of a known power. End every consideration in tasbīḥ as verse 74 requires.

## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:75

> **I swear by the places where the stars descend!**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Opening of the Fourth Section**

*Fa-lā uqsimu bi-mawāqiʿi al-nujūm* — "I swear by the places where the stars descend!" The sūrah's fourth section begins with a divine oath. *Fa-lā uqsimu* is a distinctive Qur'anic oath-formula. The particle *lā* is read by most as emphatic ("indeed I swear") and by some as a negation that rebukes prior false speech before the oath ("Nay! I swear") (al-Qurṭubī, al-Shawkānī, al-Ṭabarī). In either reading, what follows is solemn.

**Mawāqiʿ al-Nujūm: Multiple Layers**

"The places where the stars descend" most likely refers to the time, place, or manner in which celestial bodies set, or to the stations of the stars (cf. 85:1; 25:61). It can also refer to the fading of the stars on the Day of Judgment (81:2). When taken as the setting of the stars, Ibn ʿAjībah and others see an allusion to the times of night best for supererogatory devotion (cf. 73:1–2, 20; 76:26).

**Nujūm as Portions of Revelation**

*Nujūm* can also mean "portions" or "progressive installments." Some early commentators thus read: "I swear by the descending of the progressive installments [of the Qur'an]" — the gradual revelation, three, four, and five verses at a time, over twenty-three years (Ibn Kathīr, al-Qurṭubī, al-Ṭabarī; cf. 17:106; 25:32). In this reading, the oath is by the mode of the Qur'an's arrival itself.

**Hearts as Landing Places**

Al-Rāzī preserves a further reading: the places where the stars descend can allude to the hearts of God's servants — angels, messengers, and the righteous — into which the "stars" or the installments of revelation descend. The oath, on this view, is by the receptive hearts that catch what God sends down.

**Why Swear by the Stars' Setting?**

The setting of stars marks the passage of night, the approach of dawn, the rhythm of time itself. To swear by their places of descent is to swear by the ordered, majestic, punctual cosmos — a cosmos that does not deny its Appointed One. The deniers of resurrection live under a sky that is already a counter-evidence to chaos and pointlessness. Verse 75 raises that sky into the form of an oath.

**Oaths in the Qur'an**

God swears by created things throughout the Qur'an — by the sun, the night, the fig, the pen, the mount. The oaths elevate the created thing as sign and elevate the statement that follows as solemn. Verse 75's oath by the stars' places (or by the installments of revelation) introduces the claim that the Qur'an is noble. Cosmic order and revealed order are paired under one Author.

**Night Prayers and Setting Stars**

If the setting of stars alludes to the times of night devotion, the oath quietly recommends what it swears by. The *tahajjud* hours, when stars descend toward dawn, are the hours the Prophet ﷺ loved. Verse 75 can be read as a cosmic invitation to those hours: the sky itself is an oath-witness, and the witness is most visible when the world sleeps and the servant rises.

**Oath by the Sky's Order**

I swear by the places where the stars descend. The fourth section opens with a divine oath. Whether read as star-settings, star-stations, the fading of stars at the Hour, the installments of revelation, or the hearts that receive them, the oath elevates what follows. Cosmic order and revealed order are paired. The deniers of resurrection live under a sky that already testifies to order and appointment. Verse 75 raises that sky into solemn witness.

**Night Sky as Oath-Witness**

Look at the stars' setting occasionally as oath-witness. Verse 75 raised that sky into solemnity. If you can, rise in the hours when stars descend toward dawn and pray. The oath alludes, in one reading, to those hours. Cosmic order and revealed order share an Author. Let the eyes see the one and the heart receive the other.

**Among the Revelation-Section**

This verse belongs to the sūrah's fourth section — the oath by the stars' places, the magnificent weight of the oath, the Noble Qur'an in a concealed Book, touched only by the pure, sent down from the Lord of the worlds, scorned by the mudhinūn who make denial their provision. Al-Ghazālī's parallel of outer and inner purity on verse 79 stands as one of the tradition's great spiritual expansions. Verse 75 participates in the defense and exaltation of the discourse itself. The practical fruit is adab toward the Book and shukr toward provision — the opposite of scorn and the opposite of denial-as-rizq. The ḥadīth of rain and stars (al-Bukhārī 846; Muslim 71) remains the lived tafsīr of attribution.

## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:76

> **And truly it is a magnificent oath, if you but knew.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Certifying the Oath's Weight**

*Wa-innahu la-qasamun law taʿlamūna ʿaẓīm* — "And truly it is a magnificent oath, if you but knew." God Himself certifies the magnitude of what He has sworn by. The oath is *ʿaẓīm* — magnificent, tremendous — the same adjective just applied to the Lord in verse 74. The reality sworn by is proportionate to the Reality who swears.

**Law Taʿlamūn: The Conditional of Ignorance**

"If you but knew" is read by some with a full stop after "oath," making the conditional an implied-response clause: if you but knew, you would affirm (Ibn ʿAjībah, al-Rāzī); if you but knew, you would act on what that knowledge entails (Ibn ʿAjībah); if you but knew, you would strive to attain certainty (al-Rāzī). Human ignorance of the oath's true weight is itself part of the rebuke. The stars' places are more significant than the heedless sky-gazer suspects.

**Knowledge as the Missing Key**

The verse does not say the oath is weak; it says the audience's knowledge is weak. The magnificence is objective. The appreciation is missing. Much of the Qur'an's pedagogy is the attempt to close that gap — to make the knower know what he already lives beneath and within.

**Connection Forward**

The oath introduces what is sworn *to*: the nobility of the Qur'an (v. 77). Stars and revelation are paired. The cosmic order and the revealed order are two books of the same Author. Verse 76 elevates the reader's readiness for the claim that follows.

**If You But Knew**

Human beings live under magnificent realities they do not adequately know — the sky's order, the soul's worth, the Book's rank, the Hour's certainty. Verse 76's *law taʿlamūn* is the sigh of revelation over that gap. Closing the gap is the work of *ʿilm* that leads to *īmān* that leads to *ʿamal*. The oath is already magnificent; the knowledge of its magnificence is what the listener must acquire.

**Magnificent Oath, Thin Knowledge**

The oath is objectively ʿaẓīm; the audience's knowledge is subjectively thin. Verse 76 locates the problem in the knower, not in the known. Education in the Qur'anic sense is closing that gap until the magnificence of what God swears by becomes part of the believer's felt world.

**The Gap in the Knower**

Truly it is a magnificent oath, if you but knew. The magnificence is objective; the knowledge is missing. Verse 76 locates the problem in the audience. If you but knew, you would affirm, act, and strive for certainty. Education in the Qur'anic sense closes this gap until the weight of what God swears by becomes part of the believer's felt world. The oath introduces the nobility of the Qur'an; the knower must rise to the introduction.

**Closing the Knowledge Gap**

The oath is already magnificent; your knowledge of its weight may be thin. Verse 76 locates the work: know. Study the sky as sign, the Book as noble, the Hour as certain, until felt appreciation matches objective weight. If you but knew — then affirm, act, and strive. Convert the conditional into a past tense by learning.

**Among the Revelation-Section**

This verse belongs to the sūrah's fourth section — the oath by the stars' places, the magnificent weight of the oath, the Noble Qur'an in a concealed Book, touched only by the pure, sent down from the Lord of the worlds, scorned by the mudhinūn who make denial their provision. Al-Ghazālī's parallel of outer and inner purity on verse 79 stands as one of the tradition's great spiritual expansions. Verse 76 participates in the defense and exaltation of the discourse itself. The practical fruit is adab toward the Book and shukr toward provision — the opposite of scorn and the opposite of denial-as-rizq. The ḥadīth of rain and stars (al-Bukhārī 846; Muslim 71) remains the lived tafsīr of attribution.

## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:77

> **Truly it is a Noble Quran**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Object of the Oath**

*Innahu la-qurʾānun karīm* — "Truly it is a Noble Quran." The *hu* ("it") can refer to everything mentioned in the sūrah or to the whole Qur'an (al-Rāzī). *Qurʾān* may also be read in its linguistic sense: "a noble recitation." In all readings, what has been sworn to is the revealed Word's nobility.

**Karīm: The Gathering of Praiseworthy Attributes**

*Karīm* means noble, generous, honorable. Al-Rāzī and al-Shawkānī note that it indicates the joining together of all praiseworthy attributes. What is *karīm* is said to be of pure origin and manifest bounty (al-Rāzī). The Qur'an is noble in source (from God), noble in content (truth and guidance), noble in effect (it ennobles those who take it), and noble in form (its Arabic is inimitable).

**The Qur'an's Self-Description**

The Book describes itself with many epithets: light, guidance, criterion, healing, mercy, clear Book, wise Book. *Karīm* is among the most comprehensive. A noble guest is received with honor; a noble book is received with purity of hand and heart — as the following verses will require.

**Against the Accusations**

The Makkan audience had called the revelation poetry, sorcery, and "fables of the ancients." Verse 77 is a counter-claim in a single adjective. It is not fable; it is *karīm*. The oath by the stars' places stands behind the counter-claim with cosmic weight.

**Karīm as a Total Description**

Calling the Qur'an *karīm* is a totalizing act of praise. It is noble in origin, content, form, and effect. A person who treats it as ordinary text — disposable, interruptible, background noise — has not yet heard verse 77. The adjective demands a corresponding adab: ritual purity where required, attentional purity always, and the refusal to scorn what God has called noble.

**Noble Against the Accusations**

Poetry, sorcery, fables of the ancients: the Makkan accusations are met with one adjective, *karīm*. Nobility of the discourse is the counter-claim, backed by the oath of the stars' places. Verse 77 is brief because the adjective is dense. A life of adab toward the Book is the adjective's proper commentary.

**Karīm as Counter-Claim**

Truly it is a Noble Quran. One adjective meets the accusations of poetry, sorcery, and fables. Karīm gathers pure origin, manifest bounty, and all praiseworthy attributes. Noble in source, content, form, and effect. Verse 77 is brief because the adjective is dense. A life of adab toward the Book — purity of hand and heart, refusal of scorn — is the adjective's proper lived commentary.

**Adab Toward the Noble**

Treat the Qur'an as karīm in schedule, posture, attention, and application. Verse 77's adjective demands corresponding adab. Do not make the Noble ordinary by disposable attention. A noble guest is received with honor; a noble Book is received with purity and presence. Live the adjective.

**Among the Revelation-Section**

This verse belongs to the sūrah's fourth section — the oath by the stars' places, the magnificent weight of the oath, the Noble Qur'an in a concealed Book, touched only by the pure, sent down from the Lord of the worlds, scorned by the mudhinūn who make denial their provision. Al-Ghazālī's parallel of outer and inner purity on verse 79 stands as one of the tradition's great spiritual expansions. Verse 77 participates in the defense and exaltation of the discourse itself. The practical fruit is adab toward the Book and shukr toward provision — the opposite of scorn and the opposite of denial-as-rizq. The ḥadīth of rain and stars (al-Bukhārī 846; Muslim 71) remains the lived tafsīr of attribution.

## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:78

> **in a Book concealed.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Preserved Tablet**

*Fī kitābin maknūn* — "in a Book concealed." Most commentators take *kitāb* here as the Preserved Tablet (*al-lawḥ al-maḥfūẓ*, 85:22), which is said to contain all of the books of revelation (Ibn ʿAjībah, al-Qurṭubī, al-Rāzī, al-Shawkānī, al-Ṭabarī). The Qur'an that is recited on earth has a celestial original, guarded and hidden.

**Maknūn: Concealed, Treasured, Protected**

The same word used for the "concealed pearls" of verse 23 returns for the Book. What is *maknūn* is protected from contamination, casual handling, and corruption. That the Qur'an is both *karīm* and *maknūn* indicates that it is preserved from the influence of anyone but God — and thus that it is not, as the Prophet's detractors alleged, the whisperings of jinn or the fables of those of old (6:25; 8:31; 16:24; 23:83; 25:5; 27:68; 46:17; 68:15; 83:13).

**Earthly Muṣḥaf and Heavenly Kitāb**

The relationship between the recited/written Qur'an and the Preserved Tablet is a classical theological topic. The mainstream affirms that the Qur'an is the uncreated Speech of God, written in the Tablet, sent down to the heart of Muhammad ﷺ, recited by tongues, heard by ears, written in volumes (*maṣāḥif*). Verse 78 anchors the earthly book in its heavenly concealment.

**The Ethics of Handling What Is Concealed**

If the original is *maknūn*, the copy deserves reverence. The next verse will draw the practical conclusion for touching. Already here, the adjective teaches an attitude: this is not ordinary text. It is treasured above before it is recited below.

**Concealed and Revealed**

The paradox of the Qur'an is that it is both concealed (*maknūn* in the Tablet) and revealed (*tanzīl* to the Prophet ﷺ and recited openly). Verse 78 holds the concealed pole; verse 80 will hold the revealed pole. Together they say: what you hear in the open has a guarded original above. The openness does not make it common in the sense of cheap; the concealment does not make it inaccessible to the pure.

**Tablet and Text**

The concealed Book anchors the recited Book. What is heard in open air has a guarded original. Verse 78's *maknūn* matches the concealed pearls of verse 23: what is most valuable is protected. The earthly muṣḥaf inherits reverence from the heavenly kitāb.

**Concealed Original**

In a Book concealed. The Preserved Tablet anchors the recited Qur'an. Maknūn matches the concealed pearls of verse 23: what is most valuable is protected. The Qur'an is preserved from the influence of anyone but God — not jinn-whisper, not ancient fable. Verse 78 holds the concealed pole of the paradox; verse 80 holds the revealed pole. Open air recitation has a guarded original above.

**Reverence from the Tablet Down**

Let the concealed original teach reverence for every copy and recitation. Verse 78's maknūn is the heavenly guard that earthly adab echoes. What is treasured above is not cheap below. Handle, recite, and interpret accordingly. The paradox of concealed and revealed is resolved in purity of approach.

**Among the Revelation-Section**

This verse belongs to the sūrah's fourth section — the oath by the stars' places, the magnificent weight of the oath, the Noble Qur'an in a concealed Book, touched only by the pure, sent down from the Lord of the worlds, scorned by the mudhinūn who make denial their provision. Al-Ghazālī's parallel of outer and inner purity on verse 79 stands as one of the tradition's great spiritual expansions. Verse 78 participates in the defense and exaltation of the discourse itself. The practical fruit is adab toward the Book and shukr toward provision — the opposite of scorn and the opposite of denial-as-rizq. The ḥadīth of rain and stars (al-Bukhārī 846; Muslim 71) remains the lived tafsīr of attribution.

## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:79

> **None touch it, save those made pure,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verse of Touch and Purity**

*Lā yamassuhu illā al-muṭahharūn* — "None touch it, save those made pure." The pronoun "it" may refer to the Noble Qur'an (v. 77) or to the Book concealed (v. 78) and thus to the Preserved Tablet. From this fork flow the major interpretations.

**Angels and the Tablet**

If "it" is the Preserved Tablet, then *al-muṭahharūn* — "those made pure" — are the angels (Ibn ʿAjībah, al-Qurṭubī, al-Ṭabarī), or the angels and the prophets. This reading connects to 80:13–14:

> *"In pages honored, exalted and purified."*

**Ritual Purity and the Physical Muṣḥaf**

If "it" is the physical book containing the written Arabic Qur'an (*muṣḥaf*), then many scholars interpret the verse to mean that no one may touch the physical Qur'an unless in a state of ritual purity (*ṭahārah*). There is extensive juristic debate on the details — whether wuduʾ is required, whether the rule is of prohibition or of adab, how translations and digital texts are treated — but the spiritual core is shared: the Book deserves a purified approach.

**Al-Ghazālī on Outer and Inner Purity**

Al-Ghazālī writes in *Iḥyāʾ ʿUlūm al-Dīn* (Kitāb al-Qirāʾah):

> *"Just as the external side of the leather of a Quranic codex and its pages are protected against the external skin of a person who touches it except when he is pure, so also its internal meaning is veiled, by the authority of its greatness and might, from the internal aspect of the reader's mind, except when it is pure from all defilement and is illuminated by the light of glorification and reverence. Just as every hand is not fit to touch the leather of the codex, so too every tongue is not fit to recite its letters, nor every mind fit to understand its meanings."*

Ibn ʿAjībah and the spiritual tradition press the same point: no one will actually understand the allusions of the verses they recite save one whose heart has been purified.

**Purity as Prerequisite of Access**

Verse 79 is therefore both a fiqh ruling (in one reading) and a spiritual law (in all readings). Access to the Book — physical, recitational, hermeneutical — is graded by purity. The purest beings (angels) touch the highest book (the Tablet). The purified human touches the muṣḥaf. The purified heart touches the meaning. The verse is a ladder of adab.

**Adab of the Book**

Whether read as ruling on ritual purity for touching the muṣḥaf or as description of angels touching the Tablet, verse 79 establishes adab as the condition of access. The Book is not common in the sense of cheap. Al-Ghazālī's parallel of outer and inner purity remains the most eloquent expansion: pure hands for pages, pure hearts for meanings. Both purities are trainable. Both are required for full touch.

**Digital Age Questions**

Later fiqh has discussed screens, translations, and audio. The spiritual core of verse 79 survives every medium: approach the discourse of God with purification appropriate to the approach. Wuduʾ before recitation, a cleaned intention before interpretation, a respectful environment for listening — these are living extensions of *lā yamassuhu illā al-muṭahharūn*.

**Access Graded by Purity**

None touch it, save those made pure. Angels touch the Tablet; the ritually pure touch the muṣḥaf; the purified heart touches the meaning. Al-Ghazālī's parallel of outer and inner purity remains the governing expansion. Verse 79 is fiqh and tarbiyah at once. In every medium — parchment, print, screen, audio — the spiritual core survives: approach the discourse of God with purification appropriate to the approach.

**Purifying for Access**

Keep wuduʾ as habit. Clean intention before tafsīr. Respectful environment for listening. Verse 79 grades access by purity at every level — angelic, ritual, spiritual. Al-Ghazālī's outer/inner parallel is the standing commentary. In digital media, the spiritual core survives: purify for the approach you are making.

**Among the Revelation-Section**

This verse belongs to the sūrah's fourth section — the oath by the stars' places, the magnificent weight of the oath, the Noble Qur'an in a concealed Book, touched only by the pure, sent down from the Lord of the worlds, scorned by the mudhinūn who make denial their provision. Al-Ghazālī's parallel of outer and inner purity on verse 79 stands as one of the tradition's great spiritual expansions. Verse 79 participates in the defense and exaltation of the discourse itself. The practical fruit is adab toward the Book and shukr toward provision — the opposite of scorn and the opposite of denial-as-rizq. The ḥadīth of rain and stars (al-Bukhārī 846; Muslim 71) remains the lived tafsīr of attribution.

## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:80

> **a revelation from the Lord of the worlds.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Source Named**

*Tanzīlun min rabbi al-ʿālamīn* — "a revelation from the Lord of the worlds." The verse is repeated at 69:43. Both instances function as direct responses to those who said the Qur'an was fables of the ancients, poetry, or whisperings from Satan and jinn. The source is not the poet's imagination, not the soothsayer's familiar, not the storyteller's repertoire. The source is the Lord of the worlds.

**Tanzīl: Progressive Sending Down**

*Tanzīl* implies gradual, successive sending down — matching the reading of *nujūm* as installments in verse 75. The Qur'an did not fall as a single unbound volume; it descended over twenty-three years, addressing events, questions, and stages of the community's life. That gradualness is itself a mercy and a pedagogical method (25:32).

**Rabb al-ʿĀlamīn: The Fātiḥah's Title Returns**

"Lord of the worlds" is the title taught in the opening of the Fātiḥah. The God whose praise begins the prayer is the God whose revelation fills the Book. Verse 80 closes the identification: the Noble Qur'an, in a concealed Book, untouched except by the pure, is a sending-down from that same Lord.

**Connection to 26:210–211**

> *"And the devils have not brought it down. It is not allowable for them, nor would they be able."* (Qur'an 26:210–211)

Verse 80 and these verses from al-Shuʿarāʾ form a single apologetic front. The revelation's source is divine; the alternative sources proposed by the deniers are impossible and unfit.

**Lord of the Worlds as Source-Citation**

Academic ethics requires source citation. Verse 80 is the Qur'an's own source-citation: *tanzīlun min rabbi al-ʿālamīn*. Any rival source-citation — "from the Prophet's unconscious," "from earlier scriptures loosely recalled," "from the jinn" — is rejected by this line. The Lord of the worlds is not one source among many. He is the Source.

**Source Citation of Revelation**

Tanzīl from the Lord of the worlds is the Qur'an's own source line. Rival attributions — human genius, jinn whisper, recycled myth — are refused. Verse 80 closes the identification chain: noble, concealed, touched only by the pure, sent down from the Rabb of all worlds.

**Source Line**

A revelation from the Lord of the worlds. The Qur'an's own source-citation. Tanzīl implies gradual sending-down, matching the nujūm-as-installments reading of verse 75. Rival sources are refused. Verse 80 and 26:210–211 form a single apologetic front: the satans did not bring it down; it is not allowable for them; they are not able. The Fātiḥah's Rabb al-ʿālamīn is the source of the Book that teaches the Fātiḥah.

**Citing the Source in Your Heart**

When you recite, know the source line: tanzīl from Rabb al-ʿālamīn. Verse 80 refuses rival attributions. Let no interior cynicism rewrite the source as human genius or recycled myth. The Lord of the worlds is speaking. Listen as such. The gradual sending-down was mercy; the Source is one.

**Among the Revelation-Section**

This verse belongs to the sūrah's fourth section — the oath by the stars' places, the magnificent weight of the oath, the Noble Qur'an in a concealed Book, touched only by the pure, sent down from the Lord of the worlds, scorned by the mudhinūn who make denial their provision. Al-Ghazālī's parallel of outer and inner purity on verse 79 stands as one of the tradition's great spiritual expansions. Verse 80 participates in the defense and exaltation of the discourse itself. The practical fruit is adab toward the Book and shukr toward provision — the opposite of scorn and the opposite of denial-as-rizq. The ḥadīth of rain and stars (al-Bukhārī 846; Muslim 71) remains the lived tafsīr of attribution.

---
## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:81

> **Do you then scorn this discourse,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Rebuke of Contempt**

*A-fa-bi-hādhā al-ḥadīthi antum mudhinūn* — "Do you then scorn this discourse?" *Mudhinūn* from *id-hān* implies demeaning, taking lightly, being unctuous or compromising (Ibn ʿAjībah). Al-Qurṭubī notes it can indicate those who appear tractable and compliant in order to hide their disbelief. The verbal form *dahana* is translated "compromise" in 68:9: "They wish that you would soften [in your position], and they would soften [theirs]."

**Hadīth as Discourse**

*Al-ḥadīth* here means this discourse, this recital, this speech — the Qur'an itself. To scorn it after the oath, after the declaration of its nobility, after the naming of its source, is a culpable act of contempt. The question is rhetorical and accusatory: is this your response to such a Book?

**Taking Religion Lightly**

In this sūrah, *mudhinūn* can also indicate those who take religion lightly and are thus willing to deny the revelation secretly while pretending to believe publicly. The hybrid of outward compliance and inward scorn is a known spiritual disease — hypocrisy's cousin. Verse 81 names it and shames it.

**Modern Scorn**

Scorn of the discourse today may take the form of academic dismissal, media mockery, or the polite smile that places the Qur'an among "world scriptures" without ever letting it address the self as command. Verse 81's question remains live: given what this Book claims and what it is, is scorn the proportionate response? Or is the scorn a defense against the demands the Book would make if taken seriously?

**Idhān as Soft Betrayal**

*Mudhinūn* captures a particularly slippery form of rejection — not always loud blasphemy, but the soft scorn that greases itself with politeness, the compromise that smiles while hollowing out commitment. Verse 81 is alert to the unctuous denier as well as the aggressive one. Taking the discourse lightly while pretending to honor it is named and refused.

**Scorn as Disproportion**

Given the oath, the nobility, the concealment, the source — is scorn the proportionate response? Verse 81's question exposes the disproportion of *idhān*. Taking lightly what God has made heavy is a failure of measure. The correct measure is the *tasbīḥ* already commanded and soon commanded again.

**Disproportion of Scorn**

Do you then scorn this discourse? Given the oath, the nobility, the concealment, and the source, scorn is disproportionate. Mudhinūn captures soft scorn, unctuous compromise, taking lightly while pretending to honor. Verse 81 names the slippery rejection as well as the loud. The correct measure of response is the tasbīḥ already commanded. Taking lightly what God has made heavy is a failure of measure.

**Measuring Response to the Discourse**

Check for soft scorn in yourself — polite distance, academic reduction, background-noise treatment of the Book. Verse 81 names mudhinūn. The proportionate response to a discourse sworn by the stars, called noble, concealed in a Tablet, sent from the Lord of the worlds, is not scorn. It is tasbīḥ and obedience. Correct the measure.

**Among the Revelation-Section**

This verse belongs to the sūrah's fourth section — the oath by the stars' places, the magnificent weight of the oath, the Noble Qur'an in a concealed Book, touched only by the pure, sent down from the Lord of the worlds, scorned by the mudhinūn who make denial their provision. Al-Ghazālī's parallel of outer and inner purity on verse 79 stands as one of the tradition's great spiritual expansions. Verse 81 participates in the defense and exaltation of the discourse itself. The practical fruit is adab toward the Book and shukr toward provision — the opposite of scorn and the opposite of denial-as-rizq. The ḥadīth of rain and stars (al-Bukhārī 846; Muslim 71) remains the lived tafsīr of attribution.

## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:82

> **and make the denial thereof your provision?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Rizq of Denial**

*Wa-tajʿalūna rizqakum annakum tukadhdhibūn* — "and make the denial thereof your provision?" The verse is interpreted to mean: do you deny that your provision is from God, when in fact you should thank God for your provision? (Ibn ʿAjībah, al-Qurṭubī). Some early readings or glosses placed "thanks" (*shukr*) in the place of "provision" (*rizq*) — probably an interpretive gloss recorded as if a reading (Ibn Kathīr, al-Qurṭubī, al-Shawkānī, al-Ṭabarī, al-Zamakhsharī).

**The Hadith of Rain and Stars**

According to a ḥadīth, one morning after a downpour the Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"God has said: 'Some of My servants arose as believers in Me and some as disbelievers. He who has said, "We have had a rainfall by the grace and mercy of God," is a believer in Me and a disbeliever in the stars; and he who has said, "We have had a rainfall by the rising of such and such a star," is a disbeliever in Me and a believer in the stars.'"* (al-Bukhārī 846; Muslim 71; cited by Ibn Kathīr, al-Qurṭubī)

Some say this verse was related to that event (al-Shawkānī, al-Ṭabarī). Making denial one's "provision" means feeding on the attribution of gifts to anything but God — stars, nature, luck, the self.

**Provision as a Test of Attribution**

Every provision raises a question: to whom do you attribute it? The companions of the left made denial their food. The believers make thanks their food. Verse 82 asks which diet the listener has chosen. The four proofs of seed, crop, water, and fire all return here as a single demand: when you receive them, do you thank or do you deny?

**Spiritual Nutrition**

The metaphor of denial as *rizq* is precise. What you feed on, you become. A diet of denial produces the body of the left — eventually filled with Zaqqūm. A diet of gratitude produces the body of the right — eventually filled with the fruits of the Garden. Verse 82 is a nutritional label on two possible lives.

**Attribution as Nutrition**

Making denial one's *rizq* means feeding on wrong attribution — "the rain came by the star," "the success came by my genius alone," "the provision came by the system." The ḥadīth of rain and stars (al-Bukhārī 846; Muslim 71) is the practical tafsīr of verse 82. Every provision raises an attribution-question. Answer with thanks, and the provision becomes food of faith. Answer with denial, and the provision becomes food of ruin.

**Shukr as the Alternative Diet**

The interpretive gloss that places *shukr* where *rizq* stands points to the intended alternative. The diet of the believer is gratitude. Gratitude attributes gifts to God and uses them under His law. Verse 82 asks which diet you have been eating. The body of the left is built by one diet; the body of the right by the other.

**Diet of Attribution**

And make the denial thereof your provision? Feeding on wrong attribution — rain by the star, success by the self alone — is the diet of denial. The ḥadīth of rain and stars (al-Bukhārī 846; Muslim 71) is the practical tafsīr. Every provision raises an attribution-question. Answer with shukr, and provision becomes food of faith. Answer with takdhīb, and provision becomes food of ruin. Verse 82 is a nutritional label on two possible lives.

**Attribution Diet Audit**

Audit your attributions for a week. Rain, success, provision, skill — to whom do you assign them in casual speech and interior monologue? Verse 82 warns against denial as rizq. Feed on shukr instead. The ḥadīth of rain and stars is your practical guide. Every provision is a test of attribution. Pass it with thanks.

**Among the Revelation-Section**

This verse belongs to the sūrah's fourth section — the oath by the stars' places, the magnificent weight of the oath, the Noble Qur'an in a concealed Book, touched only by the pure, sent down from the Lord of the worlds, scorned by the mudhinūn who make denial their provision. Al-Ghazālī's parallel of outer and inner purity on verse 79 stands as one of the tradition's great spiritual expansions. Verse 82 participates in the defense and exaltation of the discourse itself. The practical fruit is adab toward the Book and shukr toward provision — the opposite of scorn and the opposite of denial-as-rizq. The ḥadīth of rain and stars (al-Bukhārī 846; Muslim 71) remains the lived tafsīr of attribution.

## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:83

> **Why, then, when it reaches the throat,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Opening of the Fifth Section: Death**

*Fa-law-lā idhā balaghati al-ḥulqūm* — "Why, then, when it reaches the throat..." The sūrah's final section begins at the deathbed. *Al-ḥulqūm* is the throat. "It" is the soul at the moment of departure. The same scene appears in Sūrah al-Qiyāmah:

> *"Nay! When it reaches the collarbones, and it is said, 'Who will [make him] ascend?' And he knows for certain that it is the parting."* (Qur'an 75:26–28)

**The Moment That Ends All Theory**

All the debates about resurrection, all the scorn of the discourse, all the luxury and the persistence in sin, arrive at this moment: the soul at the throat. The dying person becomes the argument. Whatever he denied about the unseen is now, for him, no longer unseen. Verse 83 places the reader at that bedside in advance, while there is still time to change what the bedside will mean.

**Why, Then: The Logic of Helplessness**

*Law-lā* here introduces a challenge that will be completed in verses 86–87: if you are truly independent of God, why can you not stop what happens when the soul reaches the throat? The deathbed is the laboratory in which human claims of autonomy are experimentally falsified.

**The Sūrah's Return to the Body**

Al-Wāqiʿah began with the cosmos shaking. It ends with a single throat. The scale collapses from the universal to the intimate, because every universal claim of the sūrah is decided, for each person, at this intimate point. You will not watch the mountains turn to dust as a spectator only; you will feel the soul at the throat as the protagonist.

**The Throat as the Global Threshold**

Every human story, however long, narrows to a throat. Verse 83 is the most intimate memento mori in a sūrah full of cosmic ones. The mountains' powder and the soul's departure are two scales of the same Event. Preparing for the first without preparing for the second is a strange inconsistency. The sūrah will not allow it. Look at the throat now, while yours still answers you freely.

**Memento Mori in One Throat**

The cosmic images of the sūrah's opening collapse, at verse 83, into a single throat. Every human story narrows here. The verse is the most intimate memento mori of the chapter. Prepare for mountains of powder and for the soul at the ḥulqūm — they are one Event at two scales.

**Throat as Threshold of All Claims**

Why, then, when it reaches the throat... The fifth section begins at the deathbed. All debates about resurrection, all scorn of the discourse, arrive here. The dying person becomes the argument. Verse 83 places the reader at the bedside in advance. The cosmic powder of mountains and the intimate departure of the soul are one Event at two scales. Prepare for both.

**Visiting the Throat in Advance**

Visit a dying person if you can, or sit in khalwah and imagine the soul at the ḥulqūm. Verse 83 is memento mori at the most intimate scale. The cosmic images and the throat are one Event. Preparation for both is one preparation: faith, repentance, glorification, and the refusal to scorn the discourse that warned you.

**Among the Deathbed and the Final Certification**

This verse belongs to the sūrah's fifth section — the soul at the throat, the watchers who cannot return it, God nearer than the watchers, the threefold welcome in the singular (rawḥ and rayḥān; salām; ḥamīm and jaḥīm), the certification as ḥaqq al-yaqīn, and the final command to glorify. Ibn ʿAjībah and others read the welcomes as including the barzakh; the mainstream holds both intermediate and final stages. Verse 83 is part of the sūrah's most intimate pedagogy. Death is undergone in the first person; the conditionals of 88–94 are mirrors; Qatādah's distinction on certainty (beneficial now or convicting later) governs the response. End in tasbīḥ. The Event is certain. Glorify.

---
## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:84

> **while you are looking on**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Witnesses of Death**

*Wa-antum ḥīnaʾidhin tanẓurūn* — "while you are looking on." The address is to those present at the death — family, friends, attendants — who watch the soul depart and have no power to make it stay (Ibn Kathīr, al-Ṭabarī). The most love-filled hands on earth cannot grip the soul and hold it in the body.

**The Unique Perspective of Witnessing Death**

Verses 83–85 point to the unique perspective that death imparts both to the dying and to those witnessing. Suddenly the connection to this world is revealed as ephemeral, and God is revealed as ever near. Upon witnessing death, one is reminded:

> *"Indeed we belong to Allah, and indeed to Him we will return."* (Qur'an 2:156)

**Looking Without Being Able to Act**

The particular pain of *tanẓurūn* is the pain of sight without agency. You see the struggle. You cannot end it. You cannot reverse it. Modern medicine has extended the time of this looking and multiplied its technical apparatus, but it has not abolished the structure verse 84 names. At the final threshold, the lookers are still lookers, not controllers.

**Pastoral Implication**

Being present at a death is a Qur'anic education. The community that shields itself from dying bodies — outsourcing death to institutions, avoiding funerals, hiding the fact of mortality from children — deprives itself of one of revelation's most effective teachers. Verse 84 assumes that people *are* looking on. The assumption is itself a quiet command: do not look away from what God uses to wake hearts.

**The Ministry of Presence**

Those who look on at death have a ministry: presence, prayer, gentle reminder of the shahādah, recitation, and the refusal to fill the room with panic or worldly chatter. Verse 84 names their looking; the Sunnah fills that looking with recommended acts. The watchers cannot return the soul (v. 87), but they can accompany it with *salām* and dhikr. That accompaniment is the last earthly gift.

**Sight Without Power**

Looking on without being able to return the soul is the specific helplessness of the deathbed witness. Verse 84 names it. Modern medicine extends the looking; it does not abolish the structure. The ministry of the watcher is presence, prayer, and reminder — not control. Accepting the limit is part of faith.

**Watchers Without Power**

While you are looking on. Family and attendants see the soul depart and cannot hold it. Sight without agency is the specific pain of the deathbed witness. Verse 84 names it. The ministry of presence — prayer, gentle shahādah reminder, recitation of Yā Sīn and al-Wāqiʿah — is what remains when control is removed. Do not look away from what God uses to wake hearts. Do not fill the room with worldly chatter at the hour of the throat.

**Ministry of the Watcher**

Learn the adab of the deathbed: presence, prayer, gentle reminder, recitation, minimal worldly talk. Verse 84 names the looking; the Sunnah fills it with acts. You cannot return the soul. You can accompany it. Do not outsource all dying to institutions in a way that deprives the community of this teacher. Look. Learn. Accompany.

**Among the Deathbed and the Final Certification**

This verse belongs to the sūrah's fifth section — the soul at the throat, the watchers who cannot return it, God nearer than the watchers, the threefold welcome in the singular (rawḥ and rayḥān; salām; ḥamīm and jaḥīm), the certification as ḥaqq al-yaqīn, and the final command to glorify. Ibn ʿAjībah and others read the welcomes as including the barzakh; the mainstream holds both intermediate and final stages. Verse 84 is part of the sūrah's most intimate pedagogy. Death is undergone in the first person; the conditionals of 88–94 are mirrors; Qatādah's distinction on certainty (beneficial now or convicting later) governs the response. End in tasbīḥ. The Event is certain. Glorify.

## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:85

> **—and We are nearer to him than you, though you see not—**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Divine Nearness at the Moment of Death**

*Wa-naḥnu aqrabu ilayhi minkum wa-lākin lā tubṣirūn* — "and We are nearer to him than you, though you see not." The watchers stand inches from the dying body. God is nearer than they are. The nearness is not spatial in the corporeal sense; it is the nearness of knowledge, power, and the agency that actually takes the soul.

**Nearer Than the Jugular**

> *"And We have already created man and know what his soul whispers to him, and We are closer to him than [his] jugular vein."* (Qur'an 50:16)

Verse 85 is the deathbed application of 50:16. The nearness that was always true becomes, at the throat, impossible to ignore for the dying, and is announced to the living watchers who still "see not."

**The Angels of Death**

The manner of God's taking the soul is described in 6:61–62:

> *"And He is Dominant over His servants. He sends guardians over you, until, when death comes to one of you, Our messengers take him, and they do not fail [in their duties]. Then they are returned to Allah, their true Master. Unquestionably, to Him belongs the judgement, and He is the swiftest of accountants."*

God's nearness operates through the angelic messengers who do not neglect their duty. The watchers see the body; they do not see the messengers. *Lā tubṣirūn* — you see not.

**Comfort and Warning Together**

For the believer dying, the verse is comfort: the One nearer than the loved ones at the bedside is the One who promised mercy to those who believed. For the denier dying, the verse is warning: the One nearer than the bedside is the One whose discourse was scorned. For the watchers, the verse is both: you are not the most important presence in this room. Act accordingly — with prayer, with gentle reminder, with the recitation of Yā Sīn and al-Wāqiʿah that the tradition has loved at such hours.

**Unseen Nearness**

The watchers see the body and each other. They do not see the nearness of God or the presence of the angelic messengers. *Lā tubṣirūn* is the epistemic limit of the deathbed's human participants. Faith is the faculty that accepts the nearness without seeing it. Verse 85 is 50:16 applied to the hour when seeing fails and knowing must carry the day.

**Comfort for the Believing Dying**

For the believer, "We are nearer to him than you" is comfort beyond the comfort of family. The loved ones are there; the Loved One is nearer. The tradition of reciting Yā Sīn and al-Wāqiʿah at the deathbed is an attempt to fill the room with words that match that nearness. Verse 85 authorizes the sense that the room is more crowded with mercy than eyes report.

**Nearer Than the Watchers**

We are nearer to him than you, though you see not. The watchers stand inches away; God is nearer. 50:16's jugular-nearness applied to the deathbed. Angelic messengers take the soul and do not fail (6:61–62). Lā tubṣirūn — you see not — is the epistemic limit of human participants. For the believing dying, the verse is comfort: the Loved One is nearer than the loved ones. For the denier, it is warning. For the watchers, it is both.

**Trusting Unseen Nearness**

At every future bedside, remember: God is nearer than the watchers. Verse 85 comforts the believing dying and sobers the watchers. Fill the room with words that match the nearness — Qur'an, dhikr, salām. Faith accepts what eyes do not see. The jugular-nearness of 50:16 becomes deathbed-nearness here. Rely on it.

**Among the Deathbed and the Final Certification**

This verse belongs to the sūrah's fifth section — the soul at the throat, the watchers who cannot return it, God nearer than the watchers, the threefold welcome in the singular (rawḥ and rayḥān; salām; ḥamīm and jaḥīm), the certification as ḥaqq al-yaqīn, and the final command to glorify. Ibn ʿAjībah and others read the welcomes as including the barzakh; the mainstream holds both intermediate and final stages. Verse 85 is part of the sūrah's most intimate pedagogy. Death is undergone in the first person; the conditionals of 88–94 are mirrors; Qatādah's distinction on certainty (beneficial now or convicting later) governs the response. End in tasbīḥ. The Event is certain. Glorify.
