## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:86

> **why, then, if you are unconstrained,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Challenge to Claimed Autonomy**

*Fa-law-lā in kuntum ghayra madīnīn* — "why, then, if you are unconstrained..." *Ghayr madīnīn* means not under obligation, not indebted, not subject to judgment — free of the debt of the soul to God. The verse hypothetically grants the deniers' claim: suppose you are truly independent, not owned, not indebted, not under *dīn* (debt/judgment/religion). Then complete the challenge in the next verse.

**Madīnīn: Debt, Judgment, Religion**

The root *d-y-n* gives *dīn* (religion, recompense), *dayn* (debt), and *madīn* (one subject to judgment or indebted). All these senses hover. If you are not indebted to God for your soul, if you are not subject to His judgment, if you owe no *dīn* — then prove it at the throat.

**The Structure of the Argument**

Verses 86–87 complete the *law-lā* begun conceptually at verse 83. The logic is: if your anthropology of independence were true, you would be able to return the soul when it reaches the throat. You cannot. Therefore your anthropology is false. The deathbed is the experimental disproof of human self-sufficiency.

**The Fiction of the Unconstrained Self**

Modern culture often celebrates the unconstrained self — free of debt to history, community, or God. Verse 86 names that fiction and prepares its refutation. *Ghayr madīnīn* is the anthropology of the denier: I owe nothing; I am not under judgment; I am not a subject of *dīn*. The deathbed is where this anthropology goes to die, because the self that claimed freedom cannot free the soul from the throat.

**If You Are Truly Independent**

The hypothetical grants the denier's anthropology for the sake of argument: suppose you are unconstrained, un-indebted, not under dīn. Then prove it when the soul reaches the throat. Verse 86 sets up the falsification. The next verse runs the experiment that has never favored the denier.

**Hypothetical of Independence**

Why, then, if you are unconstrained... Ghayr madīnīn: not indebted, not under judgment, not subject to dīn. The verse grants the denier's anthropology hypothetically and prepares its falsification. The modern cult of the unconstrained self stands inside this hypothesis. Verse 86 sets the terms. The next verse runs the experiment at the throat — an experiment that has never favored the claim of independence.

**Dropping the Fiction of Non-Debt**

You are madīn — indebted, under dīn, subject to judgment. Verse 86's hypothesis of ghayr madīnīn is granted only to be falsified. Drop the fiction of the unconstrained self. Own the debt of the soul to God. The deathbed will enforce the ownership; better to confess it now in worship than then in helplessness.

**Among the Deathbed and the Final Certification**

This verse belongs to the sūrah's fifth section — the soul at the throat, the watchers who cannot return it, God nearer than the watchers, the threefold welcome in the singular (rawḥ and rayḥān; salām; ḥamīm and jaḥīm), the certification as ḥaqq al-yaqīn, and the final command to glorify. Ibn ʿAjībah and others read the welcomes as including the barzakh; the mainstream holds both intermediate and final stages. Verse 86 is part of the sūrah's most intimate pedagogy. Death is undergone in the first person; the conditionals of 88–94 are mirrors; Qatādah's distinction on certainty (beneficial now or convicting later) governs the response. End in tasbīḥ. The Event is certain. Glorify.

## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:87

> **do you not return it, if you are truthful?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Unanswerable Challenge**

*Tarjiʿūnahā in kuntum ṣādiqīn* — "do you not return it, if you are truthful?" "It" is the soul. Return it to the body. Push it back from the throat. Resume the life. If you are truthful in your claim of independence and your denial of the Creator's ownership, this should be within your power. It is not. The challenge ends the debate.

**Ṣādiqīn: Truthfulness as the Stakes**

"If you are truthful" places the entire structure of denial under the criterion of truthfulness. The deniers have made claims — about creation, about resurrection, about the discourse of revelation. The deathbed asks for a practical demonstration. Failure to return the soul is failure of the claim.

**The Humbling of Medicine and Love**

Every physician and every loving family member has stood inside this verse. Everything possible is done. The soul is not returned by them. When it returns, after a crisis, the believer says *al-ḥamdu lillāh* and recognizes a reprieve, not a human conquest. Verse 87 does not forbid medicine; the Prophet ﷺ sought treatment and taught that God created both disease and cure. It forbids the metaphysics that treats medicine as ultimate and God as optional.

**Preparation for the Three-Fold Return**

Having established human helplessness at death and divine nearness at death, the sūrah is ready to return to the three categories — those brought nigh, the companions of the right, and the deniers who are astray — and to describe what each meets at this threshold. Verses 88–94 are the deathbed application of verses 7–56.

**Experimental Disproof of Autonomy**

The challenge to return the soul is an experiment that has been run billions of times with the same result. Human autonomy is real within limits and unreal at the limit that matters most. Verse 87 is the laboratory note. Medicine, love, wealth, and status all fail the challenge. The truthful conclusion is the confession of ownership: we belong to God, and to Him we return.

**Medicine Within Metaphysics**

The verse does not forbid medicine. The Prophet ﷺ sought treatment, taught that God created disease and cure, and allowed the sick to be cared for. What it forbids is the metaphysics that stops at the physician. When the soul returns after a crisis, the believer thanks God first and the means second. When the soul does not return, the believer does not conclude that the means were the real gods and failed; he concludes that the Owner has taken what was His.

**Return It If You Are Truthful**

Do you not return it, if you are truthful? The unanswerable challenge. Medicine, love, wealth, and status fail it. The verse does not forbid medicine; it forbids metaphysics that stop at the physician. When the soul returns after crisis, thank God first. When it does not, do not conclude that the means were gods and failed; conclude that the Owner has taken what was His. Verse 87 ends the debate of autonomy and opens the threefold welcome at the threshold.

**Medicine Under Metaphysics**

Seek treatment. Care for the sick. Then place medicine inside metaphysics, not above it. Verse 87's challenge stands after every round of CPR and every failed round. When the soul returns, al-ḥamdu lillāh first. When it does not, the Owner has taken His own. Truthfulness is the stakes. Be truthful about who holds the soul.

**Among the Deathbed and the Final Certification**

This verse belongs to the sūrah's fifth section — the soul at the throat, the watchers who cannot return it, God nearer than the watchers, the threefold welcome in the singular (rawḥ and rayḥān; salām; ḥamīm and jaḥīm), the certification as ḥaqq al-yaqīn, and the final command to glorify. Ibn ʿAjībah and others read the welcomes as including the barzakh; the mainstream holds both intermediate and final stages. Verse 87 is part of the sūrah's most intimate pedagogy. Death is undergone in the first person; the conditionals of 88–94 are mirrors; Qatādah's distinction on certainty (beneficial now or convicting later) governs the response. End in tasbīḥ. The Event is certain. Glorify.

## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:88

> **So if one be among those brought nigh,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Three-Fold Map at the Threshold**

*Fa-ammā in kāna min al-muqarrabīn* — "So if one be among those brought nigh..." The final section returns to the categories of the sūrah's first half. Each reference is now in the singular, as if referring to the soul of the dying person of verses 83–87, while still describing what confronts all souls. The foremost, who were "the ones brought nigh" in verse 11, reappear at the moment of death.

**Barzakh and the Two Resurrections**

Some commentators, including Ibn ʿAjībah, read verses 88–94 as describing the intermediate realm (*barzakh*) experienced after the lesser resurrection in the grave and before the greater resurrection of the Day of Judgment. The soul's first sorting occurs at death and in the grave; the final public sorting occurs on the Day. Al-Wāqiʿah's threefold map applies to both thresholds.

**The Singular Soul**

By shifting to "if one be" (*in kāna*), the sūrah personalizes what had been collective portraits. The reader can no longer think only in groups. He must ask: if *I* am among... The pastoral force of the singular is greater than the plural. Death is always undergone in the first person.

**Those Brought Nigh at the Hour of Departure**

The *muqarrabūn* leave the world under a particular set of signs, which the next verse will name: comfort, bounty, and a Garden of bliss. The nearness that defined their rank in life becomes the quality of their departure. They do not leave as orphans of meaning; they leave as guests already welcomed.

**Personalizing the Map**

The shift from plural groups to singular soul is the sūrah's final pedagogical masterstroke. You have seen the three companies. Now: if *you* are among the brought nigh... The conditional *in kāna* is a mirror. The dying person of verses 83–87 is every reader in prospect. Which welcome is yours? The answer is being written now.

**Singular Soul at the Map's Return**

The threefold map returns in the singular. You are not only a member of a demographic; you are a soul who will be one of three. Verse 88 begins the deathbed application of the sūrah's central taxonomy. The conditional 'if one be' is a mirror held to every reader.

**Map in the Singular**

So if one be among those brought nigh... The threefold map returns as singular conditionals. Death is undergone in the first person. Barzakh and the two resurrections both receive this sorting. Verse 88 personalizes what had been demographic. The mirror: if you are among... The answer is being written in the life that precedes the throat. The muqarrabūn leave under a particular welcome the next verse names.

**Answering the Conditional Now**

If one be among those brought nigh — start answering that conditional in today's choices. Verse 88's singular map is a mirror. Nearness is built by farāʾiḍ and nawāfil, by love and initiative, by the race of verse 10. Do not wait for the throat to discover which conditional is yours. Write the answer now.

**Among the Deathbed and the Final Certification**

This verse belongs to the sūrah's fifth section — the soul at the throat, the watchers who cannot return it, God nearer than the watchers, the threefold welcome in the singular (rawḥ and rayḥān; salām; ḥamīm and jaḥīm), the certification as ḥaqq al-yaqīn, and the final command to glorify. Ibn ʿAjībah and others read the welcomes as including the barzakh; the mainstream holds both intermediate and final stages. Verse 88 is part of the sūrah's most intimate pedagogy. Death is undergone in the first person; the conditionals of 88–94 are mirrors; Qatādah's distinction on certainty (beneficial now or convicting later) governs the response. End in tasbīḥ. The Event is certain. Glorify.

## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:89

> **then comfort, bounty, and a Garden of bliss.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Three Gifts at Departure**

*Fa-rawḥun wa-rayḥānun wa-jannatu naʿīm* — "then comfort, bounty, and a Garden of bliss." Three words name what the one brought nigh receives. *Rawḥ* means rest, relief, a cooling breath of mercy. *Rayḥān* means fragrant herb, sweet basil, bounty, provision. *Jannat naʿīm* is the Garden of bliss already named in verse 12. The sequence moves from the immediate relief of death, through the fragrance of the transition, to the settled home of the Garden.

**Rawḥ as Mercy and Breath**

*Rawḥ* is interpreted as an allusion to being in God's mercy (*raḥmah*), a cooling breeze (*nasīm*), or the direct vision of God's Face (al-Qurṭubī and others). The one brought nigh is relieved of the cares of this world (Ibn ʿAjībah, al-Qurṭubī, al-Shawkānī, al-Ṭabarī). The tightness of the throat-moment opens into a breath.

**Rayḥān as Fragrance and Provision**

*Rayḥān* is also the name for sweet basil and is rendered "fragrant herbs" in 55:12 in a worldly sense. Here, some say it refers to the basil of this earth as a sign (al-Thaʿlabī); others say it refers to the scent of Paradise, a perfume that fills the souls of those brought nigh (Ibn ʿAjībah, Ibn Kathīr). A saying attributed to Abū al-ʿĀliyah (d. 90/708):

> *"None among those brought nigh departs this world until they have been given some of the rayḥān of the Garden; so he smells it, and is then taken."* (cited by Ibn ʿAjībah, Ibn Kathīr, al-Thaʿlabī)

Others take *rayḥān* as provision in Heaven or as Heaven itself, or read *rawḥ* as salvation from the Fire and *rayḥān* as entry into the Everlasting Abode, or *rawḥ* as the grave's comfort and *rayḥān* as the Garden (al-Thaʿlabī).

**The Full Triad**

Comfort for the moment of death, fragrance for the passage, Garden for the destination. The *muqarrab* is accompanied at every stage. No gap of abandonment appears between the throat and the Garden. Verse 89 is the welcome that matches the nearness.

**The Triad of Departure**

*Rawḥ*, *rayḥān*, *jannat naʿīm* — breath/relief, fragrance/bounty, Garden of bliss. The triad covers the moment, the passage, and the destination. No gap of abandonment appears for the *muqarrab*. Abū al-ʿĀliyah's report that the brought nigh are given *rayḥān* of the Garden to smell before they are taken is one of the tradition's tenderest images. Death, for them, begins with a scent of home.

**Dying into Nearness**

The foremost do not fall into death; they are received through it. Verse 89 is the reception protocol. The same nearness that defined their life defines their exit. Those who lived as *muqarrabūn* in aspiration die as *muqarrabūn* in welcome. The continuity is the mercy.

**Reception Protocol of the Near**

Then comfort, bounty, and a Garden of bliss. Rawḥ as relief, mercy-breeze, or vision; rayḥān as fragrance of Paradise, provision, or sweet basil of the Garden; jannat naʿīm as the settled home. Abū al-ʿĀliyah's report: the brought nigh are given rayḥān of the Garden to smell, then taken. Death begins with a scent of home. Verse 89 is the reception protocol of nearness — no gap of abandonment between throat and Garden.

**Hoping for the Scent of Home**

Ask God for the rawḥ and rayḥān of the muqarrabūn. Verse 89's triad — relief, fragrance, Garden — is the reception protocol of nearness. Live toward it. Abū al-ʿĀliyah's scent of the Garden before taking is a tender image to hold in duʿāʾ. Death, for the brought nigh, begins with home's perfume. Aspire to that beginning.

**Among the Deathbed and the Final Certification**

This verse belongs to the sūrah's fifth section — the soul at the throat, the watchers who cannot return it, God nearer than the watchers, the threefold welcome in the singular (rawḥ and rayḥān; salām; ḥamīm and jaḥīm), the certification as ḥaqq al-yaqīn, and the final command to glorify. Ibn ʿAjībah and others read the welcomes as including the barzakh; the mainstream holds both intermediate and final stages. Verse 89 is part of the sūrah's most intimate pedagogy. Death is undergone in the first person; the conditionals of 88–94 are mirrors; Qatādah's distinction on certainty (beneficial now or convicting later) governs the response. End in tasbīḥ. The Event is certain. Glorify.

## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:90

> **And if one be among the companions of the right,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Second Singular**

*Wa-ammā in kāna min aṣḥābi al-yamīn* — "And if one be among the companions of the right..." The broad company of the saved is now addressed at the threshold of death. What the foremost receive as comfort, bounty, and Garden, the companions of the right receive in the form of peace — as the next verse states.

**Different Gifts, Same Salvation**

The sūrah continues to distinguish ranks without expelling the lower rank of the saved from mercy. The right hand does not receive less than salvation; it receives salvation in the mode appropriate to it. Peace (*salām*) is no small gift. It is the greeting of Paradise itself.

**Hope for the Ordinary Believer at Death**

Many believers die without having achieved the rank of the foremost. Verse 90 exists for them. If one be among the companions of the right — and the throng of the right is many from later times (v. 40) — then what follows is peace. The deathbed of the ordinary righteous is not a lesser panic; it is a doorway marked with *salām*.

**The Wide Gate**

Verse 90 is a wide gate in a sūrah that also has a narrow peak (the foremost). Both are needed. The wide gate means that ordinary faith, ordinary prayer, ordinary repentance, ordinary honesty in trade and speech — these are not nothing. They place a person, God willing, among the companions of the right. The narrow peak means that ordinary faith should not become an excuse to stop aspiring. Enter by the wide gate; climb toward the peak.

**Wide Gate Named Again**

The companions of the right at the threshold receive the wide gate's welcome. Ordinary faith is not nothing. Verse 90 exists so that sincere believers who are not foremost do not despair at death. The throng of the right includes later times (v. 40). The gate is still open. Enter.

**Wide Gate at the Threshold**

And if one be among the companions of the right... Ordinary faith is not nothing. Verse 90 exists so that sincere believers who are not foremost do not despair at death. The throng of the right includes many from later times. The wide gate's welcome is peace. Enter by the wide gate; aspire still toward the peak. Both movements are Qur'anic.

**Entering the Wide Gate Without Stopping**

Enter the wide gate of the right hand by faith, prayer, repentance, and honesty. Verse 90 names the gate at the threshold. Do not despair if you are not foremost; do not stop aspiring toward the foremost. Wide gate and high peak are both Qur'anic. Ordinary faith is not nothing. Make it something that holds at the throat.

**Among the Deathbed and the Final Certification**

This verse belongs to the sūrah's fifth section — the soul at the throat, the watchers who cannot return it, God nearer than the watchers, the threefold welcome in the singular (rawḥ and rayḥān; salām; ḥamīm and jaḥīm), the certification as ḥaqq al-yaqīn, and the final command to glorify. Ibn ʿAjībah and others read the welcomes as including the barzakh; the mainstream holds both intermediate and final stages. Verse 90 is part of the sūrah's most intimate pedagogy. Death is undergone in the first person; the conditionals of 88–94 are mirrors; Qatādah's distinction on certainty (beneficial now or convicting later) governs the response. End in tasbīḥ. The Event is certain. Glorify.

## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:91

> **then peace unto thee from the companions of the right.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Greeting of Peace**

*Fa-salāmun laka min aṣḥābi al-yamīn* — "then peace unto thee from the companions of the right." After the questioning of the grave (as described in the ḥadīth literature), the one who is among the companions of the right is greeted with salutations of peace from the companions of the right (Ibn ʿAjībah) or from the angels (Ibn Kathīr).

**Angelical Descent**

> *"Indeed, those who have said, 'Our Lord is Allah,' and then remained steadfast — the angels will descend upon them, [saying], 'Do not fear and do not grieve but receive good tidings of Paradise, which you were promised. We were your allies in the worldly life and in the Hereafter. And you will have therein whatever your souls desire, and you will have therein whatever you request.'"* (Qur'an 41:30–31)

Ibn Kathīr connects this descent to the peace of verse 91. The angels who were unseen allies become audible greeters.

**Safety from Harm**

Al-Qurṭubī and others interpret the peace as a promise of safety from any and all harm. *Salām* as soundness, security, freedom from defect — the full range of the word — is conferred upon the dying believer of the right hand.

**The Shift to 'Thee'**

The second-person singular *laka* ("unto thee") is read by some as the companions of the right giving salutations to the Prophet ﷺ: "Peace unto thee, O Muhammad" (Ibn ʿAjībah, al-Qurṭubī, al-Thaʿlabī). Alternately, it is an address to the individual who hears or reads the verse. Both readings can be held: the Prophet is the first of the right hand's company, and every believer is addressed through him.

**Rehearsing the Greeting**

Every *al-salāmu ʿalaykum* in this world is a practice for verse 91. The believer who greets with presence of heart is already speaking the language of the deathbed's welcome.

**Peace as the Right Hand's Currency**

The foremost receive *rawḥ* and *rayḥān* and Garden; the right receive *salām*. Peace is the currency of their welcome. In a world of conflict, anxiety, and noisy death, the promise of peace at the threshold is almost beyond imagination. Verse 91 asks the believer to imagine it anyway — and to practice peace-making now, so that the greeting of the right hand is continuous with the life that preceded it.

**Peace unto Thee**

Then peace unto thee from the companions of the right. Angelic greeting, company greeting, promise of safety from harm — the readings converge on salām as the right hand's currency at the threshold. 41:30–31's angelic descent is the cousin-text. The shift to laka addresses the Prophet and every reader through him. Every al-salāmu ʿalaykum in this world practices verse 91. Mean it, and the greeting becomes continuous with the deathbed's welcome.

**Practicing the Currency of Peace**

Make peace — in homes, in communities, in the self's relation to God. Verse 91's salām is the right hand's currency at death. Continuity between life and welcome is the mercy. Greet with meaning. Reconcile before sleep when possible. The angels' descent of 41:30–31 is for those who said 'Our Lord is God' and stood firm. Stand firm.

**Among the Deathbed and the Final Certification**

This verse belongs to the sūrah's fifth section — the soul at the throat, the watchers who cannot return it, God nearer than the watchers, the threefold welcome in the singular (rawḥ and rayḥān; salām; ḥamīm and jaḥīm), the certification as ḥaqq al-yaqīn, and the final command to glorify. Ibn ʿAjībah and others read the welcomes as including the barzakh; the mainstream holds both intermediate and final stages. Verse 91 is part of the sūrah's most intimate pedagogy. Death is undergone in the first person; the conditionals of 88–94 are mirrors; Qatādah's distinction on certainty (beneficial now or convicting later) governs the response. End in tasbīḥ. The Event is certain. Glorify.

## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:92

> **But if one be among the deniers who are astray,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Third Singular: The Left at the Threshold**

*Wa-ammā in kāna min al-mukadhdhibīna al-ḍāllīn* — "But if one be among the deniers who are astray..." The double name of verse 51 returns in the singular. The soul that spent a life in *takdhīb* and *ḍalāl* meets the threshold under that identity. The sorting is continuous with the life.

**No Third Destiny Beyond the Three**

The sūrah does not offer a fourth category at death. The foremost, the right, the denying astray — these three exhaust the map. The reader who has delayed choosing is informed that delay is itself a trajectory, and trajectories arrive.

**The Gravity of Dying in Denial**

Islamic theology holds that repentance is accepted until the death rattle, but that once the soul reaches the throat in the final way, the gate of *tawbah* closes for that life. Verse 92 is the description of the one who arrives at that gate still in denial. The following verses name his welcome.

**Dying as One Lived**

The third singular category is not a surprise sprung on an innocent soul. It is the continuation of a life of *takdhīb* and *ḍalāl*. Verse 92 teaches the terrifying consistency of the self: one dies as one lived, unless mercy and repentance have intervened. The time for intervention is before the throat. The verse is severe because the stakes are severe, and because the severity still arrives while some readers have time to leave the category.

**Consistency of the Self**

One dies as one lived, unless mercy and repentance intervened. Verse 92's third singular is not a surprise; it is a continuity. The denying astray arrive at the throat still in character. The time to break the character is before. The verse is severe because the continuity is real.

**Third Singular Continuity**

But if one be among the deniers who are astray... The double name returns in the singular. One dies as one lived, unless mercy and repentance intervened. Verse 92 is continuity, not surprise. The gate of tawbah is open until the final throat; verse 92 describes the one who arrives still in denial. The following verses name his welcome. Severity here is still invitation for the reader who has not yet arrived.

**Intervening Before Continuity Hardens**

If denial and straying are present in a life you can still touch — your own or another's — intervene before the continuity of verse 92 hardens at the throat. Tawbah is the break in continuity. The third singular is not surprise but trajectory completed. Change trajectory while change is the work of choice. The severity of the verse is still invitation.

**Among the Deathbed and the Final Certification**

This verse belongs to the sūrah's fifth section — the soul at the throat, the watchers who cannot return it, God nearer than the watchers, the threefold welcome in the singular (rawḥ and rayḥān; salām; ḥamīm and jaḥīm), the certification as ḥaqq al-yaqīn, and the final command to glorify. Ibn ʿAjībah and others read the welcomes as including the barzakh; the mainstream holds both intermediate and final stages. Verse 92 is part of the sūrah's most intimate pedagogy. Death is undergone in the first person; the conditionals of 88–94 are mirrors; Qatādah's distinction on certainty (beneficial now or convicting later) governs the response. End in tasbīḥ. The Event is certain. Glorify.

## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:93

> **then a welcome of boiling liquid,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Nuzul of Ḥamīm**

*Fa-nuzulun min ḥamīm* — "then a welcome of boiling liquid." The ironic *nuzul* of verse 56 returns. The denier who is astray is "welcomed" with the boiling liquid that has been the drink of the left throughout the sūrah. The hospitality of Hell begins at the threshold, in the barzakh, and continues after the greater resurrection.

**Parallel Text**

> *"Then indeed they will have upon it a mixture of scalding water."* (Qur'an 37:67)

The consistency of *ḥamīm* as the drink of the damned across the Qur'an makes verse 93 a single note in a larger dirge. The reader has been taught this word; now it is applied to the singular dying denier.

**Welcome as Moral Mirror**

Every life prepares its own welcome. The foremost prepared nearness and receive *rawḥ* and *rayḥān*. The right prepared faith and receive *salām*. The denying astray prepared denial and receive *ḥamīm*. The welcomes are not arbitrary assignments; they are the first taste of what the life ordered from the kitchen of eternity.

**The First Taste of the Last Drink**

*Nuzul min ḥamīm* as the deathbed welcome means that the barzakh is not a neutral waiting room for the denying astray. The moral quality of the afterlife begins at once. This is why the tradition has emphasized the questioning of the grave, the pressure of the grave, and the lights or darkness of the grave. Verse 93 is one of the Qur'anic anchors of that teaching.

**Barzakh Welcome**

Boiling liquid as nuzul at the threshold means the intermediate realm is already morally qualified for the denier. Verse 93 anchors the tradition's teaching that the grave is not neutral for all. The first taste of the last drink begins when the soul is taken in denial.

**First Taste of the Last Drink**

Then a welcome of boiling liquid. Nuzul of ḥamīm at the threshold means the barzakh is already morally qualified for the denier. The grave is not neutral for all. Verse 93 anchors that teaching. The first taste of the last drink begins when the soul is taken in denial. Every life prepares its welcome; this is the welcome denial prepares.

**Fearing the First Taste**

Fear a welcome of ḥamīm enough to change. Verse 93's barzakh-welcome means the grave is not neutral for the denier. The tradition's teaching on the grave's pressure and darkness finds a Qur'anic anchor here. Use the fear as wing, not as paralysis. Flight toward salām and rawḥ is the proper use of fearing ḥamīm.

**Among the Deathbed and the Final Certification**

This verse belongs to the sūrah's fifth section — the soul at the throat, the watchers who cannot return it, God nearer than the watchers, the threefold welcome in the singular (rawḥ and rayḥān; salām; ḥamīm and jaḥīm), the certification as ḥaqq al-yaqīn, and the final command to glorify. Ibn ʿAjībah and others read the welcomes as including the barzakh; the mainstream holds both intermediate and final stages. Verse 93 is part of the sūrah's most intimate pedagogy. Death is undergone in the first person; the conditionals of 88–94 are mirrors; Qatādah's distinction on certainty (beneficial now or convicting later) governs the response. End in tasbīḥ. The Event is certain. Glorify.

## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:94

> **and burning in Hellfire.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Taṣliyat al-Jaḥīm**

*Wa-taṣliyatu jaḥīm* — "and burning in Hellfire." *Taṣliyah* means the act of being exposed to or roasted by fire. *Jaḥīm* is one of the Qur'an's names for Hell, suggesting the intensity of blazing. The welcome of boiling liquid is followed by the burning itself. Drink and fire — the two elemental punishments — complete the portrait of the third singular soul.

**Barzakh and the Greater Fire**

As with verses 88–91, some see these verses as referring to the intermediate realm, while others cite them as proof that disbelievers may taste the Fire from the moment of death (Ibn ʿAjībah). Both can be true in sequence: punishment in the barzakh after the lesser resurrection, and punishment in Hell after the greater. The Qur'an and Sunnah affirm both stages.

**The Closed Portrait**

Verse 94 completes the threefold deathbed map. The sūrah has now described the three groups at the scale of the Hereafter's gardens and fires (vv. 7–56) and at the scale of the individual death (vv. 88–94). What remains is to certify the entire discourse as certainty and to command glorification once more.

**The Fear That Saves**

Healthy fear of *taṣliyat jaḥīm* is not neurosis; it is one of the Qur'an's intended instruments of guidance. The Prophet ﷺ wept and prayed for protection from the Fire. The believer who never feels a tremor at verse 94 may need to ask whether he has domesticated the revelation into comfort alone. The sūrah that offers *rawḥ* and *rayḥān* also offers this — because mercy without warning is not the mercy of the Lord of the worlds.

**Jaḥīm Named Again**

The final word on the third singular soul is fire. The sūrah does not flinch. Having begun with the Event that abases and exalts, it ends the sorting with *taṣliyat jaḥīm* for those who denied and strayed. The severity is part of the truth of certainty that the next verse will announce. A revelation that only comforted would be less true to the moral structure of a world in which real wrong is done and real rejection of God is chosen.

**Fire as Final Word on the Third**

Taṣliyat jaḥīm closes the third singular portrait. The sūrah has now mapped the three groups at cosmic scale and at deathbed scale. Severity is part of ḥaqq al-yaqīn. A revelation that only comforted would under-describe a world in which real rejection of God is chosen and real wrong is done.

**Burning as Closure of the Third**

And burning in Hellfire. Taṣliyat jaḥīm closes the third singular portrait. The sūrah has mapped three groups at cosmic scale and at deathbed scale. Severity is part of the truth of certainty about to be announced. A revelation that only comforted would under-describe a world in which real rejection of God is chosen. Healthy fear of this verse is a wing of the heart, not a neurosis. The Prophet ﷺ sought protection from the Fire. So should we.

**Holding Severity as Truth**

Do not edit taṣliyat jaḥīm out of your religion. Verse 94 closes the third portrait with fire. Ḥaqq al-yaqīn includes this. Comfort alone would under-describe the moral world. Seek protection from the Fire as the Prophet did. Let healthy fear and healthy hope fly together. The sūrah that offered rayḥān also offers this — because it is true.

**Among the Deathbed and the Final Certification**

This verse belongs to the sūrah's fifth section — the soul at the throat, the watchers who cannot return it, God nearer than the watchers, the threefold welcome in the singular (rawḥ and rayḥān; salām; ḥamīm and jaḥīm), the certification as ḥaqq al-yaqīn, and the final command to glorify. Ibn ʿAjībah and others read the welcomes as including the barzakh; the mainstream holds both intermediate and final stages. Verse 94 is part of the sūrah's most intimate pedagogy. Death is undergone in the first person; the conditionals of 88–94 are mirrors; Qatādah's distinction on certainty (beneficial now or convicting later) governs the response. End in tasbīḥ. The Event is certain. Glorify.

## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:95

> **Verily, this is the truth of certainty;**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Ḥaqq al-Yaqīn: The Highest Certainty**

*Inna hādhā la-huwa ḥaqqu al-yaqīn* — "Verily, this is the truth of certainty." *Hādhā* ("this") is read as referring to what has been mentioned about the states of souls in the intermediate realm (al-Rāzī, al-Ṭabarī) or to all that has been mentioned in the sūrah (Ibn ʿAjībah, al-Qurṭubī, al-Shawkānī). *Ḥaqq al-yaqīn* is understood by most as "the truth of what is certain" — certainty at its most realized degree.

**Three Degrees of Certainty**

The tradition distinguishes three ascending levels of certainty: *ʿilm al-yaqīn* (the knowledge of certainty, 102:5), *ʿayn al-yaqīn* (the eye of certainty, 102:7), and *ḥaqq al-yaqīn* (the truth of certainty, 56:95). A classical analogy likens the knowledge of certainty to hearing of a fire, the eye of certainty to seeing the fire, and the truth of certainty to being consumed by the fire (cf. commentary on 27:7 and 102:5). Verse 95 places the content of al-Wāqiʿah at the third degree: not rumor, not distant vision, but the thing itself in its incontestable reality.

**Qatādah's Gloss**

Qatādah (d. 117/726), among the foremost of the third generation of commentators, said of this verse:

> *"God does not leave anyone until He has informed him of this Qur'an; as for the believer, he is made certain of it in this world, and that benefits him on the Day of Resurrection. As for the disbeliever, he is made certain of it on the Day of Resurrection when certainty of it will not benefit him."* (al-Qurṭubī, al-Ṭabarī)

Certainty is coming for everyone. The only question is whether it comes as saving light or as convicting fire.

**The Entire Qur'an as Ḥaqq al-Yaqīn**

Some take the verse as a reference to the entire Qur'an (al-Rāzī). Al-Wāqiʿah, on this reading, stands in for the whole Book: what has been recited from "When the Event befalls" to this point is not conjecture. It is the truth of certainty. The appropriate response is not further debate but the glorification of the next verse.

**Living with Ḥaqq al-Yaqīn**

To accept this verse is to reorganize life around what is certain rather than around what is merely urgent. The Event is certain. The three kinds are certain. The helplessness of the deathbed is certain. The welcomes are certain. Much of what occupies modern attention is not certain. Verse 95 is a call to invest in the certain — preparation for the Event, membership among the right or the foremost, a tongue trained to glorify rather than to scorn.

**Three Degrees, One Destination of Knowledge**

*ʿIlm al-yaqīn*, *ʿayn al-yaqīn*, *ḥaqq al-yaqīn* — hearing of the fire, seeing the fire, being in the fire. The tradition's analogy is severe because certainty's highest degree is participatory, not merely observational. Verse 95 places al-Wāqiʿah's content at that highest degree. This is not rumor about the Event. This is the Event's truth in verbal form, from the Lord of the Event.

**Certainty Before the Forced Certainty**

Qatādah's distinction remains decisive: the believer is made certain in this world, and it benefits him; the disbeliever is made certain on the Day, when it does not. Verse 95 is an offer of the beneficial certainty now. Take it, and the forced certainty later will be confirmation. Refuse it, and the forced certainty later will be prosecution.

**Certainty's Highest Degree**

Verily, this is the truth of certainty. Ḥaqq al-yaqīn above ʿilm al-yaqīn and ʿayn al-yaqīn — being in the fire, not only hearing of it or seeing it. Qatādah: the believer is made certain in this world to his benefit; the disbeliever on the Day to his loss. Verse 95 offers beneficial certainty now. Take it as confirmation that will be confirmed. Refuse it as prosecution that will be prosecuted. The entire sūrah — or the entire Qur'an — stands under this certification.

**Taking Beneficial Certainty Now**

Choose Qatādah's first option: certainty in this world that benefits on the Day. Verse 95 certifies the sūrah — and in one reading the whole Qur'an — as ḥaqq al-yaqīn. Reorganize life around what is certain: the Event, the three kinds, the deathbed's helplessness, the welcomes, the Magnificent Lord. Let uncertain urgencies yield to certain ones.

**Among the Deathbed and the Final Certification**

This verse belongs to the sūrah's fifth section — the soul at the throat, the watchers who cannot return it, God nearer than the watchers, the threefold welcome in the singular (rawḥ and rayḥān; salām; ḥamīm and jaḥīm), the certification as ḥaqq al-yaqīn, and the final command to glorify. Ibn ʿAjībah and others read the welcomes as including the barzakh; the mainstream holds both intermediate and final stages. Verse 95 is part of the sūrah's most intimate pedagogy. Death is undergone in the first person; the conditionals of 88–94 are mirrors; Qatādah's distinction on certainty (beneficial now or convicting later) governs the response. End in tasbīḥ. The Event is certain. Glorify.

---
## Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah 56:96

> **so glorify the Name of thy Lord, the Magnificent!**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Final Command**

*Fa-sabbiḥ bi-smi rabbika al-ʿaẓīm* — "so glorify the Name of thy Lord, the Magnificent!" The sūrah ends as its argumentative middle ended (v. 74): with *tasbīḥ*. The *fa-* can indicate sequence of actions or that the truth this sūrah contains requires the affirmation of God's greatness and transcendence (Ibn ʿAjībah). Because this is the truth of certainty, glorify. Certainty's proper fruit is praise.

**Inclusio: The Sūrah's Ring Structure**

The repetition of the identical command at verses 74 and 96 creates an inclusio — a ring that holds the intervening material. Between the two commands to glorify fall the oath by the stars, the nobility of the Qur'an, the rebuke of scorn, and the deathbed sorting. All of that content is framed by *tasbīḥ*. The reader who enters the frame and exits the frame is a reader who has been told twice: the right response to what you have learned is to glorify the Magnificent Lord.

**The Magnificent (*Al-ʿAẓīm*)**

*Al-ʿAẓīm* is the Name of vastness, magnitude, and absolute greatness. After a sūrah that has shaken the earth, pulverized the mountains, sorted all humanity, argued from seed and fire, sworn by the stars, and stood at the throat of the dying, the Name *al-ʿAẓīm* is the only Name that fits. Everything described is great; the One who authors it is the Magnificent.

**Embodied in the Prayer**

As noted at verse 74, the revelation of this command is linked in the Sunnah to the *tasbīḥ* of rukūʿ: *subḥāna rabbiya al-ʿaẓīm*. Every bowing in every prayer reenacts the ending of al-Wāqiʿah. The Muslim who has never consciously connected the two may begin to do so: in every rukūʿ, the Event, the three kinds, the deathbed, and the truth of certainty are present in the Name that the back bows under.

**The Sūrah of Sufficiency, Completed**

The ḥadīth promised that whoever recites al-Wāqiʿah every night will never be afflicted by need. Ibn ʿAjībah's explanation now has its full context: whoever understands what this sūrah discloses about the Hereafter will prepare for it, and the one who prepares for it is freed from the tyranny of worldly need. Verse 96 is the practical summary of that freedom. The person who glorifies the Magnificent Lord has found the provision that denial can never be (v. 82). His *rizq* is *tasbīḥ*; his welcome will not be *ḥamīm*; his company will be the right or the foremost; and the Event, when it befalls, will not find him among those who deny its befalling.

**A Closing Word to the Reader**

The expanded commentary of this sūrah has walked from the Event to the glorification, verse by verse, without compression. The reader who has stayed the course has been shown the cosmos undone, the three human destinies, the proofs of seed and harvest and water and fire, the nobility of the concealed Book, and the throat at which every claim of independence fails. What remains is not another paragraph of explanation. What remains is the act the verse commands: *fa-sabbiḥ bi-smi rabbika al-ʿaẓīm*. Glorify the Name of thy Lord, the Magnificent. The Event is coming. The glorification can begin now.

**The Sūrah's Last Word Is a Command**

Al-Wāqiʿah does not end with a description or a threat. It ends with a command to glorify. The reader who has been shown everything — Event, sorting, proofs, Book, deathbed — is told what to do: *sabbiḥ*. Knowledge becomes worship or it fails its purpose. Verse 96 is the sūrah's exit door, and the door is shaped like *tasbīḥ*.

**Nightly Recitation and Freedom from Need**

The ḥadīth of reciting al-Wāqiʿah every night and being protected from need finds its full rationale here. The one who internalizes this sūrah's map of the Hereafter is reorganized around a different provision. Worldly need loses its tyranny because the Event has become real, the three kinds have become personal, and the Magnificent Lord has become the tongue's familiar. Verse 96 is the practice that seals that reorganization: glorify, again and again, until glorification is the soul's default.

**Completion**

From "When the Event befalls" to "glorify the Name of thy Lord, the Magnificent," the sūrah has completed its arc. The expanded commentary has followed that arc verse by verse, without compression, as the method required. What remains for the reader is not more commentary but the act the final verse commands. The Event is ahead. The glorification is available now. *Fa-sabbiḥ bi-smi rabbika al-ʿaẓīm.*

**Final Exit: Glorify**

So glorify the Name of thy Lord, the Magnificent. The sūrah's last word is a command, twin to verse 74, framing the intervening oath, Book, scorn, and deathbed in an inclusio of tasbīḥ. Certainty's proper fruit is praise. Al-ʿAẓīm fits everything the sūrah has shown. Every rukūʿ reenacts this ending. Nightly recitation of al-Wāqiʿah, promised as protection from need, finds its rationale: the one who internalizes this map is reorganized around a different provision. The Event is ahead. The glorification is available now. Fa-sabbiḥ bi-smi rabbika al-ʿaẓīm.

**Beginning the Glorification Now**

Fa-sabbiḥ bi-smi rabbika al-ʿaẓīm. The commentary ends; the command remains. Glorify in rukūʿ with conscious connection to this sūrah. Recite al-Wāqiʿah at night and let its map free you from the tyranny of need. The Event is ahead. The three kinds are being joined now. The throat is coming. The truth is certain. Glorify the Name of thy Lord, the Magnificent.

---
**[End of the commentary on Sūrah al-Wāqiʿah]**
